I AM · J.N.R. Quitt
A NOVEL

I AM

J.N.R. Quitt

A medical mystery about memory, consciousness, mortality, and what it means to truly know another person without surrendering who you are.

Part One — I Am
Chapter Working draft

Chapter One

(WORKING DRAFT — recalibrated per Handoff 004 / Creator Voice Compass. Not final prose.)
01_mia_opening.md

The Okafors had been holding hands since Mina walked in, and they were still holding hands twenty minutes later, which told her — before she'd finished the second sentence of the results — that this one was going to be easy. Not easy easy. Nothing in this room was ever easy. But there was a specific stillness some couples had, the stillness of two people who'd already had the hard conversation between themselves — in the car, the night before, years ago when they decided to try for a kid in a family with a history like this one — and the Okafors had it.

"So," Grace said. "Give us the number."

Mina had learned, twenty years in, that people wanted the number before anything else, and that giving it to them straight was a kindness, not a shortcut. Softening it just made the softening the thing they remembered. "Emeka doesn't carry the variant," she said. "He can't pass it on. For the two of you, the risk to any pregnancy is the general population risk. Not zero. Never zero. But not what we were screening for."

She watched it land. Grace made a sound that wasn't quite a laugh, both hands flying up over her mouth; Emeka — who'd sat very still the whole appointment, the specific stillness of a man who'd decided in advance this was a room he wasn't allowed to fall apart in — put an arm around his wife, exhaled like he'd been holding it since March, and for one unguarded second looked ten years younger than the man who'd walked in wearing his church clothes to a Tuesday appointment, because some part of him had wanted this to count as a kind of prayer.

Mina let them have the second. Letting people have the second was, honestly, most of the job. Anyone could learn to read a karyotype in a weekend with the right cheat sheet. Learning when to shut your mouth and let two people hold each other under bad fluorescent lighting — that was the part nobody taught you in the program, and the part that actually mattered.

"Thank you," Emeka said, when he could talk again. "For — just telling us. Not the whole—" He waved a hand at an invisible cloud of hedging, disclaimers, gentle apparatus.

"That's the job," Mina said, which was true, and also not the whole truth, because she had opinions about that apparatus, formed at a kitchen table that had nothing to do with the Okafors and everything to do with the day, five years back, some other doctor had handed her mother the words early-onset like they weighed less than they did.

She kept that part to herself. She printed the summary, walked them out to the elevator, and went back to her office to eat a granola bar standing up over the trash can — her actual lunch, most Tuesdays — and to finally open, because she hadn't managed to leave it alone all morning, the flagged consult request that had landed at 6:40 a.m.

Locke, Warren. 52. Trauma admission overnight, single-vehicle MVC. Reason for consult: unable to reconcile laboratory findings with clinical presentation; imaging finding of clinical significance; patient declines to elaborate on medical history.

She read it twice. The second time, she caught herself sitting bolt upright, granola bar forgotten halfway to her mouth, in the exact posture she associated — had associated since she was old enough to notice her own body doing things without asking her — with a very specific and extremely unscientific feeling: a word showing up before she'd earned the right to know it.

She'd stopped hunting for a name for that feeling around age eleven, the year she decided it was either a personality quirk or a mildly defective wire somewhere behind her eyes, and that a person who wanted to be taken seriously filed it under noise. It was, statistically, noise. She was, statistically, a woman with a family history of dementia primed to see significance in everything, because significance was the one thing she was most afraid of missing.

She closed the file. Finished the bar. Did not go down to the fourth floor, because it wasn't her turn, because there were four more appointments between her and that door, because — she told herself, refiling the flicker under noise for the thousandth time — it was one weird panel on one uncooperative patient, and she'd watched this exact shape of nothing turn out to be nothing more times than she'd bothered counting.

She was wrong about that. She'd think about this particular Tuesday afternoon for the rest of her life as the last one that belonged entirely to her old life.

*

Perla's house sat nine minutes from the hospital, small and blue, the kind of place real estate listings called charming and everyone else called small, and Mina let herself in most evenings without knocking, because knocking made her mother anxious in a very specific way — like a knock was a pop quiz she suddenly had to pass, remembering how many steps it took from the couch to the door, remembering whose knock a knock like that usually was.

The smell hit her before the door had even finished swinging shut: garlic, vinegar, bay leaf, something dark and patient that had been on the stove for hours — and for one full second, no thought attached to it at all, Mina was eleven years old, home sick from school, half-asleep on the couch under a blanket that smelled like this exact thing, entirely, unreasonably safe. It was gone as fast as it came. It always was. Smell did that to her — got past every door she'd built, said its whole piece, and left before she could argue with it.

Perla was at the kitchen table with the newspaper, not reading it so much as folding it, smaller and smaller rectangles, the old habit from grading papers she wanted her hands busy while her brain worked something out.

"You're doing the thing," Mina said, dropping her bag.

"I'm reading."

"You're folding."

"I am reading while I fold. Women have done two things at once since the invention of women." Perla didn't look up. "There's adobo. I made too much, because I forgot for one whole minute that I'm not cooking for your father anymore, which" — she flicked her hand, a small sharp gesture that meant don't, not tonight — "is a separate humiliation I am not currently equipped to discuss."

Mina got two plates down instead of answering that, which was its own kind of fluency, five years in: the things you didn't say out loud, you plated instead, set down at the right moment, still warm.

"How was work," Perla said, the way you ask a question whose answer you've already braced for.

"Somebody got good news for once."

"Only once? In a whole day?"

"It's a specific kind of clinic, Ma."

"I know what kind of clinic." Perla put the fork down. For a second her face did the thing — reached for a word, visibly reaching, the stall that from the outside looked almost like patience — then landed somewhere close enough. "The kind where people find out if the — the thing runs downhill."

"Whether it's heritable," Mina said gently, sliding the word across like the exact tool her mother had been patting the table for.

"That's it." Perla picked the fork back up, said it lightly, the way she said everything now that cost her something — which Mina had learned, the hard way, over five years, was not remotely the same as it costing her nothing. "Funny word to lose, for a woman in my particular line of work. Heritable."

"You're doing the joke on purpose now."

"I get to. My chicken, my house, my disease. I decide what's funny about it." She reached over and put her hand flat on top of Mina's, on the table — an old habit, older than any diagnosis, from back when Mina was small and had said something that scared them both — held it there a second, then took it back and asked, in a completely different voice, whether Mina thought they should get a dog.

They did not get a dog. They talked about it for forty minutes anyway, badly, happily, Perla insisting on a breed that would outlive them both out of spite, and it was, Mina thought later, driving home in the dark past a road she had no reason yet to remember the name of, a good night. She kept a count now. Hadn't always. You start counting the second you understand there's a number.

Chapter Two

(WORKING DRAFT — recalibrated per Handoff 004 / Creator Voice Compass. Not final prose.)
02_warren_opening.md

Monday morning.

Warren had been sorting Dot Reyes's pills into the little plastic days-of-the-week box for going on two years now, and in two years she had never once thanked him for it and never once let him leave without an opinion about something.

"You're limping," she said, the morning of the accident, watching him cross her kitchen with the box in one hand and her coffee in the other, black, too hot, exactly how she liked it and would deny to her grave that she liked it.

"I'm not limping."

"You're limping like a man about to tell somebody he isn't limping."

He set Thursday down next to her cup. Full stop, she'd told him, week one, I can get a coffee into my own mouth without a man supervising me, thank you — and somehow every morning since, he'd ended up standing close enough to catch the cup anyway, in case her hand did the thing it sometimes did now, first thing, before the tremor settled down for the day.

"Ankle. Ladder. It's nothing."

"It's always nothing with you." Dot took a sip and watched him over the rim with the specific unblinking attention of a woman who'd spent forty years grading eighth-graders' excuses for a living, back when she'd been the kind of teacher children were more scared of than they ever let on to their parents. "You know, in two years, I don't believe you've told me one true thing about yourself that wasn't wearing a joke as a hat."

"I told you I hate the color yellow. No joke attached."

"That's not a fact about you. That's a grudge against a color."

"It's very much a fact about me. I carry a lot of grudges nobody asked me to explain." He crouched by the handrail he'd bolted to her back steps last fall, testing it out of habit rather than need — he'd put it in himself, knew exactly how solid it was — but crouching gave him somewhere to put his face for a second, which was occasionally the whole point, with Dot. "Hip today?"

"Don't change the subject."

"I'm not changing the subject. The subject is your hip. It's been the subject since 2019, same as that ladder's been a bad idea since roughly the Carter administration, and you're going to climb it the second I leave regardless, so let's not pretend either of us is winning an argument here."

That got a real laugh out of her, short and surprised, and it did something to his chest every single time it worked, two years running, that he'd never bothered examining too closely.

"You'll come Thursday," she said at the door. Not really a question.

"I always come Thursday."

"Say it like it's a law of physics."

"For you it basically is." She swatted him with the folded newspaper — a small, dry violence that was, between the two of them, after two years, about as close as either of them would ever get to saying something neither had words for — and he was still grinning about it twenty minutes later, driving the two-lane road home with the rain coming down proper now, wipers on the second setting, radio off.

He kept the radio off most days. Not for any reason he could have explained to Dot, or particularly wanted to. Songs had a way of getting in underneath, past whatever door a person thought they'd shut, and dragging something up by the collar before he'd had any say in the matter — a smell, a room, somebody's hands, gone again before he could look straight at it. Today, ankle aching, rain coming in sideways, he didn't especially feel like being dragged anywhere. He'd rather sit with the rain. The rain never asked him anything.

He didn't see the deer so much as become aware of it, the way you become aware of a breath you've been holding the second before you finally let it go — a shape at the treeline resolving, a half-beat late, into four legs and one wet black eye catching his headlights, standing exactly in his lane, exactly the way deer did, like the specific geometry of not moving were an argument it was making on principle.

Left shoulder was a ditch. Right shoulder was a tree. There was no good version of this, and he had just enough time to know it clearly, the way a man knows weather coming: this is going to hurt.

He didn't have time for anything past that, which was, later, one of the details he kept turning over — not the impact, not the specific unbearable geometry of a car folding somewhere a car was never built to fold, but the plain fact that in the one clear second before it happened, underneath the pain he could already feel arriving, what he actually felt wasn't fear.

It was closer to relief.

He wouldn't look straight at the relief, either. Not for a long time.

One month later
Interlude Flash-forwardWorking draft

Memory-Story: I Am (Warren, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — finished conversion per Handoff 008. Replaces the prior third-person interlude. Not final prose.)
interlude_i_am.md

A month after the crash.

The first time he told her anything true, it wasn't planned — she'd asked him, sitting on his floor because his room had exactly one chair and she'd lost the argument about who got it, what he actually was, the same question she'd asked a dozen times in a dozen shapes, and instead of the usual deflection he heard himself say, "You want the real answer or the version that doesn't sound insane?"

"I've had four hours of you not sounding insane. I can handle a step down."

"Fine." He sat on the floor across from her, back against the bed frame, and found, to his own surprise, that he wasn't stalling for an exit this time. "Then I need you to actually try to picture something, and I need you to not flinch away from how strange it's going to sound, because I've had a very long time to get used to it and you're getting about ninety seconds."

"Okay."

He took a breath.

"One rule first."

"There's a rule?"

"There's always a rule when somebody says there isn't." He tipped the bottle toward her. "You're going to hear me tell things I couldn't possibly know. When that happens, ask me how I know them."

Mina frowned. "You mean whether you were there."

"Sometimes. That's one answer." He counted the others against his fingers. "Sometimes I was there. Sometimes I remember something another Eternal gave me through Harmony, which can feel uncomfortably close to having lived it yourself, but isn't the same thing. Sometimes I only felt the edge of something while it was happening. Sometimes I learned it later from people who were there. And sometimes I'm reconstructing from pieces and I may be wrong."

"That's a fairly important disclaimer."

"It's why I'm giving it to you before the impossible part."

"And you're going to tell me which is which?"

"If I know. If I forget to say, ask."

She studied him. "Why does that sound like you've had this argument before?"

"Because people are very fond of turning stories into testimony and testimony into certainty. I'd rather not help."

That landed differently than the rest. Mina set her beer down.

"How many times have you had to watch that happen?"

He hesitated. Not because he didn't know the answer. Because until that moment he'd been speaking as though the length of his life were background information instead of another impossible thing she had never agreed to believe.

"More than two thousand years' worth."

Mina stared at him.

"No."

"That's a reasonable response."

"No, Warren. You don't get to put that in the middle of a sentence and keep going. More than two thousand years?"

"Yes."

"Your chart says fifty-two."

"I know what my chart says."

"Your bones said fifty-two. Your blood said—" She stopped. His blood had said several things she still couldn't reconcile with one another. "How old are you?"

"I don't know."

"You don't know."

"Not in the way you're asking. Warren Locke is fifty-two on paper because fifty-two was useful. This body has been alive continuously for more than two thousand years. The part of me I'm trying to explain to you tonight is older than bodies, so if you want one number that makes all of that behave, I can't give you one."

She thought about his chart. Bone age fifty-two — not approximately, not within some margin that could hide two millennia, but fifty-two the way a textbook said fifty-two. Blood that had flagged every anomaly detector her lab owned. A femur that had knit in seventeen hours. She had spent twenty years learning the rules that bodies followed, and she was sitting across from a body that had been breaking the most basic one — that living things wear out — for longer than her entire field had existed. Longer than the language she was thinking in had existed.

Mina got to her feet.

The apartment was too small for pacing, but she managed three steps toward the window and three back.

"More than two thousand."

"Still yes."

"Rome."

"Yes."

"Ancient Rome."

"Among other places."

She looked at him with an expression he couldn't read at all. "Are you a vampire?"

He blinked.

Then, despite every reason not to, he laughed.

Mina pointed at him. "That is not an answer."

"No. Sorry. No. I am not a vampire."

"Do vampires exist?"

"Not that I've ever encountered."

"That's somehow worse."

"I can see how it might be."

She sat again, but not where she'd been. A little farther away now, back against the wall, studying him as though proximity itself had become a variable she needed to reconsider.

"Can you die?"

Warren's amusement disappeared.

"I don't know."

That answer frightened her more than the age had. He could see it happen.

And that was the worst part, she realized, sitting against his wall with her beer going warm in her hand. The number didn't solve anything. Two thousand years didn't tell her what he was — it only told her that whatever he was, it had been happening for longer than anyone had ever suspected, right out in the open, while the rest of the species built civilizations on top of not noticing. The mystery hadn't gotten smaller. It had gotten older.

"Have you ever—"

"That's a different Telling."

She caught the capital letter in his voice even if there wasn't really one there. "Convenient."

"Extremely."

Mina was quiet for several seconds. Then she picked up her beer again.

"Fine. But we're coming back to the two-thousand-year-old not-a-vampire situation."

"Over two thousand."

She gave him a look.

"Sorry."

"No, you aren't."

"No."

Somehow that helped. Not much, but enough that she stayed.

"So when you tell me one of these—what do I call them? Memories?"

"Some are. Not all." He considered it. "Tellings, maybe. That's what they are by the time they reach you. Me telling you what I think happened, and telling you how close I was to it."

"Tellings."

"If you hate it, we can workshop the terminology after the creation of the universe."

She smiled despite herself. "Fine. Tell me."

He looked at her for another second, then began.

"There was no before."

She waited for the rest of it. He let the silence sit there a second.

"Before needs an after standing next to it to mean anything," he said. "And for a long time — I'm going to keep using words like long and before, because I don't have better ones, but you have to understand every single one of them is already a translation, there wasn't language yet, there wasn't yet — there was just the one fact. Meaning everything it would ever mean. All at once. Nothing to compare it to."

"What fact?"

"I am."

Mina didn't say anything. He'd learned, somewhere along the way, that this was her actual listening face — most people's faces did something when they were paying real attention, and hers went very still, the same stillness he imagined she wore reading a chart that didn't make sense.

"It didn't arrive like light," he said. "There wasn't light yet to arrive like. Closest I've got is — you know the half-second before you remember your own name, right when you're waking up? You're definitely someone. Urgently someone. You just haven't caught up to who yet." He looked at his own hands, which was a habit he'd picked up somewhere in the last few centuries and never quite lost, evidence he liked having in a conversation like this one. "It was that. Except there was no falling asleep first. No name to catch up to, either, not for a long time. Just the arriving part. Stretched out beyond anything your clocks would know what to do with."

"That sounds terrifying."

"That's what everybody assumes. It wasn't, though — that's the part people get wrong. Being alone is only frightening once you know alone is a thing you can be. I didn't have a second data point yet. I just had the one fact, repeating, because repeating was the only motion I had. I am, I am, I am. Not a chant. Nothing that organized. Closer to the sound something makes purely because it just found out it's capable of making a sound at all."

"Did it hurt?"

"No. And I need you to actually believe that, because I think you're bracing for the sad version of this story, and it's not there yet. It gets sad. I promise you it gets sad. This part was just—" He searched for the word. Lately, he had started noticing that Mina would wait for him to find one instead of supplying it herself. "Astonished. I was astonished. For longer than any star's been burning. Nobody to tell. Nothing to compare it to. Just astonished, all the way down, at the plain fact of being here to be astonished at all."

"That does sound lonely. Eventually, if nothing else."

"It was. Once I had something to measure it against." He almost said the next part. Felt it come all the way up behind his teeth — a second fact, a second someone, the actual shape of what came after the astonishment — and stopped it there, deliberately, the way he'd stopped it every time this question had come close to this exact edge for over two thousand years. She wasn't ready for that part. He wasn't sure he was either, not with her specifically, not yet. "But that's a different story."

"I'm going to hold you to that."

"I know," he said, and was surprised to find he meant it as a promise and not a deflection. He'd stopped making promises a long time ago, mostly because he'd gotten too good at keeping the ones that cost him and breaking the ones that cost other people. This one felt like neither. It felt like the first honest thing he'd said in weeks that he hadn't immediately wanted to take back.

One month earlier
Chapter Working draft

Chapter Three

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 005 / Assignment 005. Not final prose.)
04_er_bridge.md

A month earlier.

Renata Abara had been an ER attending for eleven years, which was long enough to develop a genuinely reliable internal alarm for the difference between a patient who was in trouble and a chart that was in trouble, and at 2:40 that morning, bent over bed 6's imaging on a monitor that badly needed its brightness recalibrated, she had the uncomfortable, specific sensation of both alarms going off at once, disagreeing with each other.

The patient — Locke, Warren, 52, single-vehicle MVC — was, by every vital sign on the monitor, doing fine. Better than fine. His pressure had actually come up since intake, which patients' pressure did not typically do seventeen hours after a right femur fracture and a lacerated spleen, not unless somebody had given him something a great deal stronger than the modest dose of fentanyl actually charted. He was talking. Making, God help her, small talk, with Rosa at the desk, something about the Sounders game, in the specific relaxed cadence of a man who had not fully clocked yet how bad his own numbers should have looked.

"You're sure this is his first films," she said, to Marcus, the tech, without looking up.

"First and only, doc. Why?"

She turned the monitor toward him and watched his face do the thing hers had just done. The femur — the one that was, per the intake note, currently and freshly broken — showed a second injury on the same bone, higher up, fully remodeled, years old at minimum, with the specific smooth continuity of bone that had healed clean, on its own, with no plate, no screw, no scar of hardware anywhere in the marrow to explain it.

"He didn't mention a prior fracture," Marcus said.

"He didn't mention a prior fracture because I asked him twice and he said no both times." Renata straightened up, rubbing the specific spot behind her left eye that had started aching around hour nine of a twelve-hour shift that had, up to this point, been an entirely ordinary bad night — a stroke alert, two overdoses, a kid with a fishhook somewhere genuinely unfortunate — and was apparently saving its actual weirdness for the back half. "Get me his splenic recheck the second it's back. And page Genetics."

"For a broken leg?"

"For a broken leg that's already told me a story that isn't true," Renata said, "and I would very much like to know why, before this man convinces someone with less time invested than me to let him go home."

*

She went in to see him herself, because eleven years had also taught her that some things a chart couldn't tell you and a five-minute conversation could.

He looked better than a man with his imaging had any business looking — pale under the fluorescents, sure, sweating a little around the eyes from the fentanyl wearing thin, but alert, oriented, tracking her without effort, already halfway through sizing her up the way some patients did, the ones who'd spent a lot of time in rooms like this one deciding how much of themselves to hand over to the person deciding their care.

"Mr. Locke. I'm Dr. Abara. How's the pain, one to ten?"

"Generous four. You've got good drugs."

"We do. I want to ask you something, and I'd like the real answer, not the one you already gave the tech." She held up the tablet, turned it toward him, watched his eyes go to the old, healed fracture on the screen and stay there half a second too long. "Has this leg been broken before?"

"Not that I remember."

"That's an interesting way to answer that question."

"It's the honest way." He said it lightly, the specific lightness of a man laying a very small, very deliberate weight down on top of something much heavier, and something about the precision of it — not evasive, exactly, more like a door being closed with real care so it wouldn't slam — made the back of her neck do a thing it hadn't done since residency. "Look, Doc. I appreciate you're thorough. I really do. Whatever's going on with the leg, I'm sure there's a boring explanation, and I promise I'm not hiding a dramatic one just to be difficult."

"I didn't say dramatic."

"You didn't have to. It's in the face you're making."

She left it there, because pushing a stable, oriented, competent adult past the point of a polite no wasn't her job, whatever her neck thought about it, and went back to the desk, and paged Genetics, and got herself the worst cup of coffee available anywhere in the building, and did not think about Warren Locke again for almost forty minutes, until Nadia found her to say the splenic recheck was back and it was, in Nadia's exact words, "extremely not what we expected," and Renata thought: of course it is, and went to go look.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Four

(WORKING DRAFT — recalibrated per Handoff 004 / Creator Voice Compass. Not final prose.)
05_first_encounter.md

The chart made no sense, which usually meant one of three things: lab error, mislabeled sample, or a patient who'd conveniently forgotten to mention an oncology history that explained everything. Mina stood outside room 412 a second longer than strictly necessary, scrolling through it a second time, laying odds on which of the three this was going to turn out to be.

Locke, Warren. 52. Single-vehicle MVC, right femur fracture, splenic laceration managed non-operatively. Unremarkable, except the labs read like they belonged to somebody thirty years younger recovering from something a lot smaller, and the CT showed — incidental finding of uncertain significance, the radiologist had written, with the specific caution of a man covering his own ass — a fully remodeled fracture of that exact femur. Old. Completely healed. No hardware. No record of it anywhere in his chart, or, when she'd checked twice out of pure stubbornness, in any chart at all.

Nobody's femur healed like that without a plate holding it together. Nobody's.

She knocked once and went in. The room smelled like every hospital room smelled — bleach doing its best over something older and less fixable underneath — and something about the specific layer under the bleach made her stomach drop half an inch before her brain caught up and told her why: it was the smell from the ICU hallway, four years back, the week they'd finally gotten Perla's diagnosis, back when Mina still thought a diagnosis was a kind of ending instead of a kind of beginning. She put it away. She was good at putting things away. It was possibly her single most useful skill, and also, she suspected, the thing that was going to kill her eventually.

He was sitting up in a hospital gown that had clearly lost an argument with him about dignity, doing the crossword with the hunched concentration of a man trying hard to look like someone with nothing else going on. Fifty-two looked plausible. Dark hair, almost black and not a gray strand she could see, cut short without much interest in what the cut was called. Dark eyes. Warm olive skin gone a little pale under the hospital lights. His face was difficult to place in the way a lot of faces were in the Northwest — Hispanic, maybe, or Mediterranean, possibly Middle Eastern. Five-eight or so, she guessed, lean rather than thin, with the forearms and hands of somebody who actually used them for work. Nothing about him looked remarkable enough to explain the chart.

He glanced up at her, and the ordinary first impression broke in one small place. She would spend an embarrassing amount of time later trying to name his expression and eventually, unsatisfyingly, settle on: recognized. Not that he recognized her. More like weather he'd been watching build on the horizon had finally arrived.

"You're not my ortho follow-up," he said.

"No."

"You're not going to tell me what you are, though. Gonna make me guess."

"Mina Mendez. Genetics." She held up the badge, which felt, absurdly, like producing evidence in a trial nobody had agreed to hold. "I got a consult."

Something crossed his face, there and gone fast enough that if she hadn't spent twenty years reading faces professionally, for money, she'd have missed it clean. "For a broken leg."

"For a broken leg that's been broken before and isn't supposed to be able to bring that up."

He set the crossword down on the blanket. "Don't know anything about that."

"You don't know anything about your own leg."

"I know plenty about the leg. Considerably less about what it got up to before I started renting it." Light, easy, the way Perla said heritable — and Mina went very still, very interested, with the specific stillness she got right before a result was about to matter. "Look, Doc. I appreciate the house call. Genuinely. But I'm going home inside the hour whether the leg's finished cooperating or not, and I don't think there's a form for whatever this is."

"There's always a form."

"There might not be, for this particular one."

She pulled the chair over — he watched her do it with an expression working hard at patient and mostly landing there — and sat close enough that this stopped being a doorway conversation and became the other kind. "Mr. Locke. I'm not here to make your discharge harder. I'm here because your bloodwork is doing something I've only ever read about in textbook edge cases, and bodies that heal the way yours apparently likes to don't usually get to just walk out. Somebody, eventually, wants to know why. I'd rather it be somebody actually trying to help you than whoever pulls this chart next."

He went quiet. Out in the hall a cart squeaked past, and neither of them looked at it.

"That's a hell of a speech," he said finally. "You give it a lot?"

"First time, actually."

"Well. It worked, a little. I'm almost telling you things." He looked down at the crossword, thumb resting against the edge of the paper. For one full second — a real second, not the earlier flicker — the easy deflection left his face. He looked tired. Not hospital tired. Older than that. "What I can tell you is I heal fast. Always have. Runs in the family, or it did, or — it's complicated, and it's not actually a secret so much as it's a story that doesn't fit inside a consult form, and I'm asking you as a favor, not as a patient invoking some right I probably don't have: let this one go."

It should have ended the conversation. She'd had this exact conversation, structurally, a hundred times before — the patient who didn't want to know, the family that didn't want the test, the entirely reasonable human wish to leave one particular stone unturned — and she was good, actually good, at knowing when to respect that and walk away.

She didn't walk away. Something old and unscientific, the same thing that had come to attention over a consult request at 6:40 that morning, was standing up very straight inside her now, and it was not, this time, willing to be filed under noise.

"Okay," she said, surprising herself as much as him. "Officially, I'll let it go. Chart note, no further workup, your call, your right." She stood, gathered her tablet, and then, at the door, without quite deciding to, turned back. "Unofficially — whatever's complicated, Mr. Locke — I hope somebody besides a form gets to know about it eventually."

He looked at her a long moment. He was tired — tired in a way that had nothing to do with fifty-two — and underneath the tiredness, unmistakably and only for a second, lonely. It made her want, absurdly, to sit back down and stay a while.

"Yeah," he said quietly, mostly to himself. "So do I."

She thought about that sentence the whole drive home. She was still thinking about it three days later, when the follow-up address he'd given her turned out to be a laundromat in Tacoma that had closed in 2019.

Chapter Five

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 005 / Assignment 005. Not final prose.)
06_warren_leaves.md

Wednesday, 5 a.m.

Getting out of a modern American hospital without officially leaving, Warren had discovered over the last several decades, was a genuinely different problem than it used to be, and he'd never quite forgiven the twentieth century for that.

A hundred years ago you waited until the right nurse was on a smoke break, got yourself vertical, and walked. Fifty years ago you signed one form nobody read closely and were gone by lunch. Now there was a wristband with a barcode on it, an insurance file that wanted verifying, a social worker named Elaine who had, in the four minutes since introducing herself, established with terrifying efficiency that his listed emergency contact number rang a landline that had been disconnected since 2019, that his address matched no property tax record in the county, and that she was, quote, "not trying to make this weird, Mr. Locke, I just need somewhere real to write down before I can sign off on you leaving against medical advice."

"It's a rental," he said. "I move around a lot for work."

"Sure." Denise did not look up from her clipboard, which he was beginning to suspect was less a clipboard and more a small, extremely competent weapon. "What kind of work has you moving around so much you don't show up on a lease anywhere?"

"Home health. Mostly elderly clients. It's a small-town kind of job — a lot of it's informal." All entirely true, which was, he'd learned centuries ago, generally the fastest way through a conversation like this one: never lie about the parts that were easy to check.

She finally looked up. "You've got a fractured femur, Mr. Locke, and your last set of vitals had a doctor paging Genetics at three in the morning. I'm not trying to keep you here. I'm trying to make sure somebody besides a hospital bracelet knows where you are for the next seventy-two hours, in case that femur has opinions about being walked on."

He was, underneath the specific and increasingly urgent desire to be anywhere else, a little impressed with her. It happened more than he let on — some ordinary person doing an unglamorous, thankless job with more integrity than the job strictly required, and some old reflex from a very long time ago still wanted to stop and actually see them for it. He didn't have time for that today. His leg, which had already knit considerably further than it should have in two days and was going to need him to be somewhere private and horizontal before a nurse noticed that on a follow-up film, was making its own opinions known, loudly, every time he shifted his weight on the crutches.

"I've got a neighbor," he said. "Runs the hardware store I live over. I'll give you his number, and he'll actually pick up, which is more than I can say for whatever's on file for me at this point — my old emergency contact's out of date, obviously, and that's on me, I should've fixed it months ago."

This, too, was true, which was the trick of it. Denny would absolutely pick up. Denny would also, if this Elaine woman actually called him, say something like "yeah, Warren's fine, he's just an idiot about doctors," and hang up, and never once think to ask why a hospital was calling about a man he'd been buying beers for the better part of twenty-five years, because that was the whole design of a life built this carefully — not lies, exactly, just very precisely arranged true things, none of which, examined alone, meant anything at all. A quarter century of genuine trust, doing the quiet work of camouflage.

She wrote the number down. She made him repeat it. She made him sign four separate things, one of which he was fairly sure just said, in smaller print, that the hospital was not responsible if his leg fell off in a Motel 6 somewhere, and by the time she finally, genuinely let him go, it was almost five in the morning and he had used up nearly every trick he still remembered for making a modern institution believe he was less complicated than he was.

He called a cab, because his own phone had not survived the accident any better than the car had, and paid cash, because a card left a trail he'd rather not leave this particular week, and sat in the back seat with his leg propped at an angle that was doing significantly better than the forty-four hours since impact should have allowed for, watching Seattle go gray and wet outside the window, and felt the familiar, low-grade dread settle in. He knew this part. A loose end became a name; a name became a question; a question became somebody persistent enough to keep asking it.

This one was going to take some cleaning up.

Chapter Six

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 005 / Assignment 005. Not final prose.)
08_the_drive.md

The drive to the coast took four hours the way Google said it would and closer to five and a half the way it actually went, because Mina had made the specific mistake of trusting a blue dot on a phone screen over a paper map she didn't own, and forty minutes past Aberdeen the blue dot's confidence and the actual road's cooperation with reality parted ways completely.

The turnoff GPS wanted her to take was gated, chained, and posted, in the specific unhurried handwriting of somebody who had made this exact sign more than once, with the words PRIVATE LOGGING RD — NOT A THRU-WAY — TOW AT OWNER EXPENSE, which was either an out-of-date map error or evidence that whoever built the app had never once actually driven Highway 105 west of Raymond, and Mina sat there at the gate for a full minute, wipers on intermittent against a rain that had gone from decorative to genuinely committed somewhere around Montesano.

The closed laundromat had been the lie. The updated emergency contact in the discharge record had been the mistake: one phone number, publicly listed to D. Vasko at Vasko Hardware in Corbin's Landing. Mina had stared at the result for ten minutes before deciding that looking was already a line crossed and pretending otherwise wouldn't uncross it.

She'd left Seattle at four with a granola bar, a full tank, and the specific, slightly unhinged confidence of a woman who had decided that one real number attached to one fake address meant something and was not going to be talked out of it, not by her own better judgment and certainly not by whatever this road was doing.

She found the actual route eventually, the long way, past a Willapa Bay tideflat so wide and flat and silver under the low cloud that it looked, for one disorienting stretch, less like land meeting water than like the world hadn't finished deciding which one this particular acre was going to be — oyster stakes marching out into it in long dark rows, a couple of battered flat-bottomed boats pulled up on the mud, the whole bay smelling, even through a closed window, like salt and something green and faintly, healthily rotten, nothing at all like the ferry-dock postcard version of the Washington coast she'd half expected without admitting she was expecting it.

Corbin's Landing turned out to be smaller than the map made it look and more alive than the word "small" implied — a single main street doing the work of a whole downtown, a bait shop, a diner with its lights still on, a Grange hall with a marquee advertising a pancake breakfast three weeks out, and, at the far end, a hardware store with an apartment window lit up above it and a hand-painted sign reading VASKO HARDWARE — EST. 1962 in paint that had faded to almost nothing and been touched up, badly, more than once.

She parked across the street and sat there for a second with the engine off, rain ticking on the roof, and had the sudden, sinking realization that she had absolutely no plan for what came after finding the building. She didn't have his real address. She didn't have his real phone number. What she had was a working theory, four hours of driving, and a genetics consult she wasn't technically authorized to still be pursuing.

The diner was closest, and warm, and had a bell over the door, and the woman behind the counter — sixty-something, reading glasses on a beaded chain, a name tag that said BETTE in handwriting gone soft from repeated laundering — looked up at Mina with the specific, instant, small-town calculation of somebody deciding, correctly, that she was looking at a stranger.

"You lost, hon, or looking for something?"

"Actually — I'm trying to find someone. Warren Locke? I think he lives around here."

Something happened in Bette's face that Mina would spend the whole drive back trying to name — not suspicion exactly, and not quite the automatic friendliness of a small-town diner either. Something closer to the specific, guarded fondness people get when you ask about somebody they've quietly decided is theirs to protect.

"Depends who's asking," Bette said, and set down her coffee pot, and did not pour Mina a cup.

Chapter Seven

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 005 / Assignment 005. Not final prose.)
09_corbins_landing.md

Bette didn't call anybody in front of her. She disappeared into the back for what felt like considerably longer than pouring a to-go cup should take, and came back with the to-go cup and a look that had firmed up into something more like a decision than a feeling, and said, "Hardware store. Ask for Denny. Tell him Bette sent you, or he won't give you the time of day, and honestly he might not anyway."

The bell over Vasko Hardware's door was louder than the diner's, an actual old-fashioned shop bell on a coiled spring, and the man who looked up from behind the counter — sixty-something, forearms like somebody who'd done actual manual work for actual decades, a Seahawks cap that had seen better administrations — did not look especially glad to see her.

"Help you?"

"I'm looking for Warren Locke. Bette sent me."

"Bette sends everybody. Bette likes sending people." Denny didn't come around the counter. He had a radio in pieces in front of him, an old CB by the look of the casing, wires laid out in a row with more care than the job strictly needed, and he went back to it while he talked, which Mina understood, correctly, as a specific kind of unhurried disrespect. "What's your business with Warren."

"I'm with Clinical Genetics at Elliott Bay. He was in the hospital, briefly, and I need to follow up with him."

"He give you this address?"

"No."

"Right." Denny set down the wire stripper. Looked at her properly for the first time, and there was nothing performative in it — just a flat, unhappy assessment, the kind she recognized from her own face in the mirror on the worst days, the specific look of somebody deciding how much trouble a stranger might be. "See, here's my problem. I had a niece, years back, needed to get somewhere nobody who'd hurt her could follow, and it took some doing, and some people looking the other way on purpose, and it worked, and she's alive today because of it. So when somebody I don't know comes around asking pointed questions about a man who keeps to himself and doesn't advertise where he lives, my first instinct isn't curiosity. It's deciding how much trouble you might be. So far, you're not making the easy case."

"I'm not trying to hurt him."

"Everybody who's ever hurt anybody said that first." He said it without heat, which was somehow worse than if he'd been angry — just a fact he'd learned the hard way and wasn't interested in relitigating. "Warren's a good neighbor. Fixes my own bad ankle into a joke instead of a lecture, which is more than my doctor manages. Shows up Thursdays even when his own leg's apparently held together with duct tape and stubbornness, which is a whole separate conversation I intend to have with him. I don't know what he is or isn't hiding, and it is none of my business, and it's none of yours either, unless he decides otherwise."

Mina thought, absurdly, of Perla folding the newspaper into smaller and smaller rectangles, needing her hands busy while her mind worked something out, and said the only true thing she had left. "His bloodwork doesn't make sense. Not a little. Not a lab-error amount. And I have watched somebody's biology decide to become impossible before, Mr. Vasko, and it wasn't a metaphor, and it wasn't good news, and I would like to actually understand what's happening to him before someone with a lot more money and a lot less patience than me finds the same numbers I did."

That landed somewhere. She watched it land — watched the certainty in his face give a little around something he hadn't known a minute ago.

"He's up above the store," Denny said finally, jerking his chin at the ceiling. "Side door, round back. And one more thing — " He picked the wire stripper back up, already half back to the radio. "If you're wrong about being one of the good ones, I want you to know I will absolutely figure that out eventually. I've got time. I'm not going anywhere."

She believed him completely, and found, climbing the stairs a minute later with rain starting up again against the tin roof, that she was glad of it — glad that whatever Warren Locke actually was, he had at least one person in his corner sharp enough, and stubborn enough, to make her work for this.

Chapter Eight

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 006 / Assignment 006. Not final prose.)
10_the_confrontation.md

He opened the door on the second knock, already halfway into an expression that was clearly meant for Denny — something easy, something about to be a joke — and it fell off his face so fast and so completely that Mina understood, before either of them said a word, that whatever came next was not going to go the way the drive down here had let her imagine it going.

"No," he said.

"Warren—"

"No. You don't get to be here." He wasn't loud. That was almost worse than loud would have been — flat, controlled, the voice of a man doing serious work not to raise it. "I told you to let it go. I asked you, as a favor, and you looked me in the eye and said okay, and then you drove four hours to a town I have never once told a hospital chart the name of, based on an address I made up specifically so nobody would do exactly what you just did."

"I know."

"You know." He laughed, once, no humor in it at all. "That's supposed to make this better?"

"My mother has the disease I've spent my whole career trying to understand, and I met a man whose body doesn't follow any rule I know, and I couldn't—" She stopped, made herself slow down, because she could hear how it sounded, how close it was to an excuse dressed up as an explanation. "I'm not saying that makes it okay."

"Good, because it doesn't." He hadn't moved from the doorway. Behind him she could see a room with almost nothing in it — a bed, a chair, a window — the bareness of somewhere a person had decided, on purpose, never to accumulate. "You get that this isn't a privacy thing, right? Not a HIPAA thing. This is the actual structure of how I stay alive in the world I've had to build, and you walked into the middle of it, followed a name I invented on purpose, talked your way past somebody who has genuinely put himself out for me, and you're standing here explaining that you had feelings about it. I don't doubt you had feelings about it. I'm asking why your feelings got to outrank the one thing I actually asked you for."

She didn't have a good answer to that. She had a true one, which wasn't the same thing, and some part of her — the part that had spent five years learning exactly how much a person could want something and still not be entitled to it — knew better than to offer the true one up like it settled anything.

"You're right," she said. "I don't have a version of this where I'm the reasonable one. I just couldn't let it be nothing. That's not an excuse. It's just what happened."

He looked at her for a long moment. His face didn't soften, exactly — more like a door deciding not to shut all the way.

"You should go," he said. "Not forever. Right now. I need to not be looking at you for a while, and you need to think seriously about whether whatever you're chasing is worth what it's already cost two people you don't actually know."

He shut the door. Not hard. Just completely.

She stood on the landing for a long minute, rain starting up again on the tin roof over Denny's back room, and did not, for once, have the first idea what to do next.

Chapter Nine

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 006 / Assignment 006. Not final prose.)
11_denny_and_warren.md

Denny was waiting at the bottom of the stairs, arms crossed, standing the way a man stands when he's already decided a conversation is going to be long whether the other party likes it or not.

"She's gone," he said. "Drove off about ten minutes ago. Didn't look real happy."

"No, I imagine she didn't."

"You want to tell me what that was, or you want me to guess?"

Warren came down the last few steps slower than the leg strictly required, buying himself a second he didn't end up using well. "She's from the hospital. Genetics. I told you that."

"You told me she was hospital — genetics, you said — and I gave her your address because Bette vouched for her, and now I'm standing here putting together that the reason she drove four hours to find you is that you gave a hospital my phone number without asking me first." Denny's voice hadn't risen, which Warren had long since learned to read as considerably more dangerous than if it had. "That true?"

"...Yes."

"So when that woman called — Denise, whatever her name was — and I told her you were fine, you were just an idiot about doctors, I wasn't doing you a favor. I was doing your job for you. Lying to a hospital on your behalf. Without knowing that's what I was doing."

"It wasn't a lie. You believed it."

"That's not the part I'm upset about." Denny said it slow, like he wanted it to land exactly where he was aiming it. "The part I'm upset about is that you made that decision for me. You didn't ask if I was willing to be the guy who vouches for you to a hospital. You just used me. Because I was there, and I was convenient, and you were confident enough that I'd say the right thing without knowing why I was saying it."

Warren opened his mouth to say something about how careful he'd had to be, how few people he could actually risk this kind of arrangement with, how Denny of all people should understand what it meant that he'd trusted him with even that much — and heard, before he said any of it, exactly how much of an excuse it was going to sound like, and closed his mouth again.

"You're right," he said instead.

Denny blinked. Whatever he'd braced for, it hadn't been that.

"I don't get to decide that for you," Warren said. "I've had a long time to get comfortable making those calls alone, because for a long time there wasn't anybody to ask. That's not a good reason. It's just the actual one." He looked down at his own hands for a moment, the braced leg suddenly the least tired thing about him. "I'm sorry. Genuinely. Not the kind of sorry where I explain myself until you feel bad for being mad. Just sorry. You didn't sign up to be part of my exit plan. I should have asked."

Denny looked at him a long moment, still not uncrossing his arms, and the fight went out of his face, leaving it merely tired. Warren had learned that arguments with Denny rarely ended in victory. Usually they ended here: two men who had run out of useful things to throw at each other.

"Next time," Denny said, "you ask. Doesn't mean I say yes. Means you ask."

"I'll ask."

"Twenty-five years," Warren said, "and you've never once let me finish apologizing before you started negotiating terms."

"Eleven," Denny said.

"You don't get to just pick the number."

"Somebody has to. You're hopeless at it."

"And you're still coming Thursday, to Dot's."

"I always come Thursday."

"Don't say it like a law of physics right now, I'm not in the mood." But something had eased in his shoulders, and he turned back toward the store without waiting to see if Warren followed, which Warren understood, correctly, as forgiveness arriving in its actual native shape: not a statement, just a door left open.

Chapter Ten

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 006 / Assignment 006. Not final prose.)
12_perla_alone.md

It was the Saturday after the crash. Perla had been getting herself to the cemetery on the anniversary for thirty-one years, and she had no intention of starting this particular year with an audience.

"I can drive myself," she said, for the third time, standing in her own doorway with car keys already in hand and a bouquet of yellow chrysanthemums — Ernesto's favorite, though he'd never once said so out loud; she'd simply known, the way you know things about a person you've spent three decades reading — wrapped in newspaper on the counter behind her.

"I know you can drive yourself. I want to come."

"You want to supervise."

"I want to spend time with you."

"You want to sit in the passenger seat of my own car making sure I remember where the cemetery is, which, for your information, I have found on my own every single year since before you had a driver's license, including the year it snowed, including the year the GPS in my head was working considerably better than the actual electronic one in your father's old truck." Perla picked up the flowers, checked her reflection in the hallway mirror with the critical squint she reserved for exactly two occasions — church, and this — and adjusted a bobby pin that did not need adjusting. "I let you come to the grocery store with me. I let you sit through Mass with me, even though you fall asleep during the homily and think I don't notice. This is mine, Mina. One day a year. Your father and I had a whole marriage's worth of things that were nobody's business but ours, and this is what's left of that, and I would like to have it without somebody watching my face for signs I've forgotten what year it is."

Mina opened her mouth, closed it again.

"He proposed to me in a cemetery, did you know that? Not this one — back home. His grandmother's grave. Very morbid, very Catholic, very much your father, who thought crying at funerals was for people who hadn't properly prepared themselves for death and then cried harder than anyone at every single one we ever went to." Perla's mouth did something complicated — most of a smile, with real weather moving underneath it. "I am going to go stand by a rock with his name on it and tell him about his granddaughter — born two years after we buried him, never laid eyes on her, his loss entirely — and complain about my hip and eat lunch by myself afterward at the restaurant he liked that you think has bad service, and none of that requires a witness. It requires the opposite of a witness. Let me have this badly, embarrassingly, entirely to myself."

"Okay," Mina said, and meant it, and hated, only a little, how much it cost her to mean it.

Perla was almost out the door before she turned back, flowers under one arm, and said, in a completely different voice, sharper, more herself than she'd sounded in weeks: "And don't you dare have somebody follow me to make sure I'm all right. I will know. I have always known when you people think I need minding, and I have never once appreciated it, diagnosis or no diagnosis."

She was gone before Mina could decide whether to be embarrassed at having been caught planning exactly that.

Chapter Eleven

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 006 / Assignment 006. Not final prose.)
13_voss.md

Two days earlier — the Thursday after the crash — Julian Voss kept exactly one photograph of Ana on his desk, and it was not the one people expected.

Visitors — donors, mostly, the ones who came through Halcyon's Seattle offices wanting to see where their money went before they wrote the next check — always assumed it would be a graduation photo, or something from before, back when the disease was still a shadow on a scan instead of a fact eating a calendar. It wasn't. The photograph on Julian's desk was from eleven months into her decline, taken by his ex-wife on a phone none of them had thought to make sure was charged, and it showed Ana at twenty-six, bald from a trial drug that hadn't worked, laughing so hard at something off-camera that she'd knocked over a water glass, one hand still raised mid-gesture, entirely unaware the picture was being taken.

He kept that one because it was true. The others were true too, but they were true about a version of her that hadn't needed him to fail her yet.

"Dr. Voss?" His assistant, Priyanka, in the doorway with a tablet she was visibly nervous to be holding. "The Elliott Bay data-sharing report came in — through the standing partnership. I think you're going to want to see this one yourself."

He took the tablet. Read the flagged summary twice, the way he read everything that mattered twice, and felt an unwelcome lift in his chest, the kind he'd learned, over six years of running a company built entirely on other people's worst days, to distrust in himself more than almost anything else he felt.

Hope, arriving uninvited, had cost him more than grief ever had.

He read it a third time anyway.

A fifty-two-year-old male, trauma admission, healing and remodeling markers inconsistent with any documented human physiology, currently declining further workup. He thought, unbidden, the way he always thought it, in the voice he still sometimes heard clearest of all the voices he had left of her: Dad, if there's even a chance—

She had never actually said that. He knew that. She had, in fact, spent her last eight lucid months telling him to stop — stop funding the trial, stop flying in specialists, stop treating her dying like a problem with a solution he simply hadn't purchased yet, because watching him refuse to accept it was, in her words, the actual thing that was killing her faster than the disease was. He had not listened. He had built an entire company out of not listening, and he had told himself, every day since, that the company was proof he'd been right to keep going, not evidence he'd never actually heard her.

He believed — genuinely, not as a cover story he told donors, not as a thing he'd talked himself into — that if this man's biology could be understood, actually understood, down to the mechanism, it could plausibly do for a hundred thousand families exactly what nothing had been able to do for his. He believed a company with the resources to pursue that had an obligation to pursue it that outweighed almost anything else on the table, including, if it came down to it, one man's preference not to be studied.

He knew how that sounded. He'd made his peace with it a long time ago — the same week he'd stopped being able to tell the difference between his convictions and his grief — and had decided not to go looking for that line again, because he suspected that if he ever found it, it would cost him the only thing he had left that felt like purpose.

"Get me everything on the patient," he said. "Discreetly. And find out who requested the genetics consult — I want to talk to them before anyone else does."

Whoever had asked the question would know the patient. Follow the curiosity; it always led to a body.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twelve

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 008, minimum present-day connective material. Not final prose.)
14_denny_and_voss_approach.md

Denny caught him on the stairs a week into it — into whatever this was becoming, evenings and beer and Warren talking longer, in more directions, than he'd let himself talk to anyone since before Denny's grandfather was born — and didn't say anything at first, just looked at him with the weighing expression of a man deciding how much of his own business a friend's business actually was.

"You're telling her things," Denny said finally.

"Some things."

"Good things or the kind of things that get a man's whole life burned down?"

"I don't know yet which category it's going to turn out to be." Warren sat down on the step above him, leg fully healed now, which was its own small ongoing miracle nobody in this conversation was going to mention. "I keep telling myself I'll stop before it matters. I've stopped believing that particular lie about three tellings ago."

"You want my opinion?"

"Not really, but you're going to give it to me anyway."

"You've spent longer being careful than most people spend being alive. I don't know what you are, and I've made my peace with not knowing." Denny picked at a splinter on the railing, not looking at him. "You think I never noticed you don't age? Twenty-five years, Warren. I'm not blind. I'm polite."

"But careful's not the same as safe. A man can be careful his whole life and still die alone in a room nobody ever really got into. I watched my brother do it. Wasn't pretty from the outside, either."

Warren didn't have anything to say to that for a second.

"There's also," Denny added, standing, brushing off his knees, "a fella been asking around town this week. Not local. Nice suit, nicer car, staying out at the motel on 105, asking Bette real polite questions about the guy who lives above the hardware store, like he already half-knew the answers and just wanted to hear somebody say them out loud." He looked up at Warren with something that wasn't quite a warning and wasn't quite reassurance. "Twenty-three years I've known you and you're still the most careful careless man I've ever met."

"Eleven," Warren said.

"You're terrible at this."

"You're the one who can't keep a number."

"Bette didn't tell him anything. But you should know people are starting to look."

Warren sat with that for a long moment after Denny had gone back inside, listening to the rain start up again on the tin roof, thinking that he had exactly two live options — go quiet again, disappear the way he knew how to disappear better than almost anything else he'd ever learned how to do, or keep doing the thing he was currently doing, which was standing very still at the exact center of his own life for the first time in longer than he cared to remember, while something with a nice suit and a nicer car got steadily, patiently closer.

He went upstairs. Mina was already there, sitting on his floor with two beers she'd apparently decided didn't need an invitation anymore.

That still surprised him.

Not the beer. The fact that she was there at all.

The first time she'd climbed those stairs, he'd shut the door in her face. She had deserved it. More importantly, she'd known she deserved it. She hadn't come back the next morning with another argument or an apology engineered to make him forgive her.

*

Three days after he'd shut the door, Denny knocked and held out a plain envelope with the air of a man delivering a summons he hadn't read.

"Left it downstairs," Denny said. "Said to give it to you. I told her the stairs were your problem, not mine, and she said she'd already figured that out."

"You read it."

"It's a business card, Warren. There's only so much mystery available."

There was no letter inside. Just her card — Minerva Mendez, Clinical Genetics — with one sentence written across the back in a fast, impatient hand:

You were right. I won't come looking again. If you ever want to talk, you know where I am.

He put it on the windowsill. He told himself it was just to keep it from getting lost, which was true the way most of his lies to himself were true — technically accurate and completely beside the point.

On the fourth day he moved it six inches to the left while dusting a sill he had never dusted before in the entire time he'd lived there, and caught himself, and put it back, and didn't dust anything else.

On the ninth day Denny came up to check a water stain on the ceiling, looked at the card, looked at Warren, and said, "You gonna call her or just keep rearranging that thing?"

"I'm not rearranging it."

"You've moved it four times today."

"I've moved it twice."

"Still rearranging."

On the eleventh night he sat on the edge of the bed for a long time with the card in his hand, running his thumb over the embossed letters of her name, and thought about all the names he'd stopped answering to over the years, all the doors he'd shut and meant it, and how this one small rectangle of cardstock from a woman who'd written you were right and meant it was somehow heavier than all of them.

On the twelfth day, he called — from the cheap prepaid he'd bought with cash on the fourth day, his old phone not having survived the accident any better than the car had.

She picked up on the second ring. "Mendez."

"This is Warren Locke."

A pause. Not a surprised one — a careful one, like she was deciding which version of herself to be. "I know."

"I thought — coffee. Somewhere public. I owe you at least the beginning of an explanation, or that chart's going to eat the next six months of your life."

"When."

"Wednesday. There's—"

"I know the diner," she said. "Bette's. Ten o'clock. And Warren — thank you for calling."

He sat holding the dead phone for a while after.

She drove down Wednesday. He'd intended public, neutral, an hour at most — enough to make the impossible numbers less likely to ruin her career, not enough to start anything. Instead Bette took one look at the two of them, said "corner booth's quieter," and by the third refill Mina had asked about the scar on his left hand and he'd told her the truth — a fishing accident, seventy years before she was born, a boat whose name he still remembered — and watched her do the arithmetic in her face and decide, visibly, not to flinch.

They talked until Bette started turning chairs upside-down onto the tables around them.

"Come on," Mina said finally, standing. "Your coffee's a prosecutable offense and I want to see if it's worse at the source."

It was worse at the source. She drank it anyway. They talked until after midnight about almost everything except the thing she'd originally come to ask — her mother's illness, his first winter in Corbin's Landing, a documentary she'd watched about shipwrecks, the way the rain sounded different on his tin roof than on hers — and somewhere around eleven he realized he was telling her things he hadn't said out loud to anyone since before Denny's grandfather was born, and he let himself keep going, because stopping would have meant deciding what it meant, and he wasn't ready to decide that yet.

*

Then she'd come back.

And back again — she'd negotiated the family-medical flexibility with Okonkwo, her department head, reduced hours, the truth with the impossible parts left out, and taken a room over Bette's in town, because four hours each way had stopped being a commute and started being a punchline.

Now she had a preferred patch of floor because the apartment still had only one chair, knew where he kept the bottle opener, and had stopped pretending she didn't hate the beer he bought for himself.

Warren stood in the doorway longer than necessary.

Mina looked up. "You planning to come in?"

"Still deciding."

"You live here."

"That's weighing heavily in your favor."

She held up one of the bottles. "I brought the good kind."

"You brought the expensive kind. Different category."

"Sit down, Warren."

He did.

Denny's warning was still downstairs with him: a nice suit, a nicer car, somebody asking questions. Every sensible instinct he'd developed across a succession of names, cities, languages and graves told him this was the moment to leave. He knew how to disappear before curiosity became attention. He knew how to make Warren Locke stop existing so thoroughly that six months from now Denny would receive a postcard from somewhere Warren wasn't.

He had done versions of it before.

What he had never become good at was the part where somebody remained behind.

Mina nudged the unopened beer toward him with two fingers. "Bad day?"

"Complicated one."

"Want to tell me?"

There it was. No demand hidden inside it. No professional entitlement. Not even the relentless curiosity that had brought her to his door the first time.

A question he was allowed to refuse.

That, more than anything, was why he didn't.

He took the bottle.

"Okay," he said. "Where were we?"

Interlude Working draft

Memory-Story: You Are (Warren, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — finished conversion per Handoff 008. Replaces the prior third-person interlude. Do NOT confirm this Eternal's later identity — fair foreshadowing only. Not final prose.)
interlude_you_are.md

That night, she brought the better beer and no coffee, which he understood, correctly, as an invitation to keep talking rather than a favor.

"You stopped at the good part," she said, handing him one.

The refusal arrived automatically. He'd used some version of it in more languages than he cared to count. This time he let it die behind his teeth. "All right. But this one's longer, and it doesn't have a tidy edge to it the way the first one did, so you're going to have to just come along."

"I can come along."

He believed her.

"After the astonishment," he said, "there was a long time — I'm not going to pretend I can tell you how long, because time wasn't the kind of thing yet that has a number attached — where it was just me. Just the fact, over and over. I didn't know I was waiting for anything. You can't wait for something you don't know is possible." He picked at the bottle's label with his thumbnail, not drinking, just needing somewhere to put his attention that wasn't her face. "And then I reached for myself — the way I always did, turning the one fact over, because that was the only motion I had — and something reached back."

"Reached back how?"

"Not an echo. I want to be specific about that, because it matters. An echo gives you back what you sent, dimmer, delayed. This wasn't dimmer. Wasn't delayed. It was someone else, entirely, saying the exact same impossible thing I'd been saying, in a voice that had never been mine, about a fact I didn't own." He shook his head slightly, still a little amazed by his own memory of it. "I did not have a category for that. There'd never been a two of anything. I had to build the idea of more than one true thing from scratch, in about the time it takes to be startled."

"What was she like?" — and then, catching herself: "Sorry. He, she, it, I don't even know what pronoun—"

"She works. She'd have laughed at you for apologizing for it, for what it's worth." For the first time that evening he looked away from her. "She was quick. Quicker than me, in the way that mattered — I turned things over slowly, worried at them, and she just arrived at things, the way weather arrives, no warm-up. Where I was careful, she'd already decided. Where I hesitated, she'd already said the thing out loud and was waiting, a little impatient, for me to catch up. First thing she ever said to me, near as I can translate it, was something like finally, which tells you basically everything you need to know about her."

"You're smiling."

"Apparently I am." He didn't try to stop. "We didn't become the same thing, if that's where your head's going. That's the mistake everyone makes about all of this, later — they think recognition means dissolving into each other, sameness, one big warm soup where nobody's themselves anymore. It's the opposite. The whole staggering discovery was that she stayed exactly, entirely herself, close as we got, and so did I, and neither of us got smaller for it. That was the actual miracle. Not that we found each other. That finding each other didn't cost either of us anything real."

"You are," Mina said, quietly, testing the shape of it. "That's the next line, isn't it. After I am."

"That's the next line." He met her eyes, then reached for his beer before the silence acquired a question of its own. "She'd have liked you, incidentally. God knows why I think that. She'd have liked how you don't let anything go."

"Is that a compliment?"

"From her, it would've been the highest one available." He drank, finally, and didn't say anything else about it that night, and Mina, watching him not say it, filed the shape of that silence away next to all the others she was starting to notice he had.

Interlude Working draft

Memory-Story: We Are (Warren, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — finished conversion per Handoff 008. Replaces the prior third-person interlude. Not final prose.)
interlude_we_are.md

"So it was just the two of you for a while," Mina said, the next time, picking up exactly where they'd left off like no days had passed in between, which he was starting to recognize as one of her particular gifts. "And then what?"

"And then there were more. Not all at once." He was quiet a moment before answering. "Some of what's coming, I saw myself. Some of it, other Eternals told me, later, in the way Harmony lets you actually hand someone a memory instead of just describing it — which isn't the same as having lived it, whatever it feels like from the inside. And some of it I'm still just piecing together, because nobody who was there at the very beginning of this part is around anymore to ask. I'll try to be honest about which is which, but I want you to know going in that this one's stitched together, not lived whole the way the first one was."

"Noted."

"They kept arriving. Slowly, then not slowly. And here's the thing that mattered more than anything else that happened in that whole stretch, the thing I'd want you to actually hold onto if you forget everything else I tell you tonight: none of them collapsed into each other."

"What do you mean, collapsed?"

"I mean it would have been simpler if we had. A single note's easy to sing. A single note sung by a thousand throats is still, at the end of the day, one note." He leaned his head back against the bed frame, eyes half-closed, somewhere else for a second. "That's not what happened. Every new one that showed up stayed exactly as separate as she and I had been from each other. This one liked to reach out first. That one waited to be found. This one's I am rang low and slow, like something settling. That one's arrived quick and bright, like something catching a light. Nobody was better at existing than anybody else. We were just differently ourselves, all the way down, and it turned out that wasn't a problem the growing crowd of us needed to solve."

"That was the whole point."

"That was the entire point. Sameness was never the discovery. Difference was." He opened his eyes. "We are, we learned to say, eventually, to the whole shifting company of us — not one voice pretending to speak for everybody, never that, but a lot of separate voices choosing, every time, to mean the same true thing together without meaning it identically. Harder than I am. Immeasurably harder than you are. A hundred separate truths agreeing to stand in the same place at the same time isn't a small thing to hold, and it didn't go smoothly — there were false starts, recognitions that came too fast and scared one side or the other back into solitude for a while. We had to find a shape for it that could actually hold, and that took — I don't have a number for you. Longer than your species has existed. Considerably longer."

"And her? Where was she in all this?"

His thumb stopped against the label. "Close by. Not always at the center of it — she wasn't much for crowds. She liked finding the one or two who mattered in a room more than working the whole room — but close by. That's a fair question and I'm going to keep answering it in pieces, if that's all right. Not tonight, the rest of it."

Mina didn't push. She was, he'd noticed, getting good at knowing exactly how far the door was open before pushing on it made it shut.

"One more thing, though," he said, before she could get up. "Somewhere in the middle of all that reaching and finding — none of us quite noticed the moment it happened — we started to feel something else. Not another consciousness. Not another I am. Something with its own weight, patient, wordless, gathering out past all of us in the dark. We didn't look at it very closely yet. We were still busy learning each other's names."

"What was it?"

He smiled, finally, something almost boyish about it for just a second — and then the smile went careful, the way it did when he'd decided exactly how much of a thing to hand her.

"That's a whole different story."

"You're not going to tell me."

"Not tonight." He stood, which was as close as he ever came to ending an evening formally. "Some things I don't tell you because I don't know them. That one I don't tell you because I do."
