Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: Through the Wall (Warren, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — rewritten for Batch 2 per Creator cosmology expansion. Not final prose.)
memory_abraham.md

"This one's different from the others," Warren said, the next evening, on his floor with his back to the wall, and Mina noticed he'd brought his own beer this time, which felt like a small, deliberate act of reciprocity. "Not the dark one," she said. "Not tonight," he said. "This one first. The first two, I lived. Walked around inside, more or less, the only way a thing with no body walks around inside anything. This one I didn't live. I felt it. There's a difference, and I want you to hold onto that difference, because it matters for basically everything I tell you from here on — some of what I know, I know the way you know your own hand. Some of it I know the way you know a sound through a wall. This is a through-the-wall one."

"Okay."

"First I need you to understand what the wall was like, because without that, the rest of this is just another patriarch story and it isn't. By this point there are a great many of us joined to humans, scattered all over, and the joining is real — the Soul is there, in the person, the way yours is in you right now — but the hearing, both directions, is terrible. Fog. Most humans never consciously hear the Eternal joined to them, not once in a whole life. And most Eternals can't clearly hear their human back. We got — impressions. Dreams. A feeling that arrived with no sender. A certainty about something that turned out to be true and the human having no idea where the certainty came from. Moments, sometimes, of something shallow and temporary, two selves brushing each other and coming apart again before either of them could hold still for it." He turned the bottle slowly. "That's most of what early religion actually is, if you want the unglamorous version. People were experiencing something real. And then they had to explain it with whatever words and weather and politics they had. The explaining is where it went strange. The experiencing wasn't invented."

"So before Abraham — nobody was talking. Just static."

"Static with occasional music in it. And on our side it was just as bad. Imagine being joined to someone — joined, I mean it the way I mean it when I talk about Harmony, two real selves sharing one life — and spending centuries straining to hear them the way you'd strain to hear someone calling your name from inside a sealed room. We knew they were there. We could feel them. We couldn't reach them, and they couldn't reach us, and nobody had any idea how long that was going to go on being true."

Mina had gone very still.

"And then one night, somewhere in a stretch of country that doesn't have the name yet that it's going to have, an old man is sitting outside his tent in the middle of the night, and he is afraid."

"Abraham."

"Not yet, in the telling — he's just an old man leading a small camp through country that doesn't belong to him, with everything he owns walking on four legs and everything he loves walking on two, and there's a raiding party three days behind that everyone in camp knows about and nobody's said out loud, because saying it out loud uses up courage they need for the walking. Middle of the night. Everyone else asleep, or pretending to be. And this man is sitting outside his tent with a knife he doesn't know how to use as well as he'd like anyone to think, looking at more stars than your generation's ever going to see with your own eyes, thinking, as clear and as loud as anything I have ever felt come off anything with a body — I am so afraid, and I am still here, and that has to mean something."

Warren stopped. He was looking at the floor, and his voice, when he went on, had changed into something Mina hadn't heard in him before — not sadness, exactly. Something older. Reverence would have been too religious a word, and he would have hated it, but there was no other shape for it.

"And then the second part happened. That's the part nobody puts in the story, because nobody on the human side had the vocabulary for it and nobody on our side had ever felt it before and lived to describe it. He answered. Not to a voice — I don't think there was a voice, not the way the stories mean it. Something reached toward him, the way it had reached toward ten thousand frightened people before him, through the fog, and this time — this time the fog didn't hold. He heard. And he said, back, in whatever form a man like that has for saying it — a prayer, a thought, a decision made out loud in the dark where nobody could hear him but the stars — he said: I hear you."

The room was very quiet.

"That's it," Warren said. "That's the whole moment. No trumpets. No voice out of a burning anything. Just a frightened, stubborn old man doing arithmetic on his own courage in the dark — and then, across a wall we'd been shouting through for longer than his species had been building cities, another consciousness saying I hear you. You have to understand what that was, on our side. It was the first time. The first time any of us had ever been answered. Not felt. Not guessed at through dreams and static. Answered."

Mina's voice was barely there. "Was that — the One? Reaching toward him?"

"The One had been reaching toward everyone, for longer than I can make you feel. He was — " Warren stopped, rolled the bottle between his palms, started again. "He was the only one of us still intact, the only one who hadn't started to fracture, and he knew it, and he was afraid of it. Not panicked. The way you'd be afraid watching a crack spread across ice you're standing on. He believed — and I want you to hear this as his reasoning, not as scripture, not as an excuse — he believed he needed a human line clear enough that he could join it completely someday, from the very beginning of a life, without destroying the human or eventually losing himself. That was the project. And Abraham's night was the first proof the project could work at all."

"Because Abraham heard him."

"Because Abraham heard him back. That's the difference that matters. And then — this is the part that changed everything after it — the One started noticing that the hearing ran in the blood. Not perfectly. Not cleanly. But Abraham's children, and their children, kept turning up easier to reach than anyone else. Unusually, impossibly easy to hear, generation after generation. Whatever made that man's mind audible to us, some of it could be inherited. So the project became the lineage. Preserve it. Strengthen it. Keep it clear enough, for long enough, that someday a human life could begin already joined, all the way, from the first breath."

"That's the lineage."

"That's the lineage. And I need you to actually hear this part, because it matters more than the rest of it combined: it was never about being better. Being audible isn't being superior. A shout isn't more true than a whisper. It's just easier to hear. Abraham's descendants weren't more valuable humans. They carried a pattern — a compatibility pattern — that the One was trying to preserve and refine, the way you'd preserve anything fragile and irreplaceable that kept slipping through your fingers. That is the whole of it. Anyone who ever used that lineage to say one people matter more than another was using it for something it was never for."

Mina was quiet for a long time. "You said 'the One' like you're talking about someone you knew."

"I talk about him in the third person because the man saying this to you wasn't there." He turned the bottle slowly. "What I carry of those centuries came through the Harmony — shared, not lived. Closer than a sound through a wall; not as close as my own hands. Hold onto that difference. It matters for everything after this." He looked at her for a long moment. "You'd have been a loud one, back then."

Mina didn't ask what he meant by that — some of his sentences were doors he opened partway and then held shut, and most nights the kindest thing was to let them be.

But this time she didn't let it stay shut all the way.

"Was he angry?" she asked. "The One. In the old stories he's — the severity. The flood, the plagues, all of it. Was that real, or was that the explaining going strange?"

Warren didn't answer for a while. He finished his beer. Set the bottle down with a care that suggested the bottle had done nothing to deserve being the thing he was careful with.

"Some of it was real," he said finally. "Not the arithmetic — the retellings always get the arithmetic wrong, the body counts, the weather as punishment, that's what fear does to a story over centuries. But the frustration behind it was real. He'd waited centuries for the line to become what it needed to become, and he had that patience, the cosmic kind, genuinely. And then he'd lose his temper at a single generation for not hearing what sounded, to him, like a man speaking at ordinary volume. He cared about them. He protected them. He was also controlling, and angry, and too certain that knowing more entitled him to decide more." He looked up. "It was the same consciousness, Mina. Not a different god who got nicer later. The same one, earlier in the lesson. The severity and the tenderness were the same person learning."

"That's either the most comforting or the least comforting thing you've said all week."

"Welcome to how I feel about most of it, pretty much every day for two thousand years."

Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: Keeping Faith (Warren, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 008. Reader-visible title "Keeping Faith" per Archivist ruling. Not final prose.)
memory_moses.md

Warren didn't start this one the way he'd started the others. He was quiet a long time first, on the floor across from her, turning the bottle cap over between two fingers, and Mina had learned by now not to fill silences like that one.

"Moses is harder to tell than Abraham," he said finally. "Abraham I only ever heard from a distance, one clear night among a lot of ordinary ones. Moses I heard for years. Heard, not lived — the same arrangement as Abraham, only closer. Whole stretches of a very long, very hard life, and most of what got written down about him afterward compresses forty years of a man slowly, painfully learning how to keep faith into about a dozen dramatic scenes, because that's what stories do to a life — they keep the peaks and let the exhausted middle go. I want to give you a piece of the middle instead."

"Okay."

"There's a day — one of hundreds like it, nothing special about the date, nothing anybody would have marked on a calendar even if they'd had one — where the people he's been leading for longer than he ever agreed to are thirsty, and furious, and blaming him for it, which is fair, since he's the one who told them this was the way out. And he is so tired. I don't mean tired the way you mean it after a bad shift. I mean tired the way a rope goes, fiber by fiber, until the day it just doesn't hold anymore. And there's a rock, and there's supposed to be water in it if he does the thing he's been told to do, gently, the way faith is supposed to work — and instead he hits it. Hard. Twice. Out of rage he'd have been ashamed of an hour later, rage that had nothing to do with the rock and everything to do with forty years of a job he never asked for and never once got to put down."

"And the water came anyway."

"The water came anyway." Warren looked at his hands. "That's the part people skip past, when they tell it as a morality lesson about one man's punishment for one bad afternoon. What I actually felt, from wherever I was, wasn't a man being punished. It was a man discovering, in the single worst, most human moment of his whole impossible life, that faith had never been about doing it right. He'd been doing it right for forty years and it had cost him everything he had. This one time he did it wrong — furious, human, completely understandable — and it worked anyway, and I think that broke something open in him that all the obedience never had. Not despair. The opposite. He finally understood that the presence reaching toward him wasn't grading the form. It was just there. Steady. Whether he hit the rock right or wrong."

"Is that what faith means? In your — in the actual cosmology?"

"It's what I think he understood, that day, better than almost any human before or since. Faith isn't certainty. It isn't good behavior, either. It's continuing to reach toward something you can't prove is reaching back, long after reaching has stopped feeling like a choice and started feeling like the only shape you have left. He kept reaching for forty years through fear and rage and a level of exhaustion I don't think your species has a word ugly enough for. That's the whole thing. That's Harmony's actual on-ramp, as far as I've ever been able to tell — not belief, not proof, just refusing to stop reaching."

Warren was quiet for a moment, and when he went on his voice had changed, less storyteller now, more like a man admitting to something he'd been avoiding saying out loud for a long time.

"And since we're talking about him — I'll say something I don't say often, because it's the part of the old stories I'd rather pretend belongs entirely to the people who wrote them down scared. Some of the severity in those stories wasn't invented by frightened scribes. Some of it was real. You know my whole speech on that by now — the arithmetic, the frustration, the same consciousness earlier in the lesson. The part I skip, usually, is saying it about Moses specifically: he got the worst of the severity and the best of the tenderness, sometimes in the same week, and he kept reaching anyway."

Mina was quiet for a while. "That's not the version they taught us."

"No," Warren said. "I know."

"You said you heard him for years. Did he ever hear you back? Clearly, I mean. Not just feeling something."

"Closer than almost anyone before him. Not clean. Nothing about it was clean." "He never got the whole picture. Nobody had, yet, not cleanly. Moses was reaching toward the presence he understood as God. The oldest part of me was the presence reaching back — and what I carry of that exchange is shared Eternal memory, not something the human man sitting here lived. Moses still had to translate what reached him into the vocabulary he had, and the One was still learning how badly an Eternal could misunderstand a human even while hearing him. I think that's actually the truest thing I can tell you about most of what got written down as scripture, if you want the honest version instead of the comfortable one. Not lies. Translations. Some of them extraordinary. None of them complete."

"That's either the most comforting or the least comforting thing you've said all week."

"Welcome to how I feel about most of it, pretty much every day for two thousand years." He turned the bottle cap over once more between his fingers, then set it down on the floor between them, deliberate. "Fair warning — it starts getting personal from here."

Part Three — We Are
Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirteen

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 009. Not final prose.)
15_data_and_okonkwo.md

The data-sharing log took Mina four days to actually get access to, and about four minutes to understand once she had it, which felt, in retrospect, like an unfair ratio for something this important.

Elliott Bay's research office ran a dozen active partnerships — university consortiums, two pharmaceutical trials, a public health grant with the county — and buried in the middle of the list, unremarkable as a grocery receipt, was a line item for Halcyon Biosciences: a standing agreement, three years old, granting access to de-identified anomaly flags across several departments, including Genetics. Including, as far as she could tell from the access logs, the exact flag that had gone out on Warren Locke's chart the night of his accident.

She sat with that for a long minute in an empty conference room that smelled like old coffee and toner, thought about Denny's stranger in the nice suit, and felt something cold and precise settle into place where her stomach used to be.

She should not have been looking at this. She knew that clearly, the same way she'd known, months ago, that she shouldn't drive four hours to a coastal town chasing a patient who'd told her to let it go — and she printed the log anyway, folded it into her bag, and was three steps from the door when Dr. Okonkwo caught her in the hallway with the unhurried patience of a department head who'd been meaning to have this conversation for a while and had finally run out of reasons to put it off.

"My office. Five minutes."

It took closer to twenty. Okonkwo laid it out plainly enough: a closed consult with no further workup requested, reopened without authorization; a research-office access log pulled under her own credentials for a case that wasn't hers anymore; and — this was the part that actually landed — a call from Halcyon's own compliance office two weeks back, polite, thorough, asking pointed questions about "an employee's personal interest" in one of their shared research flags.

"They called about me."

"They called about the flag. Your name came up because you're the one who's kept touching it." Okonkwo wasn't unkind about it, which somehow made it worse. "I don't know what you're chasing, Ms. Mendez, and at this point I'm not sure I want to. What I need from you is to stop touching it through hospital systems, effective today, or I'm going to have a conversation with HR that neither of us wants to have."

"Understood."

"Is it?"

Mina thought about Warren's face in that hospital room, thought about the laundromat in Tacoma, thought about the folder currently sitting in her bag containing exactly the information she'd just been ordered to stop gathering. "Understood," she said again, and meant, this time, only that she'd heard the words.

She didn't stop. She drove back to Corbin's Landing that same evening, for the first time in her own car instead of a rental, and told Warren everything — the log, Okonkwo, Halcyon's own compliance office already circling close enough to have her name in a file somewhere — and watched him read the page twice, then fold it once along a crease that wasn't there before.

"They're not just asking around town anymore," he said finally.

"No."

"They're asking about you."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Fourteen

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 009, short vignette. Not final prose.)
16_dennys_thursday.md

Thursday night, same as every Thursday night for the better part of two decades, four men sat around the folding table in the back of Vasko Hardware and lied to each other about the size of their hands, and Denny had never once, in all that time, told any of them what he actually did for the twenty-five years — twenty-three, if you didn't count the winter — a strange man had lived above his store.

"You're distracted," said Gary Holt, who ran the marina and had a tell so obvious it was practically a public service. "You're never distracted on a Thursday."

"I'm thinking about your bet, Gary, which is a bad one."

"I fold when I want to fold."

"Nobody's ever once wanted to fold at the exact moment they were supposed to fold — that's not a strategy, that's a coincidence with good timing." Denny threw in two chips and didn't look at his own cards, because he'd learned that trick from Warren, of all people — the man could make you doubt a royal flush just by looking bored about it — and won the hand on a pair of nines nobody at the table believed he'd actually had.

He'd met Warren in the fall, twenty-five years back — twenty-six by Warren's count, which started in the January he moved upstairs, a position Denny considered cheating, since a man who's been haunting your store since October doesn't get to pretend the fall didn't happen. That winter took the power lines down for a week and half the town ended up sleeping on cots in the Grange hall, and Warren — new in town, a duffel bag and a cough and no ties anyone could see — spent the whole week hauling blankets and rebuilding the hall's ancient generator out of parts cannibalized from Denny's back room. When the lights came back on, Denny looked at the empty room over the store and said it beat paying to heat a place nobody lived in. That was the entire lease negotiation. Twenty-five years, no paperwork, which Alice still brought up every Thanksgiving like it was a crime.

There'd been the summer they'd re-roofed the store, Warren carrying bundles of shingles up the ladder two at a time like they were nothing, and Denny standing at the bottom doing the math on what a bundle weighed versus what Warren weighed and deciding, finally, that some questions were like some loads — you didn't pick them up unless you were prepared to carry them.

The poker trick Warren had taught him one slow February — don't look at your cards, Denny, the cards aren't the game, the faces are — had won him enough Thursday nights since to cover the generator parts twice over, though he'd never said so out loud. Some debts you paid by keeping them.

He liked Thursdays. He liked them for reasons that had nothing to do with Warren, or Mina, or the fella in the nice suit who'd been back in town twice more this week alone, always polite, always asking Bette or the gas station kid the same soft questions in a slightly different order. Thursdays were Gary's terrible tells and Pete's terrible jokes and Ruth from the Grange complaining from the doorway, every single week, about the exact same pothole on Route 6 the county had been "getting to" since before Denny's knees started complaining back. Thursdays were the actual town underneath the town, the part that didn't need explaining to anybody and never once asked him to keep a secret he hadn't agreed to keep.

He was glad, walking the last of them out past eleven, that at least one thing in his life still didn't have anything to do with any of it.

Question Session Working draft

Question Session: Atlantis / The Ice (Mina and Warren)

(WORKING DRAFT — split from the original question-session file per Archivist instruction to distribute rather than leave as one appendix. Placement: early in the question-session sequence. Not final prose.)
question_atlantis_ice.md

"Okay, serious question." Mina was upside down on his one chair, legs over the back of it, which he'd stopped commenting on weeks ago. "Atlantis."

"Not real. Sorry. Plato made it up to make a philosophical point and then several thousand years of people decided the point was less interesting than the setting."

"That's disappointingly boring."

"I didn't say nothing sank. Things sink constantly, historically speaking, humans build near water for the same reason they always have and water has never once respected that decision long-term. I just said the specific city with the specific name and the specific concentric rings didn't exist the way people want it to. You want an actual good mystery, ask me about the ice."

"The ice."

"There's a period — long before anything you'd call recorded history — where a particular group of very resourceful, very cold, very patient humans figured out you could move genuinely enormous stones if you built your sledges and your track out of packed and engineered ice instead of wood or stone rollers. Seasonal work. You'd spend half the year building the infrastructure and the other half using it before it melted, which is also, conveniently, why nobody ever found the machine. The machine was the weather. It did exactly what it was built to do and then it did what ice does, and by the time anyone was writing anything down, all that was left was a rock somewhere it shouldn't reasonably be and no memory of how it got there."

Mina sat up properly for that one. "That's real?"

"That's what I remember watching, from wherever I was watching from back then, and I want to be honest about how much of it is actually solid. I saw stones that shouldn't have been movable get moved. I saw ice involved — sledges, track, something engineered rather than just weather doing what weather does. What I didn't see, and can't honestly reconstruct, is the planning: how they solved it, how many failures came before the version that worked, whether every site that puzzles your century used the same method or whether I'm remembering one solution and assuming it generalizes. I'd rather tell you a true-shaped gap than a tidy answer. What I'm sure of is smaller than what makes the better story: human patience, human engineering, and a material that had the good manners to clean up after itself. No aliens required. People are so much more interesting than the shortcut everybody keeps reaching for."

Question Session Working draft

Question Session: Ghosts (Mina and Warren)

(WORKING DRAFT — split from the original question-session file per Archivist instruction to distribute rather than leave as one appendix. Placement: anywhere after the pair have an established rhythm. Not final prose.)
question_ghosts.md

"Ghosts."

"Sometimes."

"That's it? 'Sometimes'?"

"Mina, I have had two thousand years and I still don't have a clean answer for that one, so no, you don't get more than 'sometimes' tonight. Something lingers, occasionally, somewhere between memory and Harmony-resonance and whatever your species' nervous system does with grief in an empty room. I've felt things I couldn't explain. I've also watched people mistake a draft and their own longing for a whole theology. Both are true. I'm not going to pretend the second one disproves the first."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Fifteen

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 010. Not final prose.)
18_voss_approaches.md

The email came from Voss's own address, not an assistant's, which Mina understood immediately as a specific kind of message in itself.

Dr. Mendez — I imagine my name means something uncomfortable to you by now, which is fair, and I'd rather have an honest conversation than a lawyer-mediated one. Halcyon isn't interested in causing you professional harm. I'd like fifteen minutes, in person, at a location of your choosing. I think we may be able to help each other. — J. Voss

She read it four times, forwarded it to nobody, and sat with her coffee going cold for long enough that Okonkwo would have had something to say about it if he'd walked by.

She met him at a coffee shop two blocks from the hospital, public, crowded, her own choice of table facing the door, and he arrived alone, in a coat that cost more than her monthly rent and shoes that had clearly walked exactly nowhere difficult in years, and he shook her hand like he meant it.

"Thank you for coming."

"I almost didn't."

"I'd have understood." He sat, didn't order anything, folded his hands on the table in a way that read as deliberate rather than nervous. "I'm going to say the true thing first, because I think you'll respect that more than a pitch. My daughter died. Degenerative illness, five years ago, nothing we threw at it worked. I built a company on the conviction that somewhere out there is biology that could have saved her, or could save the next hundred thousand people like her, and I have spent six years occasionally doing things I'm not proud of in service of that conviction. I'm not going to pretend otherwise to you."

"That's a hell of an opening."

"It's the only opening that gets us anywhere honest." He met her eyes, steady, unhurried. "I know your patient's biology is doing something extraordinary. I know you've been protecting him, at real cost to yourself — the department flag, the compliance call, I know about all of it, and I want to be clear, none of that came from me trying to hurt you. It came from me trying to find him before someone with worse intentions does. Because Dr. Mendez, if I found this in three weeks of legitimate research channels, I am not the only interested party who's going to find it eventually. I would much rather this go through a door I can control the shape of than through one I can't."

"That's not a request. That's a threat wearing a nice coat."

"It's both, and I'm not going to insult you by pretending it isn't." "I'm not asking you to hand him over. I'm asking for a conversation — with you, eventually with him, on his terms, with real protections on the table, real consent, real oversight. I would rather build something slow and careful with his cooperation than fast and ugly without it. But I need you to understand that slow and careful is a choice I can make today and might not be able to make in six months, once this stops being mine to control."

Mina thought about Warren's face on his own doorway step, saying you don't get to be here, and thought about how thoroughly she'd already proven she didn't have a perfect right to make choices for people who hadn't consented to them.

"I'm not going to tell you anything today," she said.

"I didn't expect you to." He stood, buttoned his coat, left a card on the table she didn't pick up until he was already through the door. "Fifteen minutes, whenever you're ready. That offer doesn't expire, Dr. Mendez. My patience with the alternative does."

She drove back to Corbin's Landing that same night and told Warren everything, and watched him listen to the whole thing without interrupting once, which she'd learned by now was the version of him that scared her most.

"He's not wrong," Warren said finally, quietly. "About the door I can't control. That's the part that's going to be hard to argue with."

"So what do we do."

"I don't know yet. For the first time in longer than you'd believe, I actually don't know yet."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Sixteen: Kitchen Table

(WORKING DRAFT — new for this novelist pass. Placed between Chapters 18 and 19. Perla POV. Not final prose.)
18b_kitchen_table.md

A few days later.

Perla had a standing policy, refined over decades of widowhood, that a man did not get fed at her kitchen table until she had decided what kind of man he was, and the deciding was best done while he was eating, because people lied with their mouths right up until you put food in them.

She was violating her own policy in advance — the pancit was already on, garlic first the way it had to be, the noodles waiting their turn in the colander — because Mina had called that morning and said, in the voice she used for things she had already decided, "I'm bringing someone for dinner," and underneath the decidedness Perla had heard the particular nervousness of a daughter hoping her mother would be kind.

Kind, Perla could do. Kind was easy. It was the deciding that required dinner.

"He drove all the way out here for pancit," Mina said, coming through the door with a man behind her, "which I told him was insane, and he said —"

"That I'd driven farther for worse reasons," the man finished, and smiled at Perla, and held out his hand. "Warren Locke. Thank you for having me. It smells like somebody in this house knows what they're doing."

Perla took his hand, shook it once, and did the inventory she had perfected over three decades of parent-teacher conferences: late forties to early sixties, she guessed, and then immediately distrusted the guess, because there was something about his face that refused to settle — not young, not old, the way a road can look new in one light and ancient in another. Dark hair, no gray she could see. Dark eyes that met hers without the usual half-second of adjustment people needed around her now, the recalibration to poor Perla, which she had come to hate more than the forgetting itself.

"Somebody does," she said. "Sit. You're in Mina's chair, she'll survive."

The kitchen was small the way Perla liked kitchens — everything within reach, everything with a history. The crucifix over the stove. The newspaper folded into smaller and smaller rectangles on the table, because her hands needed something to do while her brain worked. Warren sat where he was told, looked at the folded newspaper, and said, "You grade papers."

"I did. Substitute, thirty years. Other people's classrooms, other people's children, all of the attitude and none of the report cards." She set a plate in front of him with more force than strictly necessary. "Eat. Then I interrogate you. That's the order of operations in this house."

"Yes, ma'am."

He ate like a man who meant it, which Perla counted heavily in his favor, and when she said grace — Bless us, O Lord, and these Thy gifts — he bowed his head and said "Amen" at the end the way Perla's own father had said it: not like punctuation. Like an answer.

She filed that away, the way she filed everything now, in the part of her that still worked fine, thank you.

"So," she said. "What do you do, Warren?"

"I help out at a hardware store."

"That's what you do, or that's what you tell people?"

"Both, most days." He smiled, easy. "The store's in Corbin's Landing. I fix things. I'm good at fixing things."

"And how do you know my daughter?"

"She asks me questions." He glanced at Mina. "I give her answers she doesn't like."

Mina made a sound into her pancit.

"That's not an answer either," Perla said.

"No," Warren agreed cheerfully. "I'm bad at those."

Perla pointed her fork at him. "How old are you?"

A beat. Mina went very still — the particular stillness, Perla thought with a mother's whole tired expertise, of a daughter who knows the answer and has been told not to say it.

"Old enough to be sitting at this table," Warren said.

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only one I brought."

Perla looked at him for a long moment, then at Mina, then back at Warren, and let it go the way she let things go now — deliberately, visibly, so the other person understood it was a choice and not a lapse. "Are you sleeping with my daughter?"

"Ma."

"No, ma'am," Warren said, without missing a beat.

"Pity." Perla twirled noodles onto her fork. "She could use —"

"Do not finish that sentence."

They ate. The interrogation continued in the comfortable rhythm Perla had used on boyfriends, contractors, and one memorable priest: what did his parents do, did he have siblings, did he go to Mass — no, well, nobody was perfect — and through all of it Warren answered the small questions truly and the big ones with jokes, and Perla found, to her mild annoyance, that she was enjoying herself. He had the thing — the rare thing — of actually listening when she talked, not the nodding kind, the kind where his eyes stayed on her face like there was nowhere else he'd rather look.

It was Mina who brought up the teaching. "Tell him about Mrs. Gable's class," she said, and Perla laughed, because of course Mina would aim her at her best story, the way you aim a good dog at a burglar.

"Oh, Mrs. Gable's class." Perla set her fork down, warming to it. "Thirty-two eighth graders, one me, and a boy named — named — " She reached for it. It was there. It had been there a second ago, she could feel the shape of it, the D, the — "a boy named — "

The stall. The hateful, ordinary stall, the word sitting just behind her eyes like a thing seen through frosted glass.

She felt Mina start to move — the rescue, the word slid across like the exact tool she'd been patting the table for, five years of practice at it now — and then Warren's hand covered hers on the table.

Just that. His hand over hers, warm, heavy, utterly still.

He didn't supply the word. He didn't look away, either, didn't do the bright terrible thing people did where they pretended nothing was happening. He just waited, his hand on hers, like he had all the time in the world and none of it was more important than this.

Perla breathed. "Daniel," she said. "His name was Daniel. He set the pencil sharpener on fire."

"On purpose?" Warren asked, and his hand stayed where it was another second, and then lifted, easy, no ceremony.

"On purpose, by accident, the distinction was lost on Daniel." The story came out whole after that, the way stories did once the door unstuck — Daniel and the pencil sharpener and the fire drill and Mrs. Gable's face — and Perla told it well, she knew she told it well, she could feel the timing land the way it used to land in classrooms, and when she finished Warren laughed the real laugh, the surprised one, and said, "You must have been a terror to be taught by," and Perla said, "I was the best thing that ever happened to those children and they knew it," and it was true, and for one whole minute she didn't feel like a woman with a disease at all.

She felt like herself.

Later, she would try to name what had happened in the moment his hand covered hers, and fail, the way you fail at naming most of the true things. His hand had been warm — that was the plain fact of it, warm the way living things are warm — but it was the stillness she kept coming back to. Not the stillness of waiting. The stillness of arriving. Like being held in mind by someone who had practice at it. Like — and this was the thought she didn't say out loud, even to herself, until much later — like being prayed for by somebody who knew how.

After dinner he did the dishes. Perla protested exactly once, for form, and then sat at the table with her tea and watched him, because there was something restful about watching a man do a task properly — sleeves pushed up, forearms working, no wasted motion — and because Mina had gone very quiet in the way that meant she was feeling things she didn't have names for yet, and Perla wanted to give her a minute.

"You come back," Perla said, when he was drying the last plate. "I'm making adobo next time. It's better than the pancit and I will fight anyone who says otherwise."

"I'd like that."

At the door — Mina already at the car, pretending not to watch — Perla took his hand. Deliberately, the way he'd taken hers. Held it a second.

"Warren."

"Perla."

"Whatever it is you're carrying" — she held up her other hand before he could deflect, and something in her face must have told him she meant it, because the easy smile went quiet — "you don't have to set it down here. But you're welcome at my table while you figure out where to put it."

He looked at her for a long moment. "Thank you," he said, and it came out simpler than anything else he'd said all evening. "For dinner. For — all of it."

"Don't get sentimental. It's just food." She squeezed his hand once, let go. "And Warren — thank you for not being careful with me."

He tilted his head.

"Everyone's careful with me now." She said it plainly, the way she said everything that cost her something. "My daughter counts my good days like rosary beads. The neighbors talk to me like I'm already gone. You" — she gestured at the kitchen behind her, the dishes, the evening — "you just had dinner with me. That's rarer than you'd think."

He nodded, slowly, like she had said something worth the weight of it.

"Drive safe," she said. "Deer are suicidal this time of year."

She watched the car pull away from the doorway, Mina's hand out the passenger window waving, and stood there until the taillights were gone. Then she went back inside, to her small kitchen that smelled like garlic and somebody knowing what they were doing, and sat at her table, and folded the newspaper into smaller and smaller rectangles, and thought about a man with no age and all of them, who had held her hand like it was the most ordinary thing in the world.

I like him, she thought, in the voice she used for verdicts. God help us all, I like him.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Seventeen

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 010, revised per Handoff 011. Centerpiece scene. Not final prose.)
19_perla_healing_conflict.md

"Okay, this one's not a joke," Mina said, and he could tell from her voice before she finished the sentence that it wasn't. "You can heal people. You healed people back then. You're — whatever you are, you're not nothing, medically, biologically, you're not nothing." She stopped, made herself say the rest of it. "Can you heal my mother?"

He didn't answer right away, and the not-answering was, itself, the whole answer, and Mina felt her chest go very cold and very still, the way it had the night she'd read a consult request at 6:40 in the morning and felt a word arrive before she'd earned the right to know it.

"Yes," Warren said again, when the silence had gone on long enough that he understood she needed him to say it twice before either of them could move past it. "I think I could. But I need you to actually hear what that means, because it isn't what you're picturing. It's not me repairing tissue the way a surgeon repairs a joint. Your mother has a Soul joined to her, the same as almost everyone does, and hers is badly fractured — has been for longer than her diagnosis, probably, the disease and the fracture feeding each other in ways I don't fully understand. What I could attempt is Harmony. Real, sustained Harmony, between myself and that Soul, deep enough to help it hold together long enough to reach her. That's not a small thing. That's not an afternoon."

"What does that actually mean. Practically."

"Days, most likely. Both of us unconscious the entire time — her and me — unresponsive, from the outside indistinguishable from something going badly wrong. And it would, for a while, before it got better, if it got better at all. I'd expect her to look worse partway through, not better. Frightening, medically alarming, the kind of thing that would send anyone who didn't know what they were looking at straight to an emergency room. That's not a risk I can rule out. It's closer to a certainty I'd have to warn her about, in detail, before she could actually consent to any of it."

"Then why haven't you offered."

"Because even setting aside how much I don't know about the ordeal itself, I don't know which version of your mother comes out the other side of it." He kept his eyes on her while he said it. No joke, no softening phrase to give either of them somewhere else to look. "Say the Harmony holds, say her Soul comes back together enough to give her back everything the disease has taken. Is that a gift, or is that erasing five years of a person who kept living, kept being herself, kept finding things to be stubborn and funny and afraid about, even while she was losing pieces? I don't actually know the answer to that, Mina — I want to be honest about that too, not just about the danger of the days themselves. This isn't something I've watched happen and learned the shape of. It's something I'm afraid of, and I've had over two thousand years of practice mistaking my own fear for wisdom, so I'd rather hand you the fear plainly than dress it up as something I've proven."

"That's a very convenient thing for the person with the power to just not use it to believe."

He didn't flinch from that, which she respected more than she wanted to. "You're allowed to think that. I'd think it too, in your position. And I don't have evidence that I'm right about any of it — the identity question, the risk of the days themselves, what her Soul coming back together would actually feel like from her side. It's a real set of questions, not a proven cost. It might all turn out to be nothing. I don't get to decide that from here, and neither do you — that's hers to weigh, not ours to settle by arguing about it on my floor."

"Have you asked her?"

The question landed exactly the way she'd meant it to. "No," Warren said. "I haven't asked her anything. Which is, now that you've said it out loud, a genuinely embarrassing thing for a man over two thousand years old to have overlooked. I've been having this entire conversation with you, about her, as if she weren't a person with an opinion worth hearing."

"She is."

"She is." He leaned back against the wall. "I've healed people before, Mina. It wasn't clean then either — I asked, when I could, what people actually wanted, because even then I understood that a body handed back without permission isn't a gift, it's a theft with good intentions. I don't know why I let myself skip that part with your mother. I think — " He stopped, started again. "I think some part of me has been so afraid of what it would mean to actually offer this, to actually put the option on a table where all three of us have to look at it, that skipping the asking felt safer than doing it right."

"What would it mean. If you offered it."

"It would mean I'm not just someone telling you stories on a floor anymore. It would mean this stops being abstract, for all of us, including me. There's something else. I don't know everything an intervention like that would cost, for her, or eventually, if it comes to it, for you. There are questions on the other side of this I don't have full answers to yet either. I'm not withholding those on purpose. I don't know all of it."

Mina sat with that for a long time, the two of them quiet in the particular way that had become its own kind of language between them, and when she finally spoke, her voice had none of the fight left in it, just something rawer underneath.

"I don't know what I want you to say right now."

"I know. Neither do I, and I've had considerably longer to think about it than you have tonight." He reached over, didn't quite take her hand, just set his own down close enough that she could close the distance if she wanted to, and waited to see if she would. "But I think whatever happens next, it doesn't get decided by the two of us on my floor at eleven at night. She gets to be in the room for this. Whatever this turns out to be."

"She's going to want to know why you didn't ask sooner."

"She's going to be right to want that." He almost smiled, but didn't quite manage it. "I can't un-decide the things I've already decided on her behalf without noticing I was doing it. But I can stop deciding. From here on, she gets asked — about all of it. That's the part that's still mine to get right."

← Prev Contents Next →
Chapter Working draft

Chapter Eighteen

(WORKING DRAFT — Yeshua identity reveal. Synchronized with approved public-reader Chapter Twenty.)
20_the_reveal.md

A few nights later.

Mina had stopped taking notes.

She hadn't decided to stop. At some point the pen had simply ceased moving.

Warren was talking about Miriam.

He had been careful with names from the beginning, correcting translations when they annoyed him, refusing others altogether. Mina had learned not to interrupt every time he did it or they would never get through a story.

Apparently over two thousand years had given him opinions about transliteration.

Strong ones.

“She knew before I did,” Warren said.

They were sitting on the floor again. Mina in her usual place against the bed frame, Warren across from her with his back against the wall. Two mugs sat between them.

“Knew what?”

“That Jerusalem was going to go badly.”

Mina waited.

Warren rubbed his thumb along a chip in his mug.

“She didn't know what would happen. Neither did I. That's important.”

“You've said that.”

“Yes.”

“Several times.”

“People became very invested in the idea that I knew.”

Something about the sentence bothered her.

It wasn't the first time.

There had been dozens of them.

Little grammatical peculiarities she had learned to accept because Warren spoke about history as though it were a place he'd misplaced his keys.

They didn't call it that yet.

That road wasn't there.

No, that's later.

Pilate wasn't particularly interested in theology.

And sometimes:

I don't know. I wasn't there.

That last one had done more to convince her than almost anything else.

Frauds knew everything.

Warren had infuriatingly specific holes in his knowledge.

Mina picked up her coffee.

“So Miriam thought you shouldn't go.”

“No.”

“She thought you should?”

“No.”

“Those are traditionally the choices.”

“She thought it was my decision.”

Mina lowered the mug.

“And hers?”

“To come with me.”

There was something in his voice.

Not the ancient thing.

The human thing.

Mina had begun recognizing the difference.

“What did you tell her?”

“No.”

She smiled faintly. “Very eloquent.”

“I was frightened.”

“Of Jerusalem?”

“Of her coming with me.”

That made Mina stop smiling.

Warren looked down at the mug.

“I thought I could bear what happened to me.”

The room became quiet.

“I wasn't certain I could bear what happened to her.”

Mina watched him.

“What did she say?”

Warren's mouth twitched.

“That I didn't get to make her fear into my authority.”

Mina laughed once.

“I like her.”

“Yes.”

“You say that like it's a problem.”

“It frequently was.”

“She came anyway.”

“Yes.”

“And you went to Jerusalem.”

“Yes.”

“For Passover.”

Warren looked at her.

“Yes.”

Something moved at the edge of Mina's thoughts.

She ignored it.

There had been a great deal of Jerusalem in human history. A great deal of Passover. One did not hear two nouns and leap directly into—

She picked up her pen.

“When?”

Warren didn't answer.

Mina looked up.

“When, Warren?”

“You know approximately when.”

“I know the period.”

“Yes.”

“First century.”

“Yes.”

“Under Rome.”

“Yes.”

He was being irritating again.

She had once thought it was accidental.

“What happened when you got there?”

Warren's eyes went somewhere else.

Not unfocused.

Far away.

“There were too many people.”

Mina waited.

“Jerusalem did that during the festival. People came from everywhere. Families doubled up. Courtyards became bedrooms. Prices went up. Animals everywhere. Everyone complained about everyone else.”

“That sounds sacred.”

“Almost nothing feels sacred while you're standing in animal shit.”

Mina snorted.

Warren smiled.

Then the smile was gone.

“The authorities were nervous.”

“Roman?”

“Roman. Temple. Different concerns. Same crowd.”

“And you were causing trouble.”

“I was teaching.”

“That's not a denial.”

“No.”

Mina wrote teaching.

“What were you teaching?”

Warren looked at the word she'd written.

“The kingdom of God, mostly.”

Her pen stopped.

There it was again.

That movement at the edge of her mind.

She looked at him.

Warren's expression hadn't changed.

Mina slowly put the pen down.

“What did you just say?”

“You heard me.”

“Yes.”

Her heartbeat had changed.

She didn't know why yet.

Or she did and was refusing to know.

“The kingdom of God.”

“Yes.”

“You went around teaching about the kingdom of God.”

“Among other things.”

“In first-century Judea.”

“And Galilee.”

Mina stared at him.

He had corrected her.

Of course he had corrected her.

Something cold moved down her arms.

“No.”

Warren said nothing.

She stood.

She didn't know why standing seemed necessary, except that suddenly sitting on Warren's floor felt impossible.

“No.”

“Mina.”

“Don't.”

He stopped.

She walked toward the kitchen.

Three steps.

Turned around.

Warren remained on the floor.

Not moving.

Not helping her.

Good.

She didn't want help.

Her mind had already begun doing what it did best.

Pattern recognition.

Evidence.

Contradictions.

Probability.

She thought of Perla.

The way he'd gone still, that night on his floor, when she'd asked if he could heal her mother.

The things he hadn't said, which had been louder than the things he had.

She thought of the things he had said about healing.

About consciousness.

About matter.

About death.

She thought of Miriam.

Jerusalem.

Passover.

Rome.

The kingdom of God.

Her mouth went dry.

“No.”

Warren looked almost sympathetic.

“Stop looking at me like that.”

“Like what?”

“Like I'm the last person in the room to figure something out.”

That produced the smallest movement at the corner of his mouth.

“Warren.”

“Yes?”

“What happened in Jerusalem?”

He didn't answer.

She already knew.

“What happened?”

“Mina.”

“Say it.”

“I was arrested.”

The room seemed to tilt.

She stared at him.

“By Rome?”

“Eventually.”

“What did they do to you?”

Warren was silent.

Mina heard her own breathing.

She didn't need the answer.

She knew the answer.

She suddenly knew why he had been so careful whenever death came up.

Why he spoke about religious history with irritation rather than reverence.

Why his knowledge became strangely personal in exactly one portion of history.

Why he could describe some things with absurd precision and others not at all.

Why there were events he knew because someone had told him afterward.

Why Miriam mattered.

Why Jerusalem mattered.

Why he had once said humanity had turned an ability into omnipotence.

Why he'd corrected her when she'd used the word God.

Why—

“Oh my God.”

Warren closed his eyes.

Mina pointed at him.

“No.”

His eyes opened.

“You are not allowed to react to that sentence.”

“I didn't say anything.”

“You reacted.”

“I closed my eyes.”

“Exactly.”

She was breathing too fast now.

“Warren.”

He waited.

“Were you crucified?”

The silence answered first.

“Yes.”

Mina's legs became unreliable.

She sat down in the one chair rather than risk discovering whether knees could resign without notice.

“No.”

Warren said nothing.

“No, no, no.”

She pressed both hands against her face.

The conclusion was absurd.

The conclusion was impossible.

The conclusion explained far too much.

She dropped her hands.

“You said you didn't know if they could kill you.”

“I didn't.”

“You said you went to Jerusalem knowing you might die.”

“Yes.”

“You said Miriam went with you.”

“Yes.”

“You were teaching about the kingdom of God.”

“Yes.”

“First-century Galilee.”

“Yes.”

“Judea.”

“Yes.”

“Passover.”

“Yes.”

“Rome crucified you.”

“Yes.”

Mina stared at him.

Warren stared back.

She could not make herself say it.

Not because she didn't believe it.

Because saying it would make the world before the sentence different from the world after it.

Warren understood.

She hated that he understood.

Her voice came out very small.

“Jesus?”

His face changed.

It wasn't anger.

Pain, perhaps.

And something close to embarrassment.

“Yeshua.”

Mina stared at him.

Of all the possible responses—

“That is what you're correcting?”

He looked genuinely uncomfortable.

“At the moment, it seemed safer.”

A laugh escaped her.

It was the wrong laugh.

Too loud. Almost hysterical.

She stood again.

“You're Jesus.”

“Yeshua.”

“Stop correcting me!”

“Right.”

“You're—”

She couldn't finish.

Warren rose slowly.

Mina immediately pointed at the floor.

“Don't.”

He sat back down.

That almost broke her.

Jesus Christ had just sat down because she'd pointed at the floor.

She covered her mouth.

Warren waited.

She started laughing.

She couldn't stop.

It wasn't funny.

It was the funniest thing that had ever happened.

“Oh my God.”

Warren winced.

That made it worse.

“Stop doing that!”

“Doing what?”

“Reacting every time I say God!”

“It's complicated.”

“You think?”

She paced.

Then stopped.

Turned.

“Did you know this whole time that eventually I was going to figure it out?”

“I suspected.”

“You suspected.”

“Yes.”

“You've been sitting here telling me stories about yourself and waiting for me to realize I'm talking to Jesus Christ.”

Warren frowned.

“The Christ part is also—”

“Finish that sentence and I will hit you.”

He considered this.

“Understood.”

Mina stared at him.

The laughter was leaving now.

What replaced it frightened her.

She looked at his hands.

Ordinary hands.

She had watched them wash dishes in her mother's kitchen.

Carry groceries.

Make coffee.

She had watched this man argue with Denny about a stripped screw.

She had seen him irritated by parking.

She had watched him eat leftover pancit directly from the refrigerator.

Jesus.

Her eyes filled before she could stop them.

Warren saw.

His expression changed immediately.

“No.”

“What?”

“Don't.”

“Don't what?”

“Do that.”

Mina wiped angrily at one eye.

“I'm having a moment, Warren.”

“I know.”

“I'm entitled.”

“Yes.”

“You're Jesus.”

“Yeshua.”

She glared at him.

“Sorry.”

Then she understood what he meant.

Not the name.

The tears.

The distance suddenly opening between them.

The enormous cultural weight trying to enter the room.

Saint.

Messiah.

Son of God.

Christ.

Lord.

Two thousand years of paintings and churches and wars and prayers and crosses and hymns and people falling to their knees.

Warren saw it happening.

And he was afraid.

Not of what she now knew.

Of what knowing might make her do.

Mina slowly lowered her hand.

“You don't want me to worship you.”

“No.”

The answer was immediate.

Almost frightened.

“No.”

Something in her settled.

She looked at him again.

Not at Jesus.

At Warren.

The annoying man on the floor.

The man who'd sat at her mother's table and let himself be interrogated over pancit.

“Okay.”

Warren studied her.

“Okay?”

“I don't know what anything means yet.”

“That makes two of us.”

“You've had over two thousand years.”

“It hasn't helped as much as you'd think.”

Mina sat back down in her place against the bed frame.

Not beside him.

Not farther away either.

Her notebook remained where she'd left it.

She picked up the pen.

Her hand shook.

She put it back down.

“One question.”

Warren waited.

“Did you come back?”

Everything in his face became still.

Mina's heart pounded.

“You know what I mean.”

“Yes.”

“No metaphor.”

“No.”

“No disciples having a spiritual experience and then the story getting bigger.”

“No.”

“No body stolen.”

“No.”

She swallowed.

“You died.”

“Yes.”

“And then?”

Warren looked at her.

“I woke up.”

Mina forgot to breathe.

He said it without grandeur.

Without thunder.

Without heaven opening.

The same way he might have said he'd awakened after an illness.

“I was badly injured,” he continued. “Healing took time.”

“How much?”

“A few days.”

Mina stared at him.

There it was.

The last piece.

Not proof.

Something worse.

Recognition.

History had remembered it.

Badly, incompletely, impossibly—

but it had remembered.

Mina leaned her head back against the bed frame.

“Jesus Christ.”

Across from her, Warren inhaled.

She raised one finger without opening her eyes.

“Don't.”

He said nothing.

After several seconds she heard him pick up his coffee.

Mina opened her eyes.

“You're drinking coffee.”

“Yes.”

“You're Jesus and you're sitting on the floor drinking coffee.”

“Would standing improve matters?”

She stared at him.

“No.”

“Good. My knees aren't what people assume.”

She laughed despite herself.

And there he was again.

Warren.

Still Warren.

Which, Mina realized, might be the only reason she could survive knowing who else he had been.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Nineteen

(WORKING DRAFT — revised per Handoff 012 correction for legal plausibility. Not final prose.)
21_the_competency_letter.md

A few days later.

The letter wasn't from a law firm at all, it turned out — that was the first thing Denny's cousin Alice got wrong when she read it standing at the counter, and the first thing she corrected herself on once she'd actually gotten to the letterhead. "This is Adult Protective Services. County office." She read it again, slower. "Somebody filed a welfare concern. Says here — 'subject may lack stable residency or verifiable identity consistent with self-sufficient decision-making, raising concern for possible self-neglect or undue influence by a third party.'"

Alice did title work for a living, not law, but she'd spent fifteen years reading documents nobody else in town wanted to read closely, and Warren had learned to trust her instincts about paperwork the way he trusted Denny's about people.

"What does that actually mean," Mina asked.

"It means somebody called it in — could be anonymous, county doesn't have to say who — and now a caseworker's required to at least look into it. That's not a court order. That's not even close to one yet. It's a welfare check with a fancier name." Alice set the letter down. "But it's a door. If the caseworker doesn't like what they find — no fixed address history, no family anyone can locate, medical records that don't add up — they can recommend the county pursue a guardianship evaluation. That's the part that actually has teeth. That's a judge, a court-appointed evaluator, a hearing. And Halcyon didn't have to file a single thing themselves to start any of it. All it takes is one phone call from someone who sounds appropriately concerned."

"Can they trace it back to Voss?"

"Probably not directly, no. That's rather the point of doing it this way." Alice looked at Warren with the frank, unhurried curiosity of a woman who'd decided there was a real story here and that it wasn't currently her business to ask for it. "I'll tell you what actually worries me, though, and it isn't the guardianship part. Most of these get closed out once a caseworker does one visit and decides you're not, in fact, a confused old man living in a van. What worries me is that any of this happening at all puts your name and your situation into a record. A county file. Something with your name on it that other people — reporters, other companies, anyone who comes looking after Halcyon does — can eventually find and ask questions about. The danger was never really the guardianship. It's what gets written down along the way."

Warren was quiet for a long moment. "He's not trying to win a court case."

"No," Denny said, from the doorway, arms crossed the way they always were when he'd been listening longer than he'd let on. "He's trying to make you exist on paper whether you want to or not. Once you're in a county system somewhere, you stop being a rumor he can't quite pin down."

"So what do we do," Mina said.

"We cooperate," Alice said, before Warren could answer. "Fully, politely, boringly. You let the caseworker do one visit, you have an address, a neighbor who vouches for you, a doctor's note if you can get one — the goal isn't to win dramatically. It's to be the single most unremarkable case that caseworker closes all month. Give them nothing worth writing down twice."

Warren looked at the letter a while longer, then folded it the way he folded most things that weighed more than their size suggested.

"I can do unremarkable," he said. "I've had considerable practice."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Batch 2. Mina tells Perla. Not final prose.)
21b_mia_tells_perla.md

Thursday evening.

Mina sat in her mother's kitchen and tried to work out how to tell a devout Catholic woman that she'd been having long evening conversations with Jesus Christ, and kept arriving at the same problem, which was that there was no version of the sentence that didn't sound like either a psychotic break or a very bad joke.

Fifty-one years old, a clinical geneticist, a scientist, and she was sitting at Perla's table feeling twelve.

Perla was at the stove. Pancit — the smell had been the background of every hard conversation in Mina's life, her mother cooking through anything that couldn't be said on an empty stomach. The crucifix over the stove. The newspaper folded into smaller and smaller rectangles on the table, because Perla's hands needed something to do while her brain worked. Mina had the sudden, vertiginous feeling that the kitchen itself was about to become a memory she would never be able to come back to unchanged, and she hadn't even opened her mouth yet.

"You're hovering," Perla said, without turning around. "Hovering is for flies and bill collectors. Sit."

Mina sat.

"Eat first, or talk first?"

"Talk first," Mina said. "I don't think I'll be able to eat after, and I don't want you thinking I didn't want your food."

Perla turned off the burner. That, more than anything, told Mina her mother had been bracing for this — Perla never turned off a burner mid-cooking for anything short of a fire or a confession.

"What happened."

"Someone filed a welfare report on Warren. Adult Protective Services. It's — it's not a court thing, it's a check, but it opens a file. A county file, with his name in it." Mina looked at her own hands. "People are going to start writing things down about him, Mama. Strangers, forms, caseworkers. And I needed you to hear the rest from me first. Before it comes from anywhere else."

Perla sat down across from her. Folded her hands. Waited the way she'd waited through thirty years of parent-teacher conferences for someone to finally say the thing they'd been circling.

"The rest of what."

Mina had rehearsed this in the car. It had sounded almost reasonable in the car, the way things do when there's an engine running underneath them.

"He's old, Mama."

"Men his age—"

"Not fifty old." Mina stopped. Started again, because there was no good way and the bad ways were all she had. "His chart doesn't make sense. I've told you that part. Bone age fifty-two, blood that flagged everything my lab owned, a femur that knit in seventeen hours. I ran it three times because I thought the machines were broken." She looked up. "The machines weren't broken. He's more than two thousand years old. Physically alive. The whole time."

Perla didn't move. The newspaper rectangle sat perfectly still under her hands.

"I know how that sounds."

"Do you." Not a question.

"I checked everything I could check. I did the math every way I know how to do it. It doesn't — there's no version where it's a mistake, Mama. There's only versions where it's impossible."

"And the rest."

Mina's throat closed. She made herself say it the way she'd say a diagnosis — straight, no softening, because Perla had taught her that softening was its own kind of cruelty.

"His name was Yeshua."

The kitchen was very quiet. Somewhere down the street a dog barked, once, and stopped, like it had thought better of it.

"You're telling me," Perla said slowly, "that the man who came to dinner. Who washed my dishes. Who might — who you're asking me to consider letting — " She stopped. "You're telling me he's Jesus."

"I'm telling you he's the man the stories are about. Yes."

Perla stood up. Walked to the stove. Put her hand flat on the counter next to the crucifix — not touching it, just near it, the way you'd stand near a wall in an earthquake — and stood there with her back to Mina for a long time.

"Mija." Her voice was different. Smaller, and then not smaller, because Perla didn't do small for long. "Are you safe with him."

It wasn't the question Mina had braced for. It broke something open in her chest anyway.

"Yes."

Perla studied her. The inventory look — the one that had assessed thirty years of children in doorways, and then one daughter, for much longer.

"This man matters to you," Perla said. "More than the science."

"Mama—"

"I'm seventy years old and I'm observing my daughter. Answer the observation."

Mina looked at the table. "I don't know what it is yet. I haven't — I haven't sorted it out. He's been very careful with me. More careful than anyone's ever been. That's part of what's scaring me."

Perla nodded once, as if Mina had confirmed something rather than evaded it, and didn't press further.

Perla turned around. Her eyes were wet, and her jaw was set, both at once, the way Mina had seen exactly once before — at Ernesto's funeral, when the priest had said something well-meaning and wrong about God's plan and Perla had smiled at him like a knife.

"Seventy years," Perla said. "Seventy years I've gone to Mass. Your father proposed to me at his grandmother's grave and I married him in a church and I buried him from a church and every Sunday since I was a girl I have sat in a pew and said the words. And now my daughter sits in my kitchen and tells me the man himself washes my dishes." She laughed, once, a sound with no humor in it at all. "Do you have any idea what you're asking me to hold in my head at the same time."

"Yes," Mina said quietly. "That's why I'm terrified."

"Did he say he's God."

"He says he's not. Not the way we were taught to mean it." Mina hesitated. "He's not claiming to be the Creator. Whatever that word means to you, he's not putting himself in that place. He's old and he's strange and he can do things I can't explain, and the bones say what the bones say. That's the part I actually know, Mama. The rest — he says the people who used that word about him weren't fools. He just doesn't think it's the right word." She looked at her mother. "He doesn't know, Mama. That's the part I keep coming back to. Two thousand years and he'll still say I don't know like it costs him something."

"And you believe him."

"I believe he's telling the truth as he understands it." Mina's voice steadied, the scientist finding footing. "The rest I'm still working on. But the bones, Mama. The blood. I held the films up to the light myself."

Perla came back to the table. Sat down. Picked up the newspaper rectangle and folded it once more, smaller, though there was no smaller left to fold it into.

"This is the man," she said, "who would put himself to sleep for three days next to me, while I get worse, in order to try to fix what's happening in my head."

"Yes."

"And he told you he might not be able to."

"He told me he doesn't know. He told me the deterioration would be real and frightening and that I'd have to watch it."

Perla nodded slowly. "Good. I'd rather be frightened by the truth than comforted by a lie. I've had enough of people being gentle with me." She looked at Mina — really looked, the inventory look, the one that had assessed thirty years of children in doorways. "You're carrying this alone. That's why you came. Not the county file. You're drowning in it and you came to your mother."

"I didn't know who else to tell."

"Well." Perla stood, turned the burner back on. The pancit hissed. "You're telling the right person, even if I'm the wrong audience for half of it." She moved around the kitchen with the competence of a woman whose body still knew every inch of it, and Mina watched her mother's hands — steady tonight, thank God, steady — and thought about fractured things, and harmony, and all the words Warren had given her that she hadn't said out loud in this room.

"Mama."

"Mm."

"I don't know what this does to — to Mass. To everything. I don't have an answer for that."

"No," Perla agreed, stirring. "Neither do I. I'm going to need to sit with it." A pause. "Don't you dare tell me not to go Sunday. I'll decide that myself."

"I wasn't going to."

"You were thinking it."

Mina almost smiled. "I was thinking it."

They ate. The food was good — it was always good, her mother's food, the one uncomplicated mercy in the whole impossible evening. Before they started, Perla bowed her head and said grace, the way she always did, and Mina — who hadn't said grace in years, who'd filed it away with other things she'd outgrown — bowed her head too, and found that the words were still there, word for word, waiting like they'd never left.

After, Perla walked her to the door. Held her face in both hands the way she had when Mina was small.

"Whatever he is," Perla said, "he was kind to me at my table. I noticed. Whatever else I decide, I noticed that."

Mina drove home through the dark with the taste of pancit in her mouth and her mother's grace still sitting somewhere behind her ribs, and thought that the earthquake hadn't knocked the house down after all.

It had just rearranged all the furniture.

She'd find out in the morning what still fit where.

Question Session Working draft

Question Session: Why Didn't You Stop It (Mina and Warren)

(WORKING DRAFT — revised per Handoff 012 correction. A large theological question answered with real cosmological content, not a joke, and without letting Warren off too easily. Not final prose.)
question_why_didnt_you_stop_it.md

"Can I ask you something that isn't going to have a funny answer."

"Those are usually the ones you don't warn me about first. Go ahead."

Mina had been turning it over for days before she finally said it. "Why didn't you stop things. Not — I'm not asking about one specific horror, I'm asking in general. You've lived through all of it. Wars, plagues, all of it. You could have stepped in. Sometimes, it sounds like, you actually could have. Why didn't you."

Warren didn't answer right away, which told her he'd been asked some version of this before, by someone, at some point, and hadn't found an answer he trusted any more the second time around.

"Because the second I start deciding which wars end early and which diseases break a day sooner, I've made myself the permanent arbiter of every human choice, every regime, every accident, every consequence — and I don't have a clean rule for how to do that job. Nobody could. That's the honest starting point, not individuality being some kind of shield that protects everyone equally, including the people doing the harm. It doesn't work that cleanly and I'm not going to pretend it does."

"So it's not that stopping a massacre would violate the guy doing the massacring."

"No. I want to be precise about that, because I said it wrong to myself for a long time and I'm not going to say it wrong to you. Stopping someone from murdering another person doesn't erase the murderer's individuality any more than a lock on a door erases a burglar's. If anything it protects the person being harmed — protects their agency, their actual life, which is the thing I claim to care about in the first place. I don't have a good defense for every time I didn't do that. I've intervened, sometimes, in ways nobody wrote down. I've refused to, far more often, and told myself it was principle, and some of those times it probably was, and some of those times I think I was just afraid of what it would cost me to start and not know where to stop."

"That's not the same answer you gave me a minute ago."

"No. It's the truer one, and it took you catching the easy version to get me to say it." He leaned forward, elbows on his knees, looking at the floorboards instead of at her. "I don't have a theory that makes the suffering make sense. I don't have a rule that tells you which interventions were right and which refusals were cowardice wearing a principle's coat. I have two thousand years of specific choices, made one at a time, some of which I'd defend and some of which I still don't know how to carry, and I'm not going to hand you a clean answer just because you asked me directly. You deserve better than clean. You deserve the actual mess."

"And if you got it wrong more than you got it right?"

"Then I got it wrong more than I got it right, and there's no version of this conversation where I get to find out for certain, because the world that happened is the only one there is to check against." He looked at her, tired and honest in the way she'd come to trust more than any of his certainty. "That's not an answer that lets me off anything. I know that. I'd rather you know it too."

She didn't ask it as an accusation. She asked it the way she'd started asking most things lately — like a scientist who'd stopped being embarrassed about needing the actual data.

"What did you do instead? After. After you — stopped being public about it. After the tomb, after all of it. Did you just watch?"

Warren laughed, once, with no humor in it. "God, no. That would have been cleaner. I kept intervening. For centuries. That's the part nobody puts in any story, because by then there was nobody writing it down with any accuracy and I wasn't exactly advertising." He set his mug down. "I'd pop up somewhere and try to repair a consequence of something I'd done earlier. Or undo something I regretted. Or counter something another Eternal was doing to people who couldn't fight back. Or correct some interpretation of something I'd taught that had curdled into the opposite of what I'd meant. Or protect people from a mess I was fairly sure I'd helped create in the first place."

"Did it work."

"Sometimes. That was the problem, Mina. Sometimes it worked beautifully, and then the working had consequences nobody could have predicted, and I'd have to intervene again to manage the consequences of the intervention, and that would have consequences, and — " He made a small, tired gesture with one hand, like a man describing a staircase that only went down. "One more intelligent intervention. That was always the theory. Just one more, and this time I'd be smart enough to get it right. I was wrong in interesting new ways every single time."

"So you stopped."

"Eventually I recognized the shape of it. Intervention, consequence, correction, new consequence. It wasn't a strategy, it was a loop, and the loop didn't care whether the intentions were good. I don't want you to hear this as me being noble about it. I stopped because I failed at it for long enough, in enough different centuries, that continuing would have meant I wasn't learning anything. Withdrawing wasn't hiding. It was the first honest conclusion I'd reached in about a thousand years."

Mina was quiet. Then: "That's why Voss bothers you."

It wasn't a question, but Warren answered it like one anyway.

"Yes."

"Not just because he's dangerous. Because you recognize the reasoning."

"I understand enough," Warren said quietly. "The stakes are high enough. Therefore I have the right to decide for people who haven't consented." He looked at her. "That's his argument. And it's — it was mine, Mina. At a scale his doesn't touch. I lived inside that reasoning for longer than his species has had written language. I know exactly how it feels from the inside, how reasonable it sounds when you're the one who's right most of the time, how the correctness starts to feel like permission. That's why he's hard for me to look at straight. Not because he's alien. Because he's familiar."

"And you think he's wrong."

"I think knowing more than another consciousness does not give you ownership over it." The sentence came out flat, certain, like something he'd paid heavily to learn. "That's the whole of it. That's what you are actually means, once you've finished misunderstanding it, which took me — an embarrassingly long time. Being correct doesn't entitle you to force another consciousness into correctness. I understood that as a principle for eons before I understood it as a practice. Voss is still in the principle stage. I hope he gets to the practice stage faster than I did. I'd like to think the tuition has gone down."

"Has it?"

"I genuinely don't know," Warren said. "That's the part of this conversation I'm least comfortable with. I look at him and I see a man doing the arithmetic I did, and I want to tell you it's wrong, and what I actually know is that it's incomplete. Which is worse, because incomplete arithmetic is what I was doing too."
