Question Session Working draft

Question Session: Jack the Ripper (Mina and Warren)

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 011. Demonstrates non-omniscience without undercutting the extraordinary abilities elsewhere established. Not final prose.)
question_jack_the_ripper.md

"Okay, different kind of question. Do you know who Jack the Ripper was?"

"No idea."

"Wait, really? You lived through it."

"I lived through an enormous number of Tuesdays in an enormous number of cities, Mina, I was not standing in a specific alley in London keeping a personal list of everyone's worst nights." He said it without any defensiveness at all, which was itself the most convincing part. "People hear 'two thousand years' and assume it comes with a search function. It doesn't. I understand a great deal about how reality works — thought, matter, Harmony, all of it — the way you understand how a radio works. That's not the same as having heard every single broadcast that's ever played. I have no idea who that man was. I have opinions about several men who could have been him, based on nothing but the general texture of that decade, and none of my opinions are worth anything, and I'm not going to pretend otherwise just because the not-knowing makes me sound less impressive."

"That's oddly reassuring."

"Good. It should be. The alternative is a version of me that's been quietly watching every terrible thing that's ever happened to anyone in enough detail to identify the culprit, and I promise you, that's not a version of understanding you'd actually want me to have. Knowing how reality works isn't the same as having logged every broadcast — and most of what I don't know, I never knew in the first place. That's different from forgetting. I want to be careful about that distinction with you, always."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-One

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 012. Perla's direct conversation with Warren, on her schedule, per Assignment 012 items 3 and 6. Not final prose.)
22_perla_and_warren_direct.md

Perla called him herself, which surprised him enough that he nearly didn't answer a number he didn't recognize. "This is Perla," she said, without preamble, when he picked up. "Mina doesn't know I have your number yet. I got it off her phone while she was in the shower, which I want on the record as the single most productive parenting decision I've made in months. I'd like you to come to the community garden on Fifth Thursday morning, ten o'clock, and I'd like you to come alone."

"I can do that."

"I know you can. That wasn't a question."

She was already kneeling in the dirt when he arrived, gloves on, working a row of something green and stubborn with more focus than the task strictly required, and she didn't look up until he'd been standing there a full minute.

"Sit. Not on the bench, I hate the bench, sit on the ground like a person." She waited until he had, cross-legged in the dirt in a way that visibly amused her. "I've had a week to think about what I actually wanted to ask you, and I threw out most of my list, because most of it was really just me wanting reassurance dressed up as curiosity, and I decided I didn't want reassurance from you. I wanted the truth, even the parts that don't comfort me."

"All right."

"Were you God." She said it plainly, gardening glove still on one hand, pulling a weed as she said it, like the question and the weed were roughly the same size of task. "Not 'are you the man people called God.' I know the history now, Mina's told me most of it. I'm asking you directly. When it was happening — the healing, whatever happened at the end, all of it — were you God, the way I was taught to mean the word."

Warren considered that with real care, which she seemed to appreciate more than a quick answer would have.

"I understand exactly why people reached for that word," he said. "I want to say that first, because I think it matters more than my answer to your actual question. People watched a man do things that had no other available explanation in their entire framework for what a person could be — heal a hand that had been useless for eleven years, walk out of a tomb days after his execution, do things with matter that shouldn't have been possible for anything wearing skin. 'God' wasn't a foolish word for them to reach for. It was the only word big enough in the box they had. I don't think less of a single person who used it."

"But."

"But I don't think it's the right word for what I actually am, or was. I'm not the maker of everything, Perla. I didn't create the other Eternals, I didn't create the universe, I'm not outside all of it looking in. I'm a consciousness — old, strange, joined in perfect Harmony with a human life once, still carrying what that gave me — but a consciousness, not the source everything else comes from. I think 'God,' the way two thousand years of theology built the word, describes something bigger and stranger than I am. I did real things. Extraordinary things, genuinely, not tricks, not metaphors. I just don't think the word people picked to explain those things was actually the correct shape for what was doing them."

Perla sat back on her heels, weed forgotten in her hand. "That's either the most humble thing you've ever said or the most arrogant, and I genuinely cannot tell which."

"I've had the same trouble, some days."

She laughed at that, short and real, her shoulders coming down a fraction. "I'm not going to stop going to Mass. I want you to know that. Whatever you are, whatever this turns out to mean for my hand, my head, my last however-many years — I am not giving up seventy years of showing up on Sunday because a man in my community garden has complicated feelings about a word. Your story might be true and my faith might still be true, differently, the way two people can both be right about a mountain from two different sides of it."

"I'd never ask you to give it up. I'd be a fairly poor argument against faith, all things considered, given what I actually am."

"No," Perla agreed. "You'd be a fairly strange argument for it, is what you'd be, which is its own kind of complicated I intend to keep thinking about." She pulled off one glove, looked at her own hand — steady, for now, in the morning light — and then back at him. "I'll be calling on you again before this is decided. I'm not done with my list — I just needed to know the big one first before I could ask you the smaller ones honestly."

"I'll be here."

"I know you will," Perla said, and went back to her weeding, and didn't say anything else, which he understood, correctly, as the conversation being over for today and not one syllable sooner.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-Two

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 012, the priest thread. Not final prose.)
23_father_ray.md

Father Ray had buried Ernesto, baptized nobody in this particular family, Mina having outgrown all of it before he'd ever gotten the chance, and spent the last four years watching Perla's handwriting on the parish sign-up sheets get smaller and more careful without either of them ever discussing why, so when she asked to talk after the Tuesday morning Mass instead of just going home, he didn't act surprised, just put the kettle on in the little kitchen behind the sacristy and waited.

"Hypothetically," Perla said, which was how she started most of her actual questions, "if someone told you they could heal you. Genuinely. Not a pill, not a doctor, someone with a real, specific, extraordinary ability — would you let them?"

"Hypothetically." Father Ray poured two cups without asking if she wanted one, forty years of practice making that decision correctly more often than not. "Is this a person you trust?"

"More than I expected to, given how little sense any of it makes."

"Then my hypothetical answer is that healing has never been the hard part of any story I've ever actually cared about, Perla. The hard part is always what a person does with the years they get back, or the years they don't." He sat across from her, hands around his own cup, a bad knee stretched out to the side the way it always was by the second Mass of the day. "I'm going to disappoint you and not ask who this person is. Forty years of this collar, I've learned that people bring me hypotheticals because they need to hear themselves reason through something out loud more than they need my opinion of it. I'll listen. I won't pretend I have a ruling."

"That's very convenient for you."

"It's the only honest posture I've got left, most days." He smiled, tired and genuine. "What I will say — actually say, not hypothetically — is that God has never, in my reading of any of it, been in the business of only loving the healed version of a person. Whatever you decide, whoever this is, you are not owed the Perla from ten years ago just because she'd be easier for the people around you to grieve less. You get to decide what you want your own remaining years to look like. That's not heresy, whatever the catechism-quoting types down at the diocesan office would tell you. That's just the actual shape of a soul that gets to choose."

Perla turned her cup in her hands, quiet for a while. "And if I said yes? If I let this happen?"

"Then I'd tell you the same thing I told the Alvarez boy the week he decided to leave seminary, and the same thing I told your husband the year before he died, when he finally admitted he was frightened: that God's been fairly clear, historically, about preferring people who choose things honestly over people who suffer them nobly for an audience. If healing is honestly what you want, Perla, want it without apologizing to me or anyone else for wanting it." He reached over, patted her hand once, briefly, the way he'd done at Ernesto's funeral and a hundred ordinary Sundays since. "And if you decide against it, for your own honest reasons, I'll tell you the exact same thing. Either way. You're not on trial here. You never have been."

Perla didn't tell him. Not that day, not the specifics, not the name — but she left lighter than she'd walked in, which was, she thought, driving home past the church sign advertising a fish fry nobody her age still had the stomach for, probably the whole actual point of having a priest at all.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-Three

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 012 addendum. Perla's consent, locked per Creator override. Not final prose.)
24_perla_says_yes.md

Thursday.

Perla called the meeting herself, the same way she'd called the first one — her number, her kitchen, her terms — and this time she didn't cook.

"I've made a decision," she said, before either of them had finished sitting down. "And I want to say the conditions first, because I've had two weeks to think about the conditions and I'm not interested in negotiating them."

"You told me what the three days look like," she went on. "Say it once more, in front of her, so we're all hearing the same thing."

Warren didn't argue with the order of operations. "Three days, unconscious — both of us. You'll get worse before you get better, and the worse will look frightening; I won't dress that up. There are things about your Soul I don't fully understand, and I won't pretend otherwise. That's the shape of it."

Perla held his gaze a moment, then nodded once. "Good."

"Go ahead," Warren said.

"One: Mina is in the room. The whole time, however long it takes, she does not leave for anything short of a fire. Two: you tell her exactly what to watch for, in writing, so she isn't guessing during the worst of it. Three—" Perla's voice caught, just slightly, the first crack in a performance she'd clearly rehearsed. "Three, if it doesn't work. If I come out the other side worse, or not at all, or not myself — I want it on the record, right now, in front of both of you, that this was my choice. Nobody's tragedy to apologize for. Mine."

"Understood," Warren said. "All three."

He said them back to her, the way she'd said them, so there'd be no version of this where anyone remembered it differently later. "Mina stays — the whole time, for anything short of a fire. She gets it in writing: what to watch for, what's expected, what the worst of it looks like. And the third — if you come out the other side worse, or not at all, or not yourself — it's on the record, right now, in front of both of us: your choice. Nobody's tragedy to apologize for. Yours."

"Good." Perla folded her hands on the table, and her posture settled, like a decision finally made was lighter to carry than the deciding had been. "Then yes. I want to do this. Not because I'm ashamed of what I am now — I want that clear too, I am not ashamed of the last five years, whatever they took — but because I would like more years to be stubborn in, and I am tired of being careful with hope. Heal me, Warren. On my terms. Starting whenever you're ready."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-Four: The Harmony

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 012 addendum. Major set-piece sequence per Creator override. Alternates external (Mina) and internal (Warren/Perla's Eternal) perspective, marked with *. Not final prose — first pass at the novel's highest prose ambition so far.)
25_the_harmony.md

They did it in Perla's own bedroom, blinds drawn, door locked, a note already sent to the school where Perla used to substitute and the neighbor who watered her plants, explaining a family emergency that wasn't, technically, a lie, and that the plants would have to fend for themselves for a few days.

Warren lay down beside her on top of the covers, took her hand the way he had at her kitchen table that first night, and said, to both of them, "I don't know exactly what this is going to look like from the outside. I need you to trust that whatever you see, I'm still working, even when it looks like nothing's happening. Especially then."

"How will I know if something's actually wrong? Wrong-wrong, not scary-wrong."

"I don't have a clean answer for that. I wish I did." He looked at her, steady. "Trust the note I wrote you. If her breathing changes past what's written there, if mine does, call for help, and don't apologize for it, and don't wait for permission from anybody — including a version of me that might not be able to give it."

Mina nodded, throat too tight to actually say anything back, and watched her mother's eyes close, and watched Warren's close a half-second after, and then there was nothing to do but sit in the chair she'd dragged in from the kitchen and watch two people breathe.

*

Inside, it was dark the way the space between stars is dark — not empty, just unlit — and Warren had been in this particular kind of dark before, more times than he wanted to count, and it never once stopped being disorienting to arrive somewhere with no direction that meant anything yet.

He called out the only way he knew how, the way he'd called out at the very beginning of everything, before there was a word for calling: I am. Reaching, the way he always reached, turning the fact of himself over and over until something else had room to answer.

Nothing answered whole.

What came back instead were pieces — fragments, scattered wide, some so faint he had to hold impossibly still just to register they were there at all, like trying to hear a single voice in a stadium of static. He recognized the shape of it immediately, and hated recognizing it, because he had seen fracture before, had watched entire Eternals come apart this way across the long centuries of the diminishment, and he had never once gotten used to how much it looked like grief wearing the shape of geography — pieces scattered instead of gathered, no longer able to find their own edges.

He went to the closest fragment first. It took the shape, when he reached it, of a schoolroom — chalk dust, a window painted shut, the tired satisfaction of a Tuesday afternoon when a lesson had actually landed — and inside it, faint, a single unmistakable thread of I am, worn thin as old thread, still holding.

You are, he said to it, the way he'd said it exactly once before, at the actual beginning of everything, to someone whose face he still couldn't fully picture without an ache. This fragment didn't have a face to picture. It had a classroom, and chalk dust, and thirty years of Tuesdays, and it was, unmistakably, definitely, still someone.

There were others. A kitchen that smelled like garlic and vinegar and something patient on the stove, holding a fragment that flared bright and whole for one entire second at the word daughter before scattering again, too raw yet to stay gathered. A cemetery, yellow flowers, a specific quality of grief that had clearly been carried so long it had worn its own comfortable groove, like water finding the same channel every year. A wedding, older, further back, a version of Perla young enough that Warren understood, with a lurch he hadn't expected, that he was touching a memory from before the fracture had even properly begun, back when this Soul had still been whole enough to simply live inside a human life without this much effort.

He gathered what he could reach. Each fragment resisted at first — every genuinely fractured thing resists being touched — and each one, once it stopped resisting, arrived with its own unmistakable weight, refusing, even fractured, even faint, to be anything other than exactly itself.

That was the part that mattered. Not the gathering, which was mechanical, effortful, the kind of labor that left him hollowed out even from somewhere without a body to feel hollow in. What mattered was that nothing he touched dissolved into him. Every fragment stayed stubbornly, entirely its own thing, right up until the moment it chose — chose, every single time, nobody forced — to rejoin the rest.

We are, the gathered pieces said, eventually, together, in a voice that was somehow both plural and singular at once, both many Tuesdays and one continuous life, both an old classroom and a woman standing in her own kitchen making pancit for a stranger she'd decided to trust. Not one voice pretending to speak for all the pieces. Many pieces, choosing, finally, to mean the same true thing together.

*

Outside, on the second day, nothing changed, which was its own particular kind of unbearable.

Mina had called in sick to a job she no longer had permission to touch anyway, had eaten nothing she could later remember the taste of, had checked Warren's handwritten note against her mother's breathing so many times the paper had gone soft at the creases. Perla's face had started, sometime in the night, to go the wrong kind of pale, hands twitching in small patterns that looked less like rest and more like a body arguing with something that had no visible shape, and every single thing twenty years of genetics had ever taught her was screaming, quietly, constantly, to call an ambulance that would arrive and find nothing a hospital had a name for.

She didn't call. She held the note instead, and her mother's other hand, and did the only thing left that felt like doing something: she stayed.

*

It should have been enough. For a long moment, holding the gathered weight of nearly-whole in his hands, Warren believed it was enough, and let himself, just once, feel something like relief.

Then the last piece resisted.

It was smaller than the others, and further away, and when he finally reached it, he understood immediately why it had stayed hidden the longest: it was afraid. Not fractured the way the others were fractured — afraid, specifically, deliberately, the way something makes itself small and hard to find on purpose, because being found had, at some point, cost it something it hadn't fully recovered from. He recognized fear when he found it. He had considerable, personal, two-thousand-year expertise in exactly this particular shape of hiding.

I am, he said to it, gently as he knew how to be gentle.

It didn't answer.

He tried again, and again, patient in a way he did not naturally feel, because the alternative — leaving one piece scattered, one fragment permanently apart from the rest, incomplete in some way none of them could predict — was not a version of this he was willing to accept without exhausting every other option first. He thought of Perla at her kitchen table, the exact stubbornness of a woman who had spent five years refusing to be reduced to what a disease was taking from her. He thought that whatever this hiding piece was afraid of, it had learned the fear from somewhere real, and that the only honest way through it was not force, which he could have used, easily, and would never use, not on this, not on anyone — it was patience, offered for as long as patience took.

You don't have to be whole today, he told it, finally, when he'd run out of every other true thing he knew how to say. You just have to be real. That's all any of this has ever actually asked of anyone.

The small, hidden fragment shifted.

I am, it said, so quietly he nearly missed it, and then, a moment later, stronger: I am.

You are, he answered, and meant it entirely, and felt the last piece — frightened, small, real — finally, finally choose to come home.

*

Outside, on the third day, Mina watched her mother's breathing change.

It had been the worst of the three days so far, and Mina had sat in that chair long enough to stop entirely trusting her own memory of what normal had looked like to begin with. Now, something eased. She couldn't have said what, exactly — nothing as clean as a monitor changing its tone, nothing she could have pointed to and called proof — but the twitching stopped, first, and then the gray started, slowly, to leave her mother's face, color returning the way color returns to sky at the actual end of a long night rather than all at once, and Mina found herself leaning forward without deciding to, holding her own breath along with the two people in front of her who currently weren't breathing anything that looked ordinary at all.

*

Inside, in the last full moment before it stopped being an inside at all, Warren felt the whole gathered weight of it — every fragment, every Tuesday, every yellow flower, every frightened small thing that had finally agreed to come home — turn, together, toward the one presence that had been waiting, patient and human and entirely herself, at the center of all of it the whole time.

Harmony did not erase her. He wanted, more than he had wanted almost anything in two thousand years, for someone to understand that clearly: it did not erase her. Perla did not disappear into her Soul, and her Soul did not disappear into Perla, and neither one of them disappeared into him. What happened instead was smaller than a myth and larger than a sentence — three genuinely separate, genuinely whole consciousnesses, recognizing each other fully, at last, without any of them needing to stop being themselves to do it.

We are, all three of them said, together, meaning three different true things by it, none of them canceling any of the others out.

And then, gently, without any drama at all, the way a held breath finally, naturally, lets go, it was over, and he was lying on top of a bedspread in a locked room with the blinds drawn, and someone was crying, close by, and it took him a full disoriented second to understand the sound was relief.

*

Perla opened her eyes first.

She didn't say anything remarkable. That was, Mina would think later, the most remarkable part of all of it — no gasp, no dramatic first word, no movie-perfect return. Her mother just opened her eyes, focused slowly on the ceiling, then on Mina's face, and said, in a voice that was entirely, unmistakably, gloriously her own:

"You let the plants die, didn't you. I could feel it. Somehow, wherever I was, I could feel that you let my plants die."

Mina laughed and cried at exactly the same time, which she hadn't known was physically possible until that precise moment, and put her head down on the bed beside her mother's hand, and didn't answer, because the plants had, in fact, died, and some victories didn't need to be discussed the very first minute they were won.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-Five

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 013. Not final prose.)
26_aftermath.md

Perla slept for most of the first week, real sleep this time, not the suspended unconsciousness of the three days — ordinary, exhausted, human sleep, the kind that left her disoriented and ravenous when she finally woke for good, demanding food before she'd even fully focused her eyes.

"I want the adobo," she said, the first coherent sentence out of her mouth in four days, "and I want to know what day it is, in that order, and I am not discussing my own miracle on an empty stomach."

Mina laughed, and cried again, which she was starting to suspect was just going to be her body's default setting for the foreseeable future, and made the adobo.

Okonkwo had called on the second day of the three — not HR, Okonkwo himself, which told her everything she needed to know about how the conversation had gone upstairs. Family medical leave, effective immediately; her access to the flagged datasets quietly revoked. He hadn't asked what she was doing in Corbin's Landing. She hadn't told him. She'd thanked him, and meant it, and hung up feeling the particular relief of a door closing that you'd been bracing to have slammed.

It was strange, the recovery — not clean, not a light switch. Some things came back sharp and immediate: Perla knew Mina's childhood nickname for their old dog within the first hour, a detail she hadn't reliably produced in two years. Other things stayed stubbornly out of reach — a specific vacation, a coworker's name, some argument Perla was fairly sure she'd once had with Ernesto that neither of them, apparently, got back that night. Warren had warned them about that, quietly, the day after: healing wasn't erasure of the damage's history, only restoration of the connection that let the mind reach what remained, and he was honest that he couldn't promise which was which — whether a given memory was simply harder to reach right now, or gone in some fuller sense even he didn't have the words to be certain of. "Unavailable," he told her, "isn't the same thing as proven gone. I just don't always know which one I'm looking at."

"I remember being lost," Perla said, one evening, unprompted, staring at nothing in particular. "Inside it, I mean. Wherever I was. I remember being in pieces and not minding it as much as I should have, and I remember somebody finding all of them anyway, one at a time, very patient about it, even the one that didn't want to be found." She looked at Warren, who was doing dishes at her sink with more focus than dishes required. "That was you, being patient."

"That was me."

"I don't remember being afraid you wouldn't find it in time."

"You should have been. I was."

*

Mina found her own reaction harder to name than she'd expected. She had wanted this — had asked for it, fought for it, sat through three of the worst days of her life for it — and now that it had actually happened, what she felt wasn't uncomplicated joy so much as a kind of vertigo, the exact sensation of a floor she'd spent her whole adult life trusting turning out to be considerably less solid than advertised.

She was a scientist. She had spent twenty years building a career on the specific, disciplined faith that reality followed rules, that things which happened had mechanisms, that the difference between magic and medicine was mostly a matter of how much you currently understood. She still believed that, technically — Warren had never once described anything to her as magic, had always reached for cause and structure even in the strangest parts of his own explanations — but believing the mechanism existed and having watched it work, close enough to touch, were turning out to be two very different kinds of belief, and she wasn't sure yet what the second kind was going to cost her.

"You've done this before," she said to him, a few nights later, both of them sitting on her mother's back step while Perla slept, actually slept, upstairs. "Healings. As Yeshua." She turned to look at him directly. "If you can do this — if you can actually do this, and I've seen it, I'm not asking hypothetically anymore — then why is there still a single person in this world in pain. Why isn't this the only thing you do."

"Because your mother asked. Directly, on her own terms, fully informed, and I said yes to one specific person who asked me a specific question after weeks of genuinely understanding what she was asking for." He set his glass down. "That's not a door I can leave propped open for the world, Mina. I don't have the right to walk into a hospital and decide, unasked, whose disease matters enough to override. I did that once, badly, in a different life, and people built an entire religion trying to explain why I stopped, and the honest answer was never as clean as any of the explanations. I stopped because a version of me that heals everyone stops being someone people get to have an actual relationship with. I become the emergency room. I don't get to be a person anymore, and neither does anyone I heal without asking — I just become the thing that happened to them."

"That's not a satisfying answer."

"No. It's the honest one, though, and I'd rather give you that than something that lets either of us sleep easier tonight."

She leaned her shoulder against his. "On the second night I kept thinking I'd agreed to the thing that killed her. That I'd signed the paper on losing my mother." Her voice went thin. "She's the only parent I've got, Warren. Simple as that, and I still couldn't do it."

Chapter Twenty-Six

(WORKING DRAFT — merged from 27_voss_consequence.md and 29_voss_prescription_data.md per structural pass, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)
27_voss_consequence.md

Voss noticed the prescription first — a small thing, the kind of small thing his compliance team was specifically paid to notice, a five-year refill pattern for a well-documented dementia medication that simply stopped, cold, with no death certificate, no facility transfer, no explanation in any record his people could legitimately access.

He knew, immediately, what it probably meant. He also knew that knowing and proving were two very different problems.

---

They agreed, the three of them, not to tell Perla's neurologist the truth, which meant they had to decide, carefully, what half-truth might actually hold up.

"If her scans come back different, someone's going to ask questions," Mina said. "Doctors don't get to just not notice a complete neurological reversal. It'll go in a file. Files get shared."

"Especially," Warren said, "with a hospital that already has one very interested outside partner reading its research flags."

They settled, eventually, on the least satisfying and most survivable option: no follow-up scans at all, for now, on the grounds that Perla was declining further testing, a right any patient genuinely had, however suspicious it might look to anyone paying close attention. Mina hated it. Hated managing her own mother's medical record like evidence in a case, hated that the correct move for keeping Perla safe was concealing the very proof that would have made Perla's recovery a miracle worth celebrating instead of a liability to bury.

"This is what he's actually stealing," she said, later. "Not just Warren. This. The part where my mother gets to have the best thing that's ever happened to her without it becoming a data point somebody else gets to fight over."

---

"I want everything on the daughter," Voss told Priyanka, his assistant at Halcyon, the morning the pattern crossed his desk. "Employment status, financial records, anything public. Nothing that requires a subpoena. I am not doing this the wrong way twice."

He meant it when he said it. He mostly kept the promise, for exactly nine days, before a very expensive, very discreet firm he'd used once before for a due-diligence matter quietly obtained six months of Perla Mendez's pharmacy fill history from a records broker whose actual legal standing to sell that data was, at absolute best, a matter of ongoing regulatory dispute — the kind of information access that lived in a gray zone real enough that Voss's own lawyers would have told him not to touch it, had he asked them first, which was exactly why he hadn't asked them first.

He read the report at midnight, alone, in an office two floors above a lab that had spent six years failing to save anyone the way this man apparently just had, and felt the unwelcome lift in his chest again — hope, arriving uninvited, exactly as untrustworthy as it had ever been — and told himself, the way he always told himself, that the ends of this were worth whatever the means eventually cost him.

He did not yet know that the means themselves, once they surfaced, were going to be the thing that cost him everything else.

---

Priyanka typed the wire confirmation herself, because the account was one she administered and because he had asked her to, and she had gotten good, over four years, at not asking the second question. That night she opened the confirmation email meaning to delete it. Her thumb hovered over the delete key for a long moment, and then didn't press it. She moved it to a folder she named, with a precision that would later embarrass her, "Admin - Misc," and told herself it was for the expense reconciliation.

Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: An Ordinary Tuesday (Warren/Yeshua, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 009. Mary present as "Miriam," unremarkable, identity/bond NOT explained. Not final prose.)
memory_yeshua_ordinary_day.md

"Before I start this one," Warren said, "I need you to say the name right. Not because I'm precious about it. Because everyone's gotten it wrong for so long that hearing it wrong barely even registers anymore, and I want this one time, telling you, to actually be right."

"Yeshua."

"Yeh-SHOO-ah. Close enough — you'll get the rhythm of it after you've heard me say it a few more times." He rubbed the back of his neck, a habit she'd started to recognize as the physical shape of him gathering himself. "You know what actually happened to my name? Aramaic to Greek, Greek to Latin, Latin to whatever English decided to do to it — by the time it came out the other end it had survived four languages that all had opinions about it and agreed with none of them. Jesus isn't wrong because people are stupid. It's wrong because it's been translated so many times it's basically a different word wearing my old one's coat." He almost smiled. "Warren, by comparison, nobody's ever once mispronounced. That's most of why I picked it. Two thousand years of people mangling one name will do that to a man."

"So I'm allowed to laugh at that part."

"You're required to laugh at that part. It's the only genuinely funny thing about any of this." He settled back, and something in his posture changed — not heavier, exactly, but more careful, the way a man moves differently once he's decided to actually set something down instead of carrying it past you again. "Okay. This one isn't about anything you'd recognize. No crowds, no miracles, none of the parts that made it into anybody's book. This is just a Tuesday. I want to start you on a Tuesday."

*

The workshop belonged to his father before it belonged to him, half-belonged to him still, in the way a business half-belongs to a son who's spent his whole life inside it — and on this particular Tuesday, it was hot enough that the wood shavings stuck to his arms instead of falling away from them, and a man named Old Chusa was standing in the doorway for the third time that week complaining about a yoke.

"It's crooked."

"It's not crooked. Your ox is crooked." Yeshua didn't look up from the plane in his hands, because Chusa's complaints had a rhythm to them by now, three visits deep, and this was the part where you let him finish before you actually solved anything. "I measured it against your own animal, in your own field, with your own son watching. If it sits crooked it's because the beast's shoulders don't match, not because I can't hold a straight line."

"My father never had this trouble with a yoke."

"Your father bought yokes from a man in Sepphoris who overcharged him for wood that split in a wet winter, which I know because I'm the one who's fixed three of them." He set the plane down, finally, and looked at the old man with the patience of someone who liked him anyway, underneath the argument. "Bring the ox by tomorrow. I'll look at the shoulders myself before I touch the wood again. No charge for the looking."

Chusa grumbled the whole way out, the way he grumbled about everything, and would be back in a week with some new complaint that wasn't really about a yoke at all — Yeshua had understood, a year or two into this trade, that half of what people brought you to fix wasn't the thing they said it was, and the other half of the job was letting them say it anyway.

His brother James stuck his head in the moment Chusa's shadow cleared the door. "He still on about the yoke?"

"He'll be on about the yoke till one of us dies."

"Ima wants to know if you're eating with us or sulking out here till dark again."

"I'm not sulking. I'm working."

"You're sulking with tools in your hands, it's your specialty." James grinned, ducked the wood scrap thrown at his head with the ease of a man who'd been dodging his brother's temper his whole life, and left before Yeshua could throw anything more accurate.

The heat didn't break until near sundown, and by then his shoulders ached in the honest way of a day spent doing something with his hands that mattered to somebody, and he was washing sawdust off his arms at the well when Miriam came by with water jars balanced against her hip, the way she did most evenings, and didn't say anything at first, just set the jars down and looked at him with an expression he wouldn't have been able to name even now, even with two thousand years of practice at naming things.

"Chusa again?"

"Chusa again."

"You could just tell him his ox is old and dying and the yoke's not the problem."

"I could. Then he'd have nothing left to be angry about, and I don't think he's ready to be angry about the actual thing yet." He wrung water out of his sleeve, not looking at her directly, aware of her anyway the way you're aware of sun on the back of your neck. "Some people need a smaller, fixable problem to be furious about so they don't have to look at the big one they can't fix at all."

"That's a very patient way of describing a man who's yelled at you three times this week."

"I have a lot of patience for people who are actually scared of something else."

Miriam picked her water jars back up, and didn't leave right away, which was, he understood even then, a sentence in its own right. "You're a strange man, Yeshua."

"So I'm told."

"I mean it kindly."

"I know you do," he said, and something passed between them that neither one of them had a word for yet, ordinary as the evening around it, gone as soon as she smiled and walked on toward her own door, and he stood there a moment longer than the walk back to his own house required, for reasons he wasn't in any hurry to examine.

*

"That's it?" Mina said, when he didn't keep going. "A yoke and some water jars?"

"That's it. That's a Tuesday. Most of my life was Tuesdays, before it wasn't anymore, and I think people forget that on purpose, because a life made of mostly ordinary days is a lot harder to build a religion out of than three years of extraordinary ones." He looked at his own hands again, an old habit. "I wanted you to have one ordinary day before I give you any of the other kind. So you'd know there was a person under all of it — doing a trade, annoying his brother, fixing a stubborn old man's yoke for free because the man needed the kindness more than he needed the repair."

"Who was she? Miriam."

His face closed like a door easing shut without quite latching — careful, deliberate. "Someone I knew," he said. "That's as far as that one goes tonight."

Mina let it go, the way she was learning to, and did not ask again — though she noted, the way she noted everything now, that it was the first time in weeks he'd ended a story by choosing not to finish it rather than by running out of what he was willing to say.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-Seven

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 009, short cultural-archaeology-engine test. Not final prose.)
17_the_documentary.md

Three nights after the yoke story, she found him with his laptop open to a documentary called Daily Life in Biblical Times, narrated by a man with an extremely serious voice, muttering at the screen.

"What did it do."

"They've got the yoke wrong." He didn't look up. "That's not how you'd rig a yoke to an ox that size, the proportions are backwards, whoever built that prop has clearly never stood next to an actual animal in his life."

"You're watching a documentary about Jesus and the thing that's bothering you is the yoke."

"The yoke is factually incorrect. The theology I have complicated feelings about would take considerably longer than forty-five minutes to unpack. The yoke is just wrong, and it's wrong in a way that's actually fixable, so yes, that's what's bothering me." He finally paused the video, on a frame of a dramatically lit desert landscape that looked, he privately thought, nothing at all like the actual color of that dirt at that time of year. "You want to know the real secret? Almost nobody arguing about any of this cares about getting the small true things right. They're all so busy being right about the big unknowable thing that the yoke never even makes it onto anybody's list of problems worth solving."

"That seems like it should bother you more than it apparently does."

"It bothers me exactly the right amount," he said, and hit play again, and muttered something else at the screen a minute later that made her laugh before she'd even heard what it was.

Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: The Steward's Grain (Warren/Yeshua, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 013, third Yeshua memory-story. Miriam given real agency and competence, independent of romantic framing; identity not revealed. Not final prose.)
memory_yeshua_miriam_money.md

She managed the money, which was not a detail that made it into any of the accounts later, and which Warren found, even now, mildly infuriating on her behalf, because it had been considerable work and she had been extremely good at it.

There was a night — early autumn, the group larger than it had any business being, more mouths than anyone had planned to feed, and a local steward who did not like the size of the crowd camping near his master's land and had decided, loudly, in front of everyone, that they'd be gone by morning or he'd have men see to it — when Miriam stepped between Yeshua and the steward before Yeshua had finished deciding what to say, and simply started negotiating.

"You have grain rotting in that storehouse from last season," she said, not raising her voice, which made the steward lower his. "I can see it from here, the sacks are the wrong color. We'll take it off your hands at a fair price, feed forty people who'll be gone in two days regardless, and you get paid for grain you were going to lose anyway. Or you can have your men make a scene in front of your master's neighbors over forty tired travelers, and explain to him afterward why that seemed like the better use of anyone's evening."

The steward looked at her for a long moment, looked at Yeshua, who had wisely said nothing at all, and named a price. She talked him down from it anyway, on principle.

"You didn't need to do that," Yeshua told her afterward, not quite a complaint.

"No. I wanted to. There's a difference, and I'd think a man in your line of work would recognize it by now." She counted coins into a pouch with the brisk satisfaction of someone who genuinely enjoyed this part, more than he'd ever fully understood. "You handle the crowds. I handle the actual survival of the crowds. We've had this arrangement for two years, I don't know why it still surprises you."

"It doesn't surprise me. I just like watching you do it."

She glanced up at that, something sharp and pleased crossing her face before she went back to counting. "Careful," she said, not looking up again. "A man could get used to being looked at like that."

"I know," he said, quiet enough that she might not have caught it at all, and went to go tell forty hungry people that dinner had been handled, again, by someone considerably better at this than he was.

*

"She sounds terrifying," Mina said, when he'd finished.

"She was. In the very best way anyone's ever been terrifying at me." Something eased in his face, the ease of a memory that had cost less to tell than he'd braced for. "People remember the parables. Nobody remembers that the entire operation ran on a woman who could talk a hostile steward out of spoiled grain at a profit. I've always thought that was a considerably larger loss to history than it gets credit for."

Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: Simeon's Hand (Warren/Yeshua, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 010. Shows an extraordinary capability grounded in physical cost; explains the "God" perception without endorsing the category. Not final prose.)
memory_yeshua_simeons_hand.md

"I want to understand what it actually is," Mina said, a few days after the conversation neither of them had fully recovered from yet. "Not the theology. Not what people made of it after. What it actually felt like, from your side, to heal somebody."

"All right." Warren considered that longer than usual. "I'll give you one. Not the most famous one — I don't especially want to relitigate the famous ones with you yet, some of them are still tender in ways I haven't figured out how to hand you gently. This one nobody wrote down. Which is most of them."

*

There was a man in a fishing village on the north shore — I'm not going to tell you which lake, not tonight, some of the geography still aches — named Simeon, who'd had his right hand crushed under a rope-and-stone winch when he was younger than I was, badly enough that it had healed wrong and stayed wrong for eleven years: curled in on itself, useless for netting, useless for most things, and he'd spent over a decade being the man in the village who couldn't quite pull his own weight, which in a fishing village is its own particular, grinding shame that has nothing to do with the actual pain of the hand and everything to do with what other men's faces do when they think you can't see them doing it.

I'd been in the village three days, mostly talking, mostly eating other people's food and trying to be useful with my own two working hands wherever I could, and Simeon hadn't asked me for anything. Wouldn't. I watched him decide not to, more than once, and understood exactly what that cost him, because I'd spent considerable time myself learning the specific dignity of not asking for things you badly wanted.

His daughter asked instead. Cornered me by the boats at dusk, maybe nine years old, absolutely fearless in the way only very young children and very old women ever manage to be around me, and said, more or less, everyone says you can fix things, so why haven't you fixed my father's hand — which is a question I did not have a good answer for at the time and still don't, fully, except that I'd been waiting, the way I was still waiting, decades later, with your mother, for someone to actually ask.

I went to him that night. Told him what his daughter had said, gave him every chance to tell me no, and watched him not say no for a long, long time before he finally held the hand out, shaking, less from the injury than from something closer to hope, which is a considerably more frightening thing to watch a grown man hold out to you than fear ever was.

I want to tell you it was clean. It wasn't. It cost me something to do it — I don't have better words for you than that, even now, it was work, real work, the kind that leaves you hollowed out and shaking afterward the same way his hand had been shaking before — and it took time, more time than either of us expected, standing there in the dark by the boats with his whole family gathering without either of us telling them to, drawn by something in the air I genuinely believe they could feel before they understood what they were feeling.

When it was done — and it was done, the bones set true, the strength came back to it over the following days the way strength comes back to anything, gradually, with use — he didn't say anything to me at all. He put both hands flat on my face, the way a father does to a child, which was backwards, which had always been backwards, everyone got that part backwards, and he wept in a way I don't think he'd allowed himself since the accident itself.

By morning the whole village knew. By the following week, three other villages knew, in versions that had already started growing extra details the way stories do, and within a year I stopped being able to walk into a fishing town on that coast without a crowd forming before I'd said a single word.

That's the part nobody tells you about, when they tell you what it was like to be able to do what I could do. Not the healing itself. The after. Once people believe a thing like that about you, you stop being able to just be tired, or hungry, or wrong about something small, or in a bad mood on a bad day, because none of that fits the shape they've already decided you are. I understand exactly why they called it God, watching a broken hand come back whole in the dark. I understand it, and I still think they were reaching for the only word big enough they had, and I still think the word was the wrong shape for what actually happened, which was: one very tired man, doing real work, for one father who'd finally let his daughter ask on his behalf.

*

Mina didn't say anything for a long moment. "That's not what I expected."

"What did you expect?"

"I don't know. Light, maybe. Something with less sweat in it."

"There was a great deal of sweat in it." Something almost like a laugh, tired at the edges. "That's most of what I want you to understand, actually, if you understand nothing else from this one. It was work. Extraordinary work, work nobody else could have done, but work — with a cost, done by someone who was exhausted afterward, and grateful it had worked, and a little bit afraid, every single time, that it wouldn't."

Question Session Working draft

Question Session: "Ask Me Again. Not Sometime. Soon." (Mina and Warren)

(WORKING DRAFT — split from the original question-session file per Archivist instruction to distribute rather than leave as one appendix. Placement: after enough Yeshua-era material that a Mary question lands with real weight; a deliberate, single, serious inversion of the retired "ask me again sometime" device — do not restart it as a running tic. Not final prose.)
question_mary_soon.md

She asked about Mary on a Tuesday, out of nowhere, no windup, the way she'd learned by now was the only way that ever got a real answer instead of a deflection.

"Was she your favorite? Out of everyone you've told me about so far."

The joke was already halfway out of his mouth — she watched it start, watched him catch it before it landed, watched the whole shape of the evening change in under a second. "That's not a question I get to answer lightly," he said, quiet now, all the play gone out of his voice. "Not because I'm being coy. Because the honest answer is bigger than a chair-tipped-back kind of conversation, and I'm not ready to give it to you standing in my wrong-sized room for it. Ask me again. Not sometime. Soon. I mean that."

Mina didn't push. She'd gotten good, she thought, at knowing the difference between a door being held shut and a door someone was asking her, gently, to wait outside of a little longer.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Twenty-Eight

(WORKING DRAFT — revised Author/Archivist pass 2026-09-19. Pre-attraction calibration. Not final prose.)
28_warren_parenthood_fear.md

"Can I ask you something purely as a scientist," Mina said one evening, from her usual place on Warren's floor with her back against the bed frame. He had offered her the one chair the first three times. She had eventually told him to stop.

"That phrase has never once been followed by something purely scientific."

"Your genetics. Have you ever actually looked at them? Sequenced, I mean. Properly."

"No." The answer came faster than most of his answers did. "And before you ask, no, you can't either. Not that question, not ever. I need you to actually hear how firm that no is."

Mina lowered the mug she'd been holding. "I wasn't going to steal your DNA."

"I know."

"You made that sound very much like you thought I was going to steal your DNA."

"I think you're a geneticist confronted with a medically impossible man who has made the tactical error of drinking from cups in your presence."

She looked at his mug on the floor between them.

Warren picked it up.

"That is insulting."

"It is preventive."

"I have ethics."

"So did several people who kept bits of saints."

"You're comparing me to medieval relic thieves now?"

"I was trying not to."

She almost laughed, then saw that the joke had been a door he'd closed rather than one he'd opened.

"Okay," she said. "Firm no. Heard."

Warren kept the mug.

"My line — the one that runs back through Abraham, through Moses, all of it — carries the deepest version of the compatibility trait that exists in your species. As far as I understand it, which isn't as far as you'd think." He rested his forearms on his knees. "Some of those old stories about patriarchs and prophets living extraordinary lengths of time — I don't think they're purely invention. That doesn't mean I think every number survived transmission literally. People copied, translated, symbolized, compressed, expanded, argued. Humans are very talented at preserving the shape of a thing while changing its measurements."

"That's annoyingly plausible."

"I've had practice."

"And you were the endpoint."

"The strongest concentration I know of. Which is not the same as saying I know everything it could do."

Mina turned that over. "And you're telling me this because."

"Because you're a geneticist who's spent twenty years thinking in terms of what gets passed down, and I've watched that question getting closer for months."

"What question?"

Warren looked at the mug in his hands instead of at her.

That was unusual enough to answer before he did.

"I don't know what a child of mine would inherit," he said. "Not exactly. Possibly nothing remarkable at all. Possibly considerably more than either parent could safely prepare a person for."

Mina went still.

"I've had reason to worry about that question for a very long time," he continued. "And it isn't hypothetical anxiety. I have a reason."

She waited.

"I'll tell you the reason eventually. I'm not ready tonight."

There were a dozen questions she could have asked. Six months ago she would have asked at least nine of them.

Instead she nodded once.

"All right."

Warren looked up, perhaps expecting the interrogation to arrive late.

It didn't.

Mina took another drink of coffee.

After a while Warren set his mug back down.

"You're not going to steal that, are you?"

She looked at it.

Then at him.

"No promises."

He picked it up again.

That time she did laugh.

She filed the shape of his refusal away with the others, but not as evidence against him. Whatever sat behind it had frightened a man who could discuss his own execution with less difficulty.

When he was ready, he would tell her.

For tonight, that was enough.

Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: Tamar (Warren, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Handoff 014, centerpiece of this batch. Not final prose.)
memory_tamar.md

He came to her room a week later, unprompted, sat down across from her instead of beside her, which she'd learned by now meant something formal was coming, and said, without preamble, "Her name was Tamar."

Mina didn't say anything. She'd learned that too.

"I had a daughter. Once. A long time ago, and a long time after — after everything, after the tomb, after I'd already become someone the world had decided to stop being able to see clearly. I want to tell you about her properly. Not the ending first. I've made that mistake before, with other people, telling the ending first because the ending is the part that actually costs me something to say, and it isn't fair to her, making her be only the worst thing that happened to her. So I'm going to tell you about a person. And then, because you deserve the whole thing and not a curated version of it, I'm going to tell you how she died."

*

Alexandria, this time — a hundred years or so after the tomb, give or take, in the part of my life where I'd settled into being a man named Eliezer, a scribe and sometime physician's assistant, married to a woman named Dinah who ran a dye shop three streets from the harbor and had opinions about absolutely everything, most of them correct.

Tamar was eleven when it happened, which means she was, for most of the years I actually had her, exactly as difficult as any eleven-year-old who's smarter than both her parents and has recently discovered this fact about herself. She had her mother's stubbornness and — I only understood this later, far too late — considerably more of my own history than either of us had any right to expect a child to carry.

She hated her letters and loved numbers, which used to drive Dinah into a genuine fury, since Dinah had spent considerable effort teaching her both and could not understand how a child could be brilliant at one and openly hostile to the other. She kept a cat that despised everyone except her, on principle, the same principle by which the cat seemed to despise most of existence. She had my laugh, or the laugh I used in that life — lower, quicker to arrive than this one — and she used it constantly, at things that weren't always funny, because she'd worked out early that laughing at something made it easier to argue with later.

I taught her how to read the tides, which was useless and she loved it anyway. She taught me — genuinely taught me, I was not humoring her — a knucklebone game from the dockworkers' children that I still, on certain very specific nights, catch myself wanting to play and having nobody left alive who knows the rules.

The healing started small enough that for a long time we told ourselves it was coincidence, the way parents do with the things they're most afraid to name. A scraped knee that closed too fast. A fever that broke in an hour instead of three days. The cat, of all things — I watched Tamar hold that miserable, universally hated animal after it had been kicked by a cart-horse, watched its leg straighten under her hands in a way that should not have been possible, and watched her look up at me afterward with an expression that had far more curiosity in it than fear, which terrified me considerably more than fear would have.

"Did you know I could do that," she asked me. Not a child's question. A very specific, very pointed question, the kind she'd apparently been saving.

"I suspected," I told her, which was true, and inadequate, and the beginning of the worst mistake of that entire life: I suspected, and I did not tell her enough, fast enough, about what it might cost her to be seen doing it.

I want to be precise with you about what she actually was, because I've heard myself simplify it before, in my own head, in the two thousand years since, and the simple version is a lie by omission. She was not carrying some diluted copy of what I can do, passed down like a coin from my pocket to hers. Tamar had her own Eternal Soul, the way nearly everyone does, and what she inherited from me wasn't power directly — it was an unusual, concentrated capacity for that Soul and her own human self to reach a depth of resonance most human-Soul pairs never come near. The healing came through that resonance, hers and her Soul's together, not through anything of mine passed into her like an heirloom. She was never a smaller version of me. She was herself, joined to someone of her own, reaching a kind of closeness with that someone that the accident of her birth had made unusually possible. I've spent considerable effort making sure I don't let that distinction blur, even now. It matters to who she actually was.

Alexandria, in those years, was not a city where a Jewish family could afford to be unusual. The war — the one your histories will eventually call the Kitos War, though nobody called it that while it was happening to us — had ended when Tamar's parents were barely grown, and what it left behind wasn't a community nursing old wounds. It was what was left of one. The quarter had been gutted. The people still there were a remnant — the ones who'd stayed, or come back, and started over with the memory of the suppression still fresh — and every family among them knew that Rome's patience for them was thin as ice on a puddle. The garrison watched what remained of Jewish Alexandria the way you watch something you've already decided is dangerous and are only waiting for an excuse to confirm it. And our own people — this is the part that's harder to tell you than the Romans, because you want a clean villain and I'm not going to hand you one — our own people had learned, the hard way, exactly how much a single incident could cost the few who were left, because Rome had already shown them once what it would do to everyone when it decided the whole of them was the problem.

There was a fever that spring, the kind that moves through a crowded quarter the way fire moves through dry grass, taking the youngest hardest, the way it always does, and one night it took hold of a neighbor's boy — six years old, a friend of hers, more her friend than mine.

I was in Pelusium, up the coast, on an errand I'd planned for weeks — collecting payment owed us for a set of commissioned scrolls I'd spent half a year copying, money we genuinely needed, and buying alum for Dinah's dye work while I had the trip anyway, because alum was cheaper there and we were, that season, careful with money in the ordinary way every family is careful with money. It was the most unremarkable errand of my life. I have run that fact through my memory more times than any other single detail of the whole story, because there was nothing reckless in it, nothing I'd have done differently if I'd been able to see even one hour ahead, and somehow that makes it worse, not better, that the decision which cost her everything was the single most responsible, ordinary thing I did that entire year.

Tamar didn't wait for me. She didn't wait for anyone. She went into that house at night, alone, and she did for that boy what she'd only ever done in private, small, deniable ways before — she healed him, completely, visibly, in front of his mother and his aunt and, because the world has a specific cruelty reserved for exactly this kind of moment, a Roman officer's freedman who happened to be visiting the quarter that same night on entirely unrelated business and saw the whole thing through an open window.

She knew what she was risking. I need you to understand that, because I have spent longer than I can tell you making sure I understand it myself — piecing it together afterward from what Dinah could bear to tell me and what the boy's mother told Dinah, since I wasn't there and never will pretend I saw it with my own eyes. She was eleven, and she was frightened, and she did it anyway, because a boy she loved was dying and she had the one thing in the world that could stop it, and by every account I've ever trusted, she decided, on her own, faster than anyone could have talked her out of it, that his life was worth more than her own safety. That was not recklessness. That is the bravest thing I know she ever did, secondhand as my knowing it is, and it is also the thing that killed her, and I have never once been able to make those two facts sit easily next to each other.

I got back days later. By then it was already moving the way these things move once they start — the freedman had reported what he'd seen, half out of genuine unease and half, I've always suspected, because reporting it protected him from any suspicion of witchcraft-adjacent association himself. What happened next was never a trial, never a formal proceeding either Rome or our own community would have put its name to afterward — nothing so clean as that, nothing anyone involved would ever have written down. It was a handful of frightened men, some of them people who had known Tamar her whole life, making a quiet, deniable, unrecorded decision out of raw terror of what a single accusation of harboring sorcery might bring down on everyone if it reached the wrong Roman ears in a city that had not finished burying its own dead from the last time Rome decided the whole community was dangerous. I have never found a document anywhere, in any century, that admits to what they did. I have only ever had Dinah's account, and my own certainty, born of having lived through enough frightened communities since, that this is exactly the shape terror like that usually takes: not a law, not a court, just people convincing each other, quietly, that one death was a smaller risk than what might happen to everyone if they let her live.

I will not tell you the details of what happened to her. Not because I'm protecting you from them. Because I have never once, in the retelling, made it sound like anything other than what it actually was, which was a frightened community and a frightened empire converging on one eleven-year-old girl who had done nothing but save a friend's life, and no amount of careful language makes that easier to hold, and you don't need the specific cruelty of it to understand the shape of the loss. She was gone before I reached the city. Dinah had tried, alone, to stop it, and had been hurt badly enough doing so that she never fully trusted her own left arm again for the rest of her life. I was too far away to get back in time, doing something that felt, at the time, like it mattered.

I have run the calculation more times than you could count. If I had been home. If I had understood faster what she was capable of and taught her to be more afraid of it sooner. If I had simply never left. I don't have a version of that arithmetic that doesn't end with her still alive, which is either the cruelest possible truth or the only mercy left in the whole story, and after two thousand years I still can't tell you which.

*

He was quiet for a long time after that, and Mina didn't fill it, because some silences weren't hers to end.

"That's why," he said finally. "Not the genetics. Not really. I've told myself for centuries that it's the genetics, because the genetics is a problem I can at least talk about like a rational man instead of what it actually is, which is a father who has spent two thousand years unable to decide whether loving her was worth what it cost her."

"It wasn't your fault."

"I know you believe that. I'm not sure I'm capable of believing it myself, and I don't think you talking me into it tonight is going to be the thing that finally works, after everything else that hasn't." He looked at her, tired in a way that went past centuries into something more immediate. "I'm not telling you this so you'll fix it. There isn't a fix. I'm telling you because you asked me a real question a week ago and you deserved a real answer, even the part of it that doesn't resolve."

Mina reached over and took his hand, not to say anything, just to be a fact in the room he could hold onto, and they sat like that for a long time, and she understood, more clearly than she had understood anything else he'd told her so far, that whatever came next between them, it was never going to be simple, because nothing that costs this much ever is.

Chapter Twenty-Nine

(WORKING DRAFT — revised Author/Archivist pass 2026-09-19. Emotional intimacy preserved without premature romantic signaling. Not final prose.)
30_perla_and_tamar_math.md

"Can I ask you something that's going to sound terrible," Mina said, a few days after Tamar, both of them sitting on the tailgate of Denny's truck at the end of a Thursday she'd started coming to on purpose now. "My mother's alive because you said yes to her. Your daughter's dead because nobody could say yes fast enough for her. I've been turning that over for a week and I can't find a version of it that isn't a terrible thing to have noticed."

"It's not terrible. It's the actual math of it, and I'd rather you say it out loud than carry it around not saying it." He looked at his hands, an old habit she'd stopped needing him to explain. "I don't have an answer that makes it fair. Your mother had five years of warning, a family who loved her enough to fight for her, and a version of me who'd had two thousand years to learn how to actually ask the right questions before acting. Tamar had none of that. She had an emergency, a frightened city, and a father who wasn't home. It isn't that Perla deserved saving more. It's that the two situations weren't remotely the same shape, and I've stopped pretending, even to myself, that there's a clean moral underneath the difference. Sometimes the timing is just the timing. That's a genuinely unsatisfying thing to tell you. It's also true."

Mina was quiet for a while, watching Denny's regulars pack up their folding table inside, laughter drifting out through the propped door. "I keep thinking I'm supposed to feel lucky. And I do. I just also feel guilty, a little, in a way I can't fully justify — like my mother got the version of you that had learned from every mistake, and Tamar got the version that hadn't yet."

"That's not guilt you should be carrying. That's mine, if it belongs to anyone." He looked back toward the open door, where Denny was accusing somebody of stealing a folding chair that had apparently been Denny's folding chair since the Reagan administration. "But I understand why you're carrying it anyway. I've been doing the same math about people I loved for two thousand years, and I still haven't found the version of arithmetic where it comes out even."

Mina nodded.

There wasn't anything useful to add. That had become easier between them lately — letting a conversation finish without trying to rescue it from the silence afterward. Months ago she would have filled the space with another question. Warren would have answered because answering was what he'd agreed to do, and they would have kept going until exhaustion gave one of them permission to stop.

Tonight they sat.

Denny came to the doorway, pointed at them both and said, "If either of you knows where my chair went, silence makes you accessories."

"It's behind you," Mina said.

Denny looked behind him.

"Nobody likes a smartass."

"You invited me."

"Historic lapse in judgment."

He disappeared back inside with the chair.

Warren watched him go. "He's been saying that to me for eleven years."

"Wasn't it seventeen the last time you said it?"

"He revises," Warren said. "It's a living number."

"Maybe eventually you'll take the feedback."

"I've had worse reviews."

She laughed, and the heaviness loosened without disappearing. That, too, had become familiar: Warren's refusal to pretend a terrible thing had stopped being terrible merely because the conversation had reached its natural end.

"I'm not going anywhere," Mina said after a while.

Warren looked at her then.

She meant the stories. The questions. The increasingly absurd fact of her life. Perla was downstairs arguing about folding chairs with a retired Navy radar technician because a man who had once been executed by Rome had spent three days unconscious in her house repairing a relationship between her mother's mind and a consciousness older than Earth.

There were moments when Mina could still see the life she'd had before Warren as if it belonged to somebody she'd gone to school with.

She wasn't going back to it.

"I know," Warren said.

"No, I don't think you do."

His expression changed, not dramatically. Warren rarely did anything dramatically unless gravity was involved.

"I'm not promising I'll believe everything you tell me the first time," she said. "I'm definitely not promising I won't tell you when I think you're full of shit."

"I would be concerned if you did."

"And whatever you haven't told me yet, I'm not promising I'm going to like it."

"That seems statistically unlikely."

"But I'm staying for the answer."

For a moment he looked older than the face he wore. Not physically. She had no scientific vocabulary for the difference and had stopped trying to invent one every time she saw it.

"All right," he said.

That was all.

It was enough.

Inside, Denny shouted, "Mendez, tell the Messiah he still owes me twelve dollars."

Warren closed his eyes.

Mina hopped down from the tailgate. "You heard the man."

"It was nine dollars."

"Two thousand years old and stiffing retirees."

"The historical record will vindicate me."

"Apparently you control the historical record."

"I absolutely do not. Have you been listening to anything I've said?"

She went inside before he could finish objecting.
