Chapter Thirty: The Unanswered Question

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Batch 2. Warren–Mina cosmology scene. Not final prose.)
30b_the_unanswered_question.md

It started, the way most of the important conversations did, with Mina asking something she'd been carrying around for days and Warren pretending he hadn't noticed her carrying it.

"Is there a God above you."

Warren looked up from the sink, where he was washing a mug with the particular thoroughness of a man who had once watched a plague move through a city that hadn't figured out soap yet.

"That's a big first sentence for a Thursday."

"It's not Thursday."

"I know. I'm stalling." He dried the mug, set it down, leaned against the counter. "The honest answer is that I don't know. And I need you to hear how specifically I mean that, because it's not the humble thing people say when they mean they haven't thought about it. I've thought about it longer than anyone. The Eternals have been arguing about it since near the beginning. Some of us think there's an Almighty Creator behind all of it — behind us, behind the Universe, behind the whole structure. Some of us think consciousness is just what the Universe does, the way stars are what the Universe does. Some of us think there's a plan nobody's told us the purpose of." He folded the dish towel in half, then in half again. "I don't know which of us is right. I know an astonishing amount about how the Universe works. I don't know what, if anything, made the thing I was before I thought I am."

Mina sat down on the edge of his bed — the room was too small for more than one chair, and she'd stopped pretending she was a guest a while ago. "You remember becoming conscious."

"I remember the thought. The first one. I am. Everything before that — " He shook his head. "Nothing. Not darkness, not waiting, not a before. Just the thought, arriving, and then me, arriving with it. Every Eternal has the same wall at the same place. We've argued about what's on the other side of it for longer than your species has had fire mastered, and the arguing hasn't gotten us one inch closer. That's why we say the Universe instead of the Creator, most of us, most of the time. It's not disbelief. It's that I won't claim certainty I don't have. I've seen what certainty I don't have does to people."

"The mechanism without the purpose."

"That's exactly it. I know the mechanism. I don't know the purpose. I don't know why Eternals exist. I don't know what we were before the first I am. I don't know whether human and Eternal compatibility was designed or whether it's an accident we're all benefiting from. I don't know whether Terra is the only place the restoration happens. I don't know whether there are other worlds doing the same work. I don't know whether every fractured Soul will eventually recover, or whether the supply ends, or what happens afterward, or what larger plan any of this serves." He counted them off on his fingers without looking at his hand, the way a man counts things he's counted ten thousand times before. "If Terra is the only place — if this is it, this one world, all of us doing this work here — then what we're doing could run for a trillion years. Sit with that number for a minute. Not as poetry. As bookkeeping."

Mina sat with it. The room was quiet except for Denny's radio downstairs, losing a station and finding it again.

"You know the mechanism of everything," she said finally, "and you don't know what any of it is for."

"Welcome to my entire existence."

"That's — " She stopped. "That's either the loneliest thing I've ever heard or the most honest."

"It's both. It's usually both."

She pulled her knees up, wrapped her arms around them — a posture she'd had since childhood, one of the few things about her that had never learned to be professional. "The sequence, then. I am, you are, we are, Harmony. You said it took you a long time to understand you are. What did you have to learn?"

Warren was quiet long enough that she thought he might not answer.

"That knowing more than another consciousness doesn't give you ownership over it," he said finally. "That being correct doesn't entitle you to force another consciousness into correctness. I understood it as a principle very early. As a practice — " He shook his head. "An enormous amount of time. Longer than I want to tell you, because the number makes me sound stupid, and I was. Not stupid the way you're thinking. Stupid the way a person is stupid who keeps being right about the mechanism and keeps using the rightness as a license. You encounter another genuine I am, and the temptation is to treat it as a problem you've already solved. It took me longer than everything else I've ever learned to understand that the whole point of you are is that the other consciousness gets to surprise you. If I could calculate exactly what you'd choose, you are would just be I already know you. And that isn't recognition. That's possession wearing recognition's clothes."

"Is that why you stopped," Mina said. "Intervening. Steering."

"That's part of why the stopping stuck, once I managed it. The loop was the rest." He sat down on the floor, back against the wall, because the room had run out of furniture and neither of them cared. "Humanity and Eternals together are the only thing I know of that can't be predicted. Not people — I can predict people depressingly well, probabilities, patterns, give me enough data and I'll tell you what a crowd does. But a genuine I am isn't a variable you can reduce. Put two of them together, let them actually meet each other — I am, you are — and something new can happen that wasn't in either of them before. Put a human and an Eternal together, let them reach we are, Harmony, and it's — " He stopped. "Other consciousnesses can surprise me. That's the whole thing. That's the thing I spent eons trying to manage out of existence, and it's the thing that turned out to be the point."

Mina thought about that for a while. Then, because she was Mina, she changed direction without signaling.

"The sacrifices."

"What about them."

"The whole system. The blood, the altars, the — the pleasing aroma thing. If the Creator doesn't demand blood sacrifice, what was all that?"

Warren's mouth did something complicated. "Harm reduction."

"I'm sorry?"

"They were already killing each other, Mina. Human sacrifice, war captives, children — the cultures I was dealing with had sacrifice the way your century has traffic. It was just what you did. I couldn't talk them out of the impulse in a generation, so I redirected it. If they were going to offer something, better a goat than a neighbor." He picked up the mug he'd just dried and turned it over in his hands. "Besides, the whole sacrifice thing was to try to get them to stop killing each other. Plus I couldn't eat."

Mina stared at him.

"You couldn't eat."

"That was rather the problem."

"You're telling me the pleasing aroma of the burnt offering — "

"Smelled wonderful. I mean it. Extraordinary. And I had no mouth." He looked at her, completely serious, which made it worse. "You have no idea what it's like to spend centuries smelling roast lamb you can't taste. It builds character. Or resentment. The historical record is unclear on which."

She laughed so hard she had to put her head on her knees. When she could breathe again: "That's the most Warren explanation of Leviticus anyone has ever produced."

"Over time I tried to plant the actual idea — that the whole transactional logic was backwards. In all I'd ever known of the Universe, I'd never found anything that required blood. By the time I had a life and a mouth and could teach it properly, ending that logic was half the work. Which is why there's a particular irony in what came later."

"What came later."

"People explaining my death as the ultimate required sacrifice." His voice didn't change, but the humor went out of it like air out of a room. "It wasn't a payment, Mina. Nothing in anything I'd ever known demanded it. I knew it might kill me and I went anyway, because there were things I needed to teach and do that couldn't be done from a safe distance. And afterward, people who loved me reached for the only vocabulary they had — sacrifice, offering, blood that means something — and built a theology out of it. I don't blame them. They were doing what humans have always done with something real: explaining it with whatever they had."

The room was quiet again. Mina unfolded herself, stood up, went to the window. The street below was empty.

"One more," she said. "The pyramids. The — all of it. Mathematics, architecture, all the things humans built that shouldn't have been possible yet. Was that you? Were Eternals just — handing out blueprints?"

"No." Flat. Immediate.

"Because that would be the ancient-aliens thing."

"That would be the ancient-aliens thing, and the ancient-aliens thing is lazy." Warren stood up, came to stand beside her at the window, not touching her. "Humans built them."

"Humans with Eternals."

"Yes."

"That's a fairly substantial footnote."

"Only if you insist we're separate things." He looked out at the dark street. "Nearly everything your species has ever made — the mathematics, the astronomy, the buildings, the music, all of it — was made by humans joined to Eternals, neither side understanding the partnership. A human gets an intuition out of nowhere in the middle of the night. An Eternal contributes a pattern it remembers without knowing it's contributing. Culture and education and resonance and moments of real Harmony, all braided together, and out comes something neither I am would have made alone. Don't ask me where the human part ends and the Eternal part begins. The question is malformed. We are makes things."

Mina nodded slowly. "Okay. Then the gods. The ones people worshipped. You said most of them left when they were healed."

"Most did."

"Most?"

"Yes."

"What happened to the others?"

Warren suddenly became intensely interested in whether the mug he'd dried was truly dry, picked it up, turned it over, held it to the light.

"Warren."

"Some Eternals liked being worshipped," he said to the mug. "The authority. The obedience. The offerings, the monuments, the armies, the — the psychological leverage of having people believe you made the weather. Some of them abused it badly. And the One got angry about that, in a way that — " He set the mug down. "Ancient stories about gods fighting gods. Divine orders collapsing overnight. Cults waking up one morning and their god is just — gone. You can read those however you like."

"Did you — "

"I am going to make tea," Warren said. "Do you want tea."

"I want an answer."

"You want tea. You just don't know it yet."

She watched him fill the kettle, and decided, for tonight, to let him have the evasion. There were some doors he opened partway and held shut, and she'd learned which ones to push.

"One more," she said. "A small one. The Nazca lines. The big drawings in the desert. You were there. What were they for?"

"I don't know."

"You were there."

"Being somewhere doesn't make you omniscient." He measured tea into the pot with the care of a pharmacist. "I asked, at the time. A farmer's wife tried to explain it to me for most of an afternoon, and I walked away understanding less than when I'd arrived. Some things you just have to let be mysterious. It bothers you more than it bothers me. I've had practice."

"And the Three Kingdoms? The famous generals, the big battles — "

"Armies kept commandeering my horses." He poured the water. "Three different armies, two years, same road. I had opinions about all of them and none of the opinions were about strategy. I remember a cook in Chengdu who could do things with ginger that I still think about. The generals — " He shrugged. "Mina, the world is enormous. Even to me. Especially to me. Everyone wants my memory to work like a history book with the good parts highlighted. It works like a life. I remember the cook."

She took the tea he handed her. It was too hot. She held it anyway, for the warmth in her hands.

"One more," she said. "The dark."

Warren looked up.

"In the we-are story. You said — somewhere in the middle of all that reaching and finding, you started to feel something else. Weight, out in the dark. Not another consciousness. You said it was a whole different story." She held his gaze. "I'm calling in the story."

He was quiet for a moment. Then he set the mug down, and she knew, from the set of his shoulders, that this one wasn't an evasion.

"It was the first time any of us felt the size of the room," he said. "Not a being. Not a mind. Just — weight. Patience. The sense, all at once, that we were inside something so much larger than us that our whole growing crowd of separate, certain I ams was a small noise in a large house. We'd been so busy learning each other's names we hadn't noticed the house."

"And?"

"And nothing. That's the story." He picked the mug back up. "We never found out what it was. Some of us decided it was the Creator, watching. Some of us decided it was just the Universe being the Universe — big enough to have a gravity of its own. I go back and forth, depending on the century." A brief smile. "It's the oldest version I have of the unanswered question. Before I ever thought to ask what I was before I am, I was already standing in the dark feeling noticed by something that never introduced itself."

Mina was quiet.

"That's not a satisfying ending," she said.

"No," Warren said. "It's the truest one I have."

"So the unanswered question stays unanswered."

"The unanswered question stays unanswered," Warren said. "I can tell you how everything works, and I can't tell you what any of it's for, or what I was before I woke up, or whether anyone's running the whole thing. Anyone who tells you otherwise is selling something." He sat back down on the floor. "But I can tell you this much: whatever it is, it's still going. A trillion years of it, maybe. And you're part of it now — not as a footnote. As an I am. That's not nothing. That's the only thing I'm sure is something."

Mina looked at him over the rim of her mug, and didn't say anything, because some sentences were doors too, and this one she wanted to leave open.

Chapter Thirty-One: Mina Kisses Him First

31_mia_kisses_him_first.md

(WORKING DRAFT — Creator review required before public synchronization.)

Mina kissed him first.

This was important later, when she tried to reconstruct the sequence of events as though sequence could make any of it less insane.

There had been no declaration.

No candlelight. No meaningful rain. No music except something low and forgettable coming from Denny's radio downstairs, occasionally losing the station when someone moved through the hardware store.

Warren had been standing at the little kitchen counter with his back half-turned to her, telling her that the reason people in the first century did not routinely drink wine because the water was universally poisonous was considerably more complicated than the internet seemed willing to tolerate.

Mina had been listening.

Mostly.

Then he said her name.

"Mina."

Just that.

He had noticed she wasn't listening.

She looked at him.

And kissed him.

There was enough time between deciding and doing for her to know she could stop.

She didn't.

One step. Her hand against his chest. His expression changing too late for either of them to pretend he hadn't understood what was happening.

Then her mouth was on his.

Warren went perfectly still.

Not recoiling.

Not returning it.

Still.

Mina's entire nervous system caught fire and immediately filed a complaint.

She pulled back.

"Right."

"Mina—"

"No, that's fine."

"It isn't."

"It is spectacularly fine."

She took one step backward and collided with the edge of his table.

Warren reached for her automatically.

His hands stopped before touching her.

That somehow made it worse.

For weeks—months, if she was going to be honest in a way she had no intention of being—her body had been conducting a quiet mutiny against her judgment.

It had begun stupidly.

Not with his face.

Not with some grand moment of masculine competence.

His forearms.

He had rolled his sleeves up to wash dishes at Perla's house.

That was all.

Mina, fifty-one years old, a clinical geneticist who had spent most of her adult life being entirely capable of existing around attractive men without behaving like she'd just discovered testosterone, had looked at Warren's forearms and experienced a warm, unmistakable tightening low in her body.

Her first coherent thought had been:

Oh, absolutely not.

She had subsequently become extremely interested in dishes.

After that came other betrayals.

The shape of his shoulders under an old shirt. His hands. The way his voice dropped when he was tired. Once, disastrously, the line of his back when he bent to pick up something Denny had dropped.

She had dealt with each incident in the mature and scientifically rigorous manner of refusing to think about it ever again.

Warren, infuriatingly, had helped.

He had never flirted.

Never caught her looking and smiled as though he'd won something.

Never found a reason to touch her.

If anything, as her control became more deliberate, his became nearly surgical.

Which meant either he hadn't noticed at all—

unlikely—

or he had noticed everything and had chosen to give her absolutely nothing to fight against.

Until she kissed him.

And he stood there.

"Mina."

"Please stop saying my name like that."

"Like what?"

"Like you're about to explain my own behavior to me."

A corner of his mouth moved.

"Don't."

It vanished.

That was worse too.

She folded her arms. "I misread something."

"No."

The answer came immediately.

She stared at him.

"No?"

"No."

"Then you have a very strange method of communicating enthusiasm."

"I was trying not to make a decision for you."

"I kissed you."

"I noticed."

"Did you?"

His eyes closed briefly.

When they opened again, something had changed.

Not much.

That was the frightening part.

For all the impossible things she had watched this man do, Warren's most unnerving ability might have been the degree to which he could contain himself.

Now she could see the containment.

Not absence.

Containment.

"Oh," Mina said.

"Yes."

The room seemed considerably smaller.

She uncrossed her arms.

Warren did not move.

"You could have mentioned that."

"I've been trying very hard not to."

"Why?"

His gaze held hers.

"You know why."

She did.

Mary, though she did not yet know all of Mary.

The Soul inside her, though she understood only pieces.

A relationship older than humanity that Warren could remember and Mina could not.

Every reason in the world for a man with his history to be careful about wanting a woman whose interior life contained something he had once loved.

"Then look at me," she said.

"I am."

"No."

She stepped closer.

"Warren. Look at me."

His breathing changed.

She had never heard that before.

It was a tiny thing, almost nothing, and because it was Warren it felt enormous.

"Who are you looking at?"

He understood the question.

"Mina Mendez."

"Not her."

"No."

"Not whoever is in here."

His eyes did not leave hers.

"Mina Mendez."

Something inside her unclenched.

"Good."

She kissed him again.

This time Warren kissed her back.

There was nothing hesitant about it.

Restraint, Mina discovered, was not the same thing as lack of appetite.

His hand came toward her waist and stopped there, hovering absurdly a few centimeters from the fabric of her blouse.

She broke the kiss.

"Seriously?"

His forehead rested against hers.

"I'm trying."

"I can tell."

"I have had a complicated few thousand years."

"So you've mentioned."

She took his hand and put it on her waist.

Warren made a sound so quiet she felt it more than heard it.

That was when Mina realized how much of this had been hers to start.

Not because he didn't want her.

Because he did.

Enough to be afraid of wanting her incorrectly.

She kissed him again.

His hand remained exactly where she'd placed it for perhaps three seconds.

Then his fingers tightened gently through the loose fabric at her waist, and something in Mina that had spent months being disciplined, analyzed, redirected and ignored simply gave up.

She laughed against his mouth.

Warren pulled back a fraction.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You're laughing."

"I'm fifty-one and apparently making out over a hardware store."

"Technically we're above it."

"Thank you. That's much more dignified."

"I thought accuracy might help."

"It doesn't."

She kissed him again before he could answer.

Later she would remember pieces.

Her scarf ending up on the floor.

Warren picking it up automatically.

Mina taking it out of his hand and deliberately dropping it again.

His expression.

That made her laugh so hard she had to sit down.

"You've survived crucifixion," she said.

"Yes."

"Empires."

"Several."

"The Black Death."

"Unfortunately."

"But a woman dropping a scarf on purpose has defeated you."

"I don't understand the rules."

"There aren't rules."

"That's deeply irresponsible."

She pulled him down to her.

The humor didn't disappear.

It changed.

So did everything else.

For the first time, Mina became acutely aware of her own clothes through his attention: the loose layers she chose because she liked how they moved, the neckline softened by fabric and scarf, the body beneath them she had lived in for fifty-one years without needing anyone else's assessment of it.

Warren looked at her as though he had spent a very long time refusing to look.

Not greedily.

That would have been easier.

Carefully.

As if noticing her were itself an intimacy.

"You've seen me before," she whispered.

"Yes."

"Apparently not."

"No."

The honesty of it moved through her harder than flattery could have.

She touched his face.

"Now?"

"Now I'm having some difficulty."

"Good."

His laugh disappeared into her mouth.

They moved toward the bed without either of them announcing that they were moving toward the bed, which Mina later considered one of the few mercies of the evening.

Then Warren stopped.

Not physically this time.

She felt it almost simultaneously.

A pressure.

No.

Not pressure.

Recognition.

The room remained the room—the cheap lamp, the badly fitted curtain, the distant clunk of something downstairs—but another awareness opened beneath it.

For one impossible instant Mina knew Warren was there without seeing him.

Not his body.

Him.

A distinct presence.

A fact.

I am.

The thought did not arrive in words.

It was older than words.

She inhaled sharply.

Warren pulled back.

"Mina."

"What was that?"

He was pale.

That frightened her more than the sensation.

"Warren."

"We stop."

"What?"

"We stop now."

Her body objected with extraordinary clarity.

Her mind caught up a moment later.

"Why?"

"Because something is happening that you haven't consented to."

"I consented to this."

"To this." His hand touched her waist, deliberately ordinary. "Not to what comes after it."

"What comes after it?"

He looked at her for a long moment.

The wanting was still there.

So was fear.

Not fear of her.

Fear of taking something.

"There is more to Harmony than I've told you."

Mina stared at him.

"You have got to be kidding me."

"No."

"Now?"

"I agree the timing is poor."

"The timing is catastrophic."

"I know."

She sat back.

Warren gave her space immediately.

That irritated her too.

"Stop being so good at this."

"At what?"

"At making it impossible for me to be angry with you for the convenient reason."

"I can do something objectionable if it would help."

"It might."

"I think fitted sheets are an excellent invention."

Mina blinked.

"You monster."

"I've had centuries of blankets coming loose at the corners. Civilization occasionally gets something right."

She started laughing.

She didn't want to.

It happened anyway.

Warren sat beside her, close but no longer touching.

The laughter faded.

"What did I feel?"

He looked at his hands.

"You."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the beginning of one."

"And you?"

"Yes."

She waited.

His jaw tightened.

"You felt me?"

"Yes."

"How much?"

"Enough to know we were approaching something I will not let happen by accident."

The sentence landed differently than she expected.

Not rejection.

Not fear.

A boundary drawn around her.

Mina looked at him.

"You want it."

Warren was silent.

"That's not fair," she said.

"No."

"Answer."

"Yes."

The word barely existed.

She felt it anyway.

"How badly?"

"Mina."

"Fine."

She stood, retrieved her scarf from the floor, and wrapped it loosely around herself again.

Warren watched the scarf.

She caught him.

For perhaps the first time since she'd known him, Warren looked embarrassed.

Mina smiled.

"Oh."

"Don't."

"You notice the scarves."

"I notice many things."

"My walk?"

Silence.

Her smile widened.

"Warren."

"I have survived considerably more effective interrogators than you."

"That's a yes."

"I didn't say anything."

"You didn't have to."

She moved toward the door.

His eyes followed her for exactly one step before snapping upward.

Mina stopped.

Turned.

The expression on his face was so profoundly annoyed with himself that she nearly laughed again.

There it was.

Not the answer.

But finally, unmistakably, the question had become interesting.

"I'll see you tomorrow," she said.

Warren exhaled.

"Mina."

"What?"

"We need to talk before—"

"I know."

She opened the door.

Then looked back at him.

"For the record?"

He waited.

"You were right about one thing."

"Only one?"

She smiled.

"Fitted sheets."

And left him standing there, two thousand years old and completely unprepared for tomorrow.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Two: What Comes After

31a_what_comes_after.md

(WORKING DRAFT — new for structural-pass rulings. Not final prose.)

The next evening Mina came up the stairs with no takeout and no pretext, which Warren correctly identified as the more serious of the available approaches, and said, "Talk."

He set down the radio he'd been failing to fix. "Where do you want me to start."

"With the part where you stopped. The real part, not the chivalry part."

Warren was quiet for a moment. Then: "There's a recognition between Eternals. Older than language. Mine knew yours, a very long time ago, and last night, for about three seconds, they started toward each other."

Mina went very still. "Started."

"Like a door starting to open in a house you thought you knew." He wasn't looking at her. "If we hadn't stopped — not that night, maybe, not all at once, but if we kept going the way we were going — it would have kept opening. Harmony doesn't always wait to be invited, Mina. Not the beginning of it. The beginning can happen the way a river happens. And then you'd be inside something you hadn't chosen, and I would have let it happen because I wanted you, and I will not do that."

"Even if I — " She stopped. "I did choose. I chose you. Last night."

"You chose me." His voice was careful. "You didn't choose that. There's a difference, and the difference is the entire point of everything I've been trying to tell you since the diner."

She sat down on the edge of the bed. "So my own Soul is a tripwire."

"No." He came and sat beside her, not touching. "It's a recognition. The oldest thing I know. It's not a trap, Mina. But it doesn't ask permission, and that means I have to ask it twice — once for me, and once for the part of me that doesn't ask."

She looked at him. "Do you want it. The Harmony. Eventually."

"Yes."

"Say it like you mean it."

He met her eyes. "Yes."

"Okay." She leaned her shoulder against his, lightly, deliberately. "Then we do this in order. The talking, the choosing, all of it. No more doors opening by accident."

"No more doors opening by accident," he agreed.

"And Warren?"

"Mm?"

"Next time you decide to be noble, warn me first. I had a whole evening planned around being reckless."

The corner of his mouth moved. "I'll put it on the calendar."

"See that you do."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Three: A Beautiful Mind

(WORKING DRAFT — new for Batch 2. Warren–Mina intimate scene. Not final prose.)
31b_beautiful_mind.md

A few nights after the kiss — after the stopping, after the conversation neither of them had been ready for and both of them had needed — Mina came up the stairs above the hardware store carrying takeout and found Warren sitting on the floor with his back against the bed, turning a smooth stone over in his hand. A river stone. She had no idea where he'd gotten it and had stopped asking about the origins of small objects.

"You're doing the thing," she said.

"What thing."

"The thing where you're somewhere else and your hands are keeping you company."

He set the stone down. "I was thinking about languages."

"Yours."

"Mine." He looked at her. "You've been wanting to ask."

She set the takeout down unopened. "Say something. In it. In your own — the way you actually spoke. Not the English."

Warren was quiet for a moment. "You won't understand a word."

"That's the point. I want to hear it. The real one. Your accent, not the — whatever this is." She gestured vaguely at him, at the room, at the whole twentieth-century-plus apparatus of Warren. "Before all of this."

He rubbed the back of his neck, a gesture so ordinary it almost hid how un-ordinary the request was. "My dialect — if I'm careful about what it actually sounded like, and I'm reconstructing from memory, so take the modesty as load-bearing — it was something like the French of the Semitic languages of its place and time."

"The French."

"Softer than the neighbors thought it should be. Less — " He searched for the word. "People expect it to be all throat and gravel. It wasn't, not where I was from. It was more patient than that. More musical."

"Show me."

Warren looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, once, and spoke.

It wasn't long — a single sentence, low, unhurried. The syllables rolled rather than struck, vowels round and open, the consonants softer than Mina had expected, landing the way water lands on stone rather than the way she'd imagined ancient languages landing, all edges. There was a rhythm to it that felt like waiting without impatience. Like someone in no hurry to be finished talking, because the talking itself was the point.

She'd braced for something harsh. What she heard was almost gentle.

"What did you say."

He smiled, a little crooked. "You have a beautiful mind."

Mina felt her throat do something she hadn't authorized.

"That's — you could have said anything. You could have said the sky is blue in Aramaic."

"I know what I said."

"Say it again."

He did. Slower this time. She closed her eyes to hear it better, and the second time through she caught the shape of it — the way it rose slightly in the middle and settled at the end, the way a hand settles on a shoulder.

"You don't sound like I expected," she said quietly.

"What did you expect."

"Something harder. Older-sounding, I guess. More — " She opened her eyes. "You sound like someone who was kind."

Warren looked away, toward the window, and for once she didn't read it as evasion. "I wasn't always. You know that."

"I know what you've told me."

"The accent doesn't absolve anything."

"No," she said. "But it's yours. The real one. Underneath the — " She gestured again at the whole apparatus. "I wanted to hear the underneath."

He picked the river stone back up. Turned it over once.

"There's something I should tell you about that sentence," he said.

"What."

"I've been waiting a very long time to say it to someone and mean it the way I mean it." He looked at her. "Don't make a big thing out of that. I'm serious. If you make a big thing out of it I'll pretend I was talking about the takeout."

Mina opened the takeout instead. The smell of it filled the small room — garlic, ginger, something fried — and Warren, who could eat now and did, with visible enjoyment, reached for the container with the particular focus of a man who had spent centuries smelling food he couldn't taste and had never once gotten over the novelty of the reversal.

She watched him eat, and listened to the echo of the sentence in her head, in a language she'd never learn and a voice she was only beginning to know, and thought that there were some things you didn't need to translate to understand.

You have a beautiful mind.

She kept it where he'd put it, and didn't make a big thing out of it, and ate her dinner, and stayed.

Memory-Story Working draft

Memory-Story: The Choice at the Fire (Warren/Yeshua, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — revised Author/Archivist pass 2026-09-19. Runway toward Jerusalem expanded; Miriam agency preserved. Not final prose.)
memory_yeshua_miriam_the_choice.md

"People say I went to Jerusalem to die," Warren said.

Mina looked up from the notebook she had mostly stopped pretending she needed. "Did you?"

"No."

The answer came so quickly she waited for the rest.

"I went to Jerusalem knowing I might die. Those are not the same thing, and people have spent two thousand years making them the same thing because the cleaner version fits theology better."

"That's a fairly significant distinction."

"It was to me."

*

By then, travel had become slower because people knew my face.

Not everywhere. That's another thing the stories make larger after the fact. There was no empire-wide recognition, no portrait nailed up in every village, no Roman clerk in Caesarea maintaining a file labeled Troublesome Carpenter Who May Be God. In most places I was still simply a Jewish man traveling with an inconvenient number of other Jewish people.

But in the places we'd been before, recognition changed the road.

A meal became a gathering. A request for water became three questions about the kingdom of God and one request to look at somebody's bad knee. A night's shelter became six additional people asleep against the wall because somebody's cousin had heard I was there.

Miriam was better at saying no than I was.

"You cannot heal everyone between here and Jerusalem."

The woman in front of me had walked half a day with her son.

"I can heal this one."

"You said that about the last one."

"The last one couldn't see."

"Yes. I noticed."

The boy was trying very hard not to laugh.

Miriam caught him and pointed at him. "Do not encourage him."

His mother looked between us, uncertain whether she was allowed to find any of this funny.

"You are," I told her.

Miriam sighed. "This is why we're always late."

We were always late.

Not because I didn't understand time. I understood time considerably better than anyone around me, which unfortunately did nothing to make me good at schedules.

The roads toward Jerusalem thickened as the festival approached. Families traveled together where they could. People carried food, bedding, children, arguments, and expectations. Every mile closer made the journey feel less like a road and more like a river collecting tributaries.

Passover did that. Jerusalem drew people toward itself.

It also made Roman authority nervous.

You didn't need divine insight to understand why. A festival centered on liberation from foreign domination was an uncomfortable season for the foreign power doing the dominating.

Miriam understood the danger without needing me to explain it.

That evening we'd stopped outside a village where every available room had already been claimed. We slept outdoors, which was neither unusual nor especially romantic. The ground was hard. Someone nearby snored with genuine commitment. A cooking pot had cracked. We had less bread than Miriam believed we had because two of the men had counted the same bundle twice.

She discovered this while I was attempting to repair a sandal strap by firelight.

"Tell me again," she said, "why men who can remember twelve tribes cannot remember whether they've counted a loaf."

"I've never claimed miracles in accounting."

"You've never claimed miracles at all. Other people do that for you."

"Very considerate of them."

She sat across from me and took the sandal out of my hands.

"You're making it worse."

"I repaired yokes for a living."

"This is leather."

"I know what leather is."

"Apparently not in the dark."

She fixed the strap herself.

It was later, after most of the others had settled and the road had finally gone quiet, that she said, "They're going to kill you."

Not a question.

I looked at her.

"Not eventually," she said. "Soon. You've felt it too. Don't tell me you haven't."

"I've felt it."

"Then why are we still walking toward the city instead of away from it?"

There are questions you answer badly because you don't know the answer.

There are others you answer slowly because you do.

"Because running doesn't solve what is happening."

"That's not an answer."

"No."

"Try again."

So I did.

"Because people are already carrying stories about me into Jerusalem. Because some of those stories are true, some are wrong, and some are dangerous in ways the people repeating them don't understand. Because I've spent years telling people that God is not what power has taught them to imagine, and that a kingdom does not have to look like Caesar's to be real. And now we're approaching the place where religion, money, crowds and Roman authority all sit on top of one another."

"And you think walking directly into that is wise."

"No."

That stopped her.

I smiled despite myself. "You asked why I'm doing it. You didn't ask whether it was wise."

"Good. For a moment I was worried perfect enlightenment had finally made you stupid."

"I have never claimed perfect enlightenment."

"Other people do that for you."

"Very considerate of them."

She handed the sandal back.

The strap held.

I turned it over in my hands.

"There are people in that city I still need to speak to," I said. "And there are things I have said publicly that become lies if I disappear the moment saying them becomes dangerous."

"That sounds better than dying on purpose."

"I am not dying on purpose."

"Good."

"I may be choosing not to prevent it."

Her face changed.

That was the sentence neither of us had wanted.

Miriam knew by then that there were things I could do that other people could not. She didn't know everything. Nobody did. But she'd seen enough to understand that ordinary danger and danger to me were not necessarily the same thing.

"Can they kill you?" she asked.

"I don't know."

It was the truth.

The Eternal within me knew matter with a depth no human language around us could describe. I had healed wounds that should have remained wounds. I had changed things by understanding them so completely that the distinction between thought and substance became less absolute than human beings experienced it.

None of that told me what would happen if Rome nailed my body to wood and waited.

Perfect Harmony had never been tested that way.

Miriam stared at me for a long time.

"You genuinely don't know."

"No."

"And you're going anyway."

"Yes."

She fed a twig into the fire with far more attention than the twig deserved.

I waited for her to tell me I was a fool.

She had before.

"I'm not going to try to talk you out of it," she said.

That frightened me more than if she had shouted.

"I want you to understand that is a choice I'm making. Not a failure of nerve. I've watched you decide things before, Yeshua. I've learned the difference between a decision that still has room in it and one that doesn't."

"This one has some room."

"Does it have enough room for you to turn north tomorrow?"

I didn't answer.

"Then it doesn't."

Someone shifted in sleep behind us.

The fire collapsed inward.

Miriam looked toward the dark road south.

"Then I'm coming as far as they let me."

"No."

She turned back.

It was a mistake. I knew it as soon as I said it.

"No?"

"Miriam—"

"You've just explained that you're walking into a city where you may be arrested by Rome, you don't know whether you can survive what they do to you, and your conclusion is that the part of this situation requiring reconsideration is where I walk?"

"I don't want you caught in it."

"Neither do I."

"Then—"

"But you don't get to yoke me with my own fear."

I stopped.

She was angry now. Quietly, which was usually worse.

"I'm not coming to save you," she said. "I'm not foolish enough to think I can. I'm coming because I have spent years in this life too. I carried the money. I found rooms. I bought food. I argued with men who thought listening to you somehow made them qualified to negotiate the price of grain. I watched people arrive sick and leave well. I watched crowds turn one sentence you said into five sentences you didn't. Whatever happens in Jerusalem is not only happening to you."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Yes."

She studied me.

I made myself leave the next sentence unsaid.

I can bear losing myself. I cannot bear losing you.

It would have been true.

It would also have been a way of putting the weight of my fear onto her choice.

So I didn't say it.

Miriam looked back toward the road.

"I'm coming so that whatever happens, you don't do it without anyone there who knew you before people decided what you meant."

I couldn't speak for a moment.

"That's not nothing," she said. "Don't you dare let anyone shrink it down to nothing afterward."

*

Warren stopped.

Mina had become accustomed to the strange elasticity of his stories. Sometimes he could describe a room from two thousand years ago down to the smell of the lamp oil and then lose the name of the man who owned it. Sometimes he remembered a sentence exactly and admitted he had no idea whether it had been spoken on a Tuesday or six months later.

This silence was different.

"She was there," he said.

Mina waited.

"At the end. Closer than almost anyone."

The word end bothered her.

Not because she didn't know what story they were approaching.

Because Warren did not look like a man approaching the part where he died.

He looked like a man approaching the part where someone else had stayed to watch.

"I haven't told you that part yet," he said. "What the end actually was. What came after it."

"The crucifixion."

"Yes."

"And the resurrection."

His mouth tightened slightly.

"What?"

"Later."

"Warren."

"Mina."

"You can't object to the word and then refuse to tell me why."

"I can. I've had two thousand years of practice."

She gave him a look.

He almost smiled.

Then it was gone.

"I will tell you," he said. "But when I do, I need to tell you what happened before I tell you what people eventually called it."

Mina closed the notebook.

"Okay."

Warren looked surprised.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You were expecting an argument."

"I've learned not to become emotionally dependent on them."

"That's growth."

"Terrifying, isn't it?"

That got the smallest laugh out of him.

Then he looked away.

"She kept the promise," he said. "That's the part I can give you tonight."

Mina didn't ask which promise.

She knew.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Four

(WORKING DRAFT — Voss arc climax, part 1 of 3. Not final prose.)
32_vosss_ultimatum.md

Voss came to Corbin's Landing on a Tuesday, in a car that cost more than most of the houses on the street he parked it on, and asked Denny, politely, for ten minutes with Warren and Dr. Mendez.

Denny looked at him for a long moment, the way he looked at anyone who wanted something from his town, and said, "Back room. After close. You get the ten minutes because the lady's already said yes to hearing you, and after that you get gone."

The back room was where the Thursday game happened — folding table, mismatched chairs, the workbench of half-fixed radios along one wall, the smell of machine oil and old coffee that never entirely left. Warren sat with his back to the workbench. Mina took the chair nearest the door, which Denny noticed and approved of without saying so. Denny himself didn't sit. He leaned against the doorframe with his arms crossed, which everyone in the room understood to be a position rather than a posture.

Voss set a folder on the table. He didn't open it.

"I'll start with what I know, so we don't waste your ten minutes on me proving I did the homework." He looked at Mina. "Your mother's dementia medication — a refill pattern going back five years — stopped completely, eleven weeks ago. No death certificate. No facility transfer. No new prescription. In my experience, Dr. Mendez, there are three explanations for a pattern that stops like that, and two of them generate paperwork."

Mina said nothing.

"The third explanation," Voss said, "is that she got better in a way that doesn't happen. And I find I'm very interested in things that don't happen, because my company has spent six years failing to make them happen on purpose."

"You bought her pharmacy records," Mina said. "That's not research. That's a crime with a receipt."

"I obtained information through channels my lawyers would describe, I suspect, as aggressive." He didn't flinch from it. "I'm not going to insult you by calling it clean. I'm going to tell you why I did it anyway, and then I'm going to make you an offer, and I want you to hear the offer before you decide what kind of man I am, because the kind of man I am is the kind who's willing to be the villain in your version of this if it gets me the thing that actually matters."

"Which is?"

"A controlled study. Private. Consented — real consent, documented, with oversight I'd let you choose, Dr. Mendez, since you're the only person in this room whose ethics I actually trust. A small number of samples, a small number of questions, nobody's name on anything publishable without Warren's sign-off." He turned to Warren. "I'm not asking to own you. I'm asking for a door. A door I can walk through slowly, with your permission, instead of the one that's going to get kicked in eventually by someone with fewer scruples and better funding than me."

"And if the door stays closed," Warren said.

Voss was quiet for a moment. When he spoke again, the pitch was gone out of his voice, and what was left was plainer and harder to dismiss.

"Then I do what I should have done nine days ago, which is take what I have to people who don't need your permission. The prescription pattern. The timeline. A seventy-some-year-old woman with advanced dementia who suddenly doesn't need her medication anymore, living in the same town as a man whose trauma labs don't add up. I don't have to prove the connection, Warren. I just have to say it out loud, on the record, where reporters and competitors and every federal health agency with an acronym can hear it. Your mother becomes the most studied patient in America, Dr. Mendez. Not because I want that. Because I can't un-know what I know, and neither, eventually, will anyone else."

The room was very quiet. Somewhere out front, the store's old cooler hummed.

"That's not an offer," Mina said. "That's a hostage negotiation where the hostage is my mother."

"It's both," Voss said. "I've stopped pretending it isn't both. Seventy-two hours. That's how long I'm willing to hold the alternative."

He stood, buttoned his coat, and looked at Warren — really looked at him, for the first time, the way a man looks at something he's spent months trying to see clearly.

"You know what the worst part is?" Warren said, quietly. "I believe you. About the hundred thousand families. I believe that's real to you, not just a story you tell donors."

"Thank you."

"But I keep coming back to something, and I'd like you to sit with it, since you've got a long drive back." Warren didn't raise his voice. He never needed to, for the things that landed hardest. "You told Mina you'd done things you weren't proud of. Men don't say that about a dead daughter unless the thing they're not proud of happened while she was still alive to watch it." He let that sit. "She asked you to stop. Didn't she. Not slow down. Stop."

Voss was very still. Then, very quietly: "Eight months. Her last eight lucid months. She said watching me refuse to accept it was killing her faster than the disease."

Warren nodded, once. "You've told yourself for six years that was dedication. I've had two thousand years to watch men tell themselves that, and I want you to consider the possibility — just consider it — that the last thing Ana asked you for was the one thing you refused to give her, and everything since has been you trying to pay off a debt she never asked you to owe."

Voss looked at him for a long moment — the donor-room face gone, something unguarded and tired underneath it — and then put the face back on, the way a man picks up a coat.

"Seventy-two hours," he said, and left.

Denny waited until the car was gone before he said, from the doorway, "Well. He's not stupid."

"No," Warren said. "That's the problem. He never has been."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Five

(WORKING DRAFT — Voss arc climax, part 2 of 3. Not final prose.)
33_warren_stays.md

The bag was by the door when Mina came up the stairs.

It wasn't a large bag. That was the thing she'd remember later, how practiced it was — one bag, packed tight, nothing sentimental in it, the kit of a man who had done this enough times to have opinions about which socks lasted longest. Two thousand years of leaving, distilled into a duffel.

"How long," she said, "have you been packed?"

Warren looked up from the chair — the one chair — where he'd been sitting doing nothing, which for him was usually a sign of doing several difficult things at once. "Three days."

"Since before he came."

"Since the prescription stopped and I did the arithmetic on how long it would take a man like Voss to notice." He rubbed his face. "It's the pattern, Mina. Itch becomes attention, attention becomes paper, paper becomes someone who doesn't ask politely. I've left towns for less. I've left countries for less."

"And this time?"

"This time there's a woman downstairs who makes soup when she's worried and a retired radar tech who'd notice I was gone before my mail stopped, and you." He said it plainly, the way he said most true things, like they were heavier than they sounded. "I've never been good at the part where somebody remains behind. It turns out I'm worse at being the somebody who leaves."

She sat on the floor across from him — her spot, by now, though neither of them had ever formally assigned it — and didn't say anything for a while.

"The caseworker comes Thursday," she said finally. "Alice's plan. We do boring."

"We do boring." He almost smiled. "I've had considerable practice at boring. It's one of my better centuries."

---

The caseworker was a woman named Calloway who had done this four hundred times and had the manner of someone who intended to do it four hundred more before lunch. She looked at the apartment — the one chair, the bed frame, the floor Mina had sat on enough times to know its particular cold spots — and asked her questions, and Warren answered them the way Alice had coached him: directly, briefly, with the mild confusion of a man who genuinely couldn't understand why anyone had been concerned.

Denny came up halfway through with a plate of sandwiches he hadn't been asked to bring, vouched for the tenancy ("eleven years, never missed rent, fixes his own plumbing"), and left before anyone could ask him anything complicated.

Calloway closed her folder. "I don't see anything here that concerns me, Mr. Locke. I'll note the visit and close the inquiry."

After she left, Denny said, "Eleven years," and Warren said, "It was seventeen last time," and Denny said, "It's a living number," and that was that.

---

Mina told Perla that night, because not telling her would have been another version of what Voss was doing — deciding things about a person's information without the person in the room.

Perla listened the way she'd listened to a thousand parent conferences, hands folded on the table, and then said, "He can look at my records all he wants. He can't have me."

"What does that mean, Mama?"

"It means I'm not ashamed I got better, and I'm not going to act like I am." She reached for Mina's hand, missed a little, found it. "But I'm not going to be the reason that man gets taken apart either. You don't trade Warren for me. I didn't raise you to make trades with men like that."

Mina nodded, her throat tight. "If you tell your doctor — "

"I'll tell my doctor what I choose to tell him, when I choose." The old classroom voice, the one that had ended discussions for thirty years. "It's my body, anak. It got better. That's not a secret and it's not a specimen."

Mina didn't say that "my body got better" was exactly the sentence that would start the paper trail all over again. Some arguments you lose by winning, and she knew this was one of them.

---

That night Mina found him on the stairs above the hardware store, the bag gone from by the door — not hidden, just unpacked, back in the closet where it lived when he wasn't measuring his life in exits.

"You stayed," she said.

"I stayed." He looked down at the street, where the town was doing its ordinary evening things, unaware that anything had almost happened. "Ask me in a hundred years if it was smart."

"I'll put it on the calendar."

Below them, the store's sign creaked faintly in the wind off the water. VASKO HARDWARE — EST. 1962, touched up badly, more than once. Somebody's whole life, holding.

"Seventy-two hours," Mina said.

"I know."

"What do we do?"

He was quiet for a long moment. Then: "We wait. Not because waiting is a strategy. Because running used to be, and I'm done with it."

She nodded, and they sat on the stairs a while longer, watching nothing in particular happen in the town Warren had decided, finally, to remain inside of.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Six

(WORKING DRAFT — Voss arc climax, part 3 of 3. Not final prose.)
34_the_overreach_surfaces.md

Priyanka had worked for Julian Voss for four years, and in that time she had watched him do exactly two things he was ashamed of.

The first had been the night Ana died, when he'd sat in the hospital hallway and asked the attending, very quietly, whether money could get her into a trial that had already closed enrollment — not as a threat, not as a bribe, just as a question from a man who had run out of every other kind of question.

The second was the pharmacy report. She'd watched him read it at midnight, watched the hope move across his face like weather, watched him tell himself the ends were worth the means. She'd typed the wire confirmation to the records broker herself, because he'd asked her to, and she'd told herself that typing wasn't the same as deciding.

It was the drive back from Corbin's Landing that finished it. He hadn't said a word the whole way — just sat in the back seat looking out at the water, checking his phone twice the way he checked it when he was waiting to give an instruction he hadn't quite decided to give. She'd watched him set real deadlines before. They came with lawyers and paperwork and calendar invites. This one had come with nothing but his voice, which meant it was personal, which meant there was no version of it where he didn't follow through.

She didn't go to a reporter. That would have been a story, and she'd had enough of stories. She went to Halcyon's outside counsel — a woman named Feld who had represented the company since before Ana got sick, who had told Voss, on the record, twice, not to touch gray-market data — and she put the broker's invoice, the wire confirmation, and her own contemporaneous notes on Feld's desk, and said, "I typed it. I'm telling you because I typed it."

Feld read everything twice, the way Voss read everything twice, and then she called the board chair.

---

Voss's seventy-two hours ran out on a Friday night. Nobody noticed, because by Wednesday morning Halcyon's outside counsel had the broker's invoice, and a countdown only matters when the man holding it still has a company to hold it with.

It took eleven days after that. That was the thing nobody wrote about afterward — how undramatic it was, from the outside. No raid. No perp walk. A biotech CEO "stepping back to address personal matters." A compliance review. A quiet settlement with a pharmacy benefits manager that never used the word "broker." The company's research program "pivoted to focus on its core pipeline."

Inside Halcyon, it was simpler than that: whatever a court would eventually have called the pharmacy purchase, no board could keep a founder whose own lawyers had twice told him, on the record, not to touch gray-market data — and no compliance officer could explain away the retaliatory APS report Voss's people had placed from a prepaid phone — the one that had sent Calloway to Warren's door — sitting in the same file, followed by a very expensive law firm's very quiet admission that their client had "acted on incomplete information." The exposure was the verdict; the law could take its time. The caseworker's closed file stayed closed. Nobody with an acronym came to Corbin's Landing.

Warren's paper trail stayed thin, which was, finally, the whole point. There was nothing to find because the people most motivated to find something had just demonstrated, on the record, that they couldn't be trusted with what they'd already found.

---

The fragments went where fragments go. A Seattle business reporter wrote twelve hundred words about Halcyon's "governance concerns" that nobody outside the industry read. A dementia-caregiver forum spent three weeks arguing about an anonymized anecdote somebody's cousin's nurse had mentioned — a woman in a coastal town, medications stopped, doctors baffled — before the thread died the way threads do. A true-crime podcaster did an episode titled "The Man With No Past" that got the town's name wrong.

Nothing provable. Nothing that held still. The world got its mystery, and argued about it, and moved on.

---

Voss kept the photograph. That was the part Mina heard about, months later, from Feld's office manager's sister's book club, the way you hear everything in the end — that he'd kept the one of Ana laughing with the knocked-over water glass, that he'd taken it home when they cleared his office, that he hadn't started another company.

She thought about calling him, once. She didn't. There are some debts you can't collect on behalf of someone else, and some apologies that would only be for the person making them.

The last thing she ever heard about Julian Voss was that he'd started showing up, on Sundays, at the cemetery where his daughter was buried — not with flowers, not with anything to fix, just sitting there. Listening, maybe, for the first time in six years, to what she'd actually spent her last eight months trying to tell him.

Stop.

Part Four — Harmony
Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Seven: The Quiet Day

35_the_quiet_day.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

The morning after everything got quiet — three weeks after the countdown ran out — Mina woke up in Warren's bed — she'd stopped pretending the floor patch wasn't hers, and sometime after that she'd stopped pretending she never slept over — and for the first few seconds she couldn't figure out what was wrong with the light.

Then she figured it out. There was nothing wrong with it. It was just morning. Nobody was coming. Nobody was filing anything, or calling anyone, or parking across the street trying to look like they weren't.

She lay there until the coffee smell got too good to ignore.

Her phone buzzed where she'd left it on the floor — her daughter, sending a photo of something ordinary from her ordinary, busy, faraway life. Mina typed back love you, looks great and set the phone down, and let the small familiar ache sit next to the coffee smell. It was part of the morning too.

Warren was at the table with a mug and a book that had lost its cover sometime during the Clinton administration, and he looked up the way he did now — like her being there was the most ordinary thing in the world, which was maybe the strangest part of all of it.

"You're staring," he said.

"I'm appreciating the peace."

"Appreciate it from the kitchen. The coffee's not going to pour itself."

"You're not going to pour it?"

"I'm reading."

She got up and poured it herself, and he watched her do it, and neither of them said anything about the fact that this — this stupid small negotiation about coffee — was the thing they'd been fighting for.

---

Vasko Hardware was already open when they came down the back stairs. It was a Saturday, which meant the store opened late and Denny took his time about it, and he was in the back room with the door open, swearing at a toaster.

Not his toaster. Mrs. Henley's toaster. She'd brought it in Thursday with a note that said it only toasts one side and it's the wrong side, and Denny had promised he'd look at it, and now it was in pieces across the workbench and Denny was holding a spring like it had personally betrayed him.

"I don't suppose," Denny said, without looking up, "either of you knows anything about toasters."

Warren set his mug down. "Let me see."

He picked up the pieces one at a time. He didn't hurry. Mina watched him fit the spring back into a housing she couldn't see how it had ever come out of, and his hands did the thing they did — no wasted motion, no forcing — and then he turned the whole mechanism over and looked at it from underneath, the way a doctor looks at a chart.

"Contact plate's bent," he said. "It's not making full contact on the left element. That's your one side."

"Can you fix it?"

Warren found a small tool she didn't have a name for and bent the plate back — not all the way new, just back to where it worked. He put the toaster back together in about four minutes. He plugged it in. He put a piece of Denny's bread in it — Denny made a noise — and pushed the lever down.

It toasted both sides. Evenly. The way a toaster was supposed to, the way most toasters never quite managed.

Denny stared at it. "Huh."

"It'll outlast us both now," Warren said. "Don't tell Mrs. Henley. She'll bring you the microwave."

Mina picked up the toaster. She turned it over. The bottom panel was scratched and old — he hadn't polished it, hadn't made it look new. It looked like the same old toaster Mrs. Henley had brought in, except now it worked better than it probably had the day she bought it.

That stuck in her head. She didn't know why yet.

---

The Bavarian Breakfast & Bakery was the kind of place that had been busy since before Mina was born and would be busy after she was gone. The door bells went off when they walked in, and the sound was almost completely swallowed by the clatter from the kitchen and the conversation from every table and the particular roar of a Saturday morning crowd that had all decided, independently, that this was where they wanted to be.

The hostess — a girl who couldn't have been more than nineteen — pointed them toward a two-top by the window without asking, because Warren had been coming here for longer than she'd been alive and she knew his table.

"Warren," said the server, arriving with menus they didn't need. She was maybe sixty, with a Southern accent thick enough to spread on toast. "And you brought a friend. Well, look at you, finally bringing somebody worth looking at."

"Lucee," Warren said. "This is Mina. Mina, Lucee."

"Honey, I know who she is. Whole town knows who she is." Lucee set down two waters. "You want the usual, or are you going to pretend you're going to order something different this time?"

"The usual."

"And for you?"

Mina looked at the menu board, which was handwritten and extensive and completely unnecessary. "What is the usual?"

"Three eggs over medium, rye toast, extra crispy hash browns, and he eats the jam straight out of the little packet like a man with no shame," Lucee said.

"I have shame," Warren said. "I just also have jam."

Mina laughed — actually laughed, for what felt like the first time in weeks — and ordered a vegetable omelet and sourdough.

Lucee went off toward the kitchen, and Mina looked around. The place was everything a breakfast place was supposed to be: worn wooden booths, a pie case by the register, coffee mugs that didn't match, the smell of bacon and cinnamon doing permanent battle with each other.

And then she saw the booth.

Not their booth — the one across the aisle. The corner booth, the one with the wobble that every old booth gets, except this one didn't wobble. She'd watched a family of five slide in and out of it without the table so much as shivering, and now she was looking at the table leg, at the way it met the floor.

Someone had fixed it. Not replaced it — the wood was old, scarred, original. But the joint was... exact. The table sat perfectly level. And the fix was invisible unless you knew to look for it, which she did, because she'd just watched the same hands do the same thing to Mrs. Henley's toaster.

"Warren," she said slowly. "Did you fix this whole restaurant?"

He was mid-sip of his water. He set the glass down. "I didn't fix the whole restaurant."

"How much of it?"

"Over the years? Bits." He shrugged. "Things break. I'm here. It seemed rude not to."

Lucee came back with the coffee pot. "He's being modest, honey. He's been fixing things in this town since before I moved here, and I moved here in '89." She topped off Warren's cup. "You know what he did? The walk-in freezer door. Three different repair companies told Otto it needed replacing — eight thousand dollars, new unit. Warren spent a Saturday with it. It's still running. Otto's daughter runs the place now and that freezer door still closes like it's apologizing."

"I like things that work," Warren said.

"You're a menace is what you are," Lucee said fondly, and went off to put in their order.

Mina was still looking at the table leg. "You preserve things," she said. "You don't make them new. You make them... what they were supposed to be."

"Something like that."

"How many places in this town have you done this to?"

He thought about it. "Define 'places.'"

"Warren."

"A lot of places." He picked up his water. "It's a small town. I've been here a long time. Things break."

She sat with that for a moment. The bells on the door went off — swallowed by the noise — and a man came in who had to duck to clear the frame. He was enormous, six-six at least, with hands like catcher's mitts and a face that looked like it had been carved out of something kind.

"Morning, Chuckles," Lucee called from across the room, without looking up from the coffee machine.

"Morning, Lucee," the giant said, and sat down at the counter like it was his assigned seat, which it probably was.

Mina looked at Warren. Warren looked at his menu, which he did not need.

"You're not going to explain that," she said.

"Nope."

"Chuckles."

"Yep."

She shook her head and let it go, because some things in Corbin's Landing you just accepted, and she'd learned that faster than she'd learned anything else about this town.

The food came, and it was as good as everything had promised — her omelet was perfect, his hash browns were genuinely extra crispy, and he did, in fact, eat the jam straight out of the packet. She watched him do it and felt something in her chest loosen that she hadn't realized was still tight.

This was the thing they'd been fighting for. Not the cosmology. Not the revelations. This. A Saturday morning. Eggs. A man eating jam out of a packet like he had no shame.

"There's more," Warren said quietly, not looking at her. "You know that, right?"

She set her fork down. "I know."

"I wasn't deflecting. Earlier. I was... I needed the telling to have room. And I needed —" He looked at her. "Ask me again. Not sometime. Soon. I mean it this time."

She studied him. The containment was back — not absence, containment. The thing she'd learned to read as this is costing him.

"Mary," she said.

Warren looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, once.

"Tonight," he said. "I'll tell you about Mary tonight. The whole thing. I promise."

The bells on the door went off, swallowed by the noise. Lucee refilled their coffee.

"Okay," she said to Warren. "Tonight."

---

They walked home along the water. It was the kind of October Saturday that made you forgive the whole concept of winter in advance — the light coming off the bay in sheets, the air smelling like salt and somebody's woodsmoke, a dog barking three streets over like it had opinions.

Warren held her hand. He'd started doing that without asking, somewhere in the middle of everything, and she'd started letting him without commenting, and now it was just a thing that was true.

"Can I ask you something small first?" she said. "Before tonight. Before the big thing."

"Sure."

"Mrs. Henley's toaster. The booth. The freezer door. How long have you been doing that — just quietly fixing the town?"

He was quiet for a few steps. "Since I got here."

"Twenty-five years of fixing things nobody asked you to fix."

"People asked. They just didn't ask me. They asked Denny, or Otto, or whoever, and I was there, and —" He shrugged. "It's what I do. I was a carpenter before I was anything else. Before all of it. You fix what's in front of you."

"A carpenter," she said. "Right. I'd almost forgotten that part."

"You forget a lot of things about me. It's one of your more endearing qualities."

"Careful."

They walked. The water did what the water did. A gull screamed at nothing.

"Warren," she said. "Whatever tonight is — I'm not going anywhere."

"I know."

"You don't know. You can't know."

He stopped walking. Turned to face her. The wind off the bay moved his hair, dark brown going on black, and she still didn't know which of the things about him were performance and which were just him, and she'd stopped needing to know.

"I know," he said again.

She believed him. That was the terrifying part. She believed him.

They walked home.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Eight: Mary

36_mary.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

They sat on the floor.

That was the thing, later, that Mina remembered first — not the words, but the floor. Warren's apartment, the patch of floor by the window that had become hers somewhere along the way, the badly-fitted curtain letting in a bar of streetlight. He'd made tea. She'd said she didn't want tea. He'd made it anyway. It sat between them, going cold, doing its job, which was to be something to look at that wasn't each other.

"You asked about Mary," Warren said.

"I did. Weeks ago."

"I know. I'm sorry. I needed —" He stopped. "No. That's a deflection and you deserve better. I was afraid. That's the truth. I've been afraid of this telling for two thousand years."

Mina waited.

"Her name was Miriam," he said. "That's the name her mother gave her. Mary is what the Greeks did to it, the way they did to everything. Yeshua, Jesus. Miriam, Mary. The whole story got translated until the people in it were unrecognizable, including me."

He picked up his tea. Put it down.

"You've met her, you know. In the stories. The Miriam in the workshop — the one who argued with me about the yoke money, the one who made the choice at the fire. That was her. That's who she was before any of the rest of it."

Mina nodded slowly. She'd liked Miriam, in the stories. She'd liked her the way you liked someone you'd actually want to have dinner with.

"Tell me," she said.

---

"She was from Magdala."

The word landed in the room like a stone in water.

Mina felt it before she understood it. A cold spreading out from the center of her chest. She knew that name. Everyone knew that name.

"Say it again," she said.

"Miriam of Magdala." Warren's voice was level. "That's who she was. That's who the Miriam in my stories has been, this whole time. I didn't — I never lied to you. I just didn't say the rest of it yet."

"Mary Magdalene."

"Yes."

Mina stood up. She didn't mean to — her body did it before her brain caught up — and she walked to the window and stood in the bar of streetlight with her arms crossed, looking down at the empty street.

"Mary Magdalene," she said again, to the window.

"Yes."

"The —" She turned around. "The one at the cross. The one at the tomb. The one they spent two thousand years calling a prostitute because a sixth-century pope folded her into the sinful woman and nobody ever unfolded her."

"That's the one," Warren said quietly. "Except the prostitute part was never true. That was —" He shook his head. "My reading, for what it's worth: she was there for all of it, and they couldn't erase her completely, so they shrank her. Made her a cautionary tale. I've watched institutions do that to witnesses they can't control."

"Was she —" Mina stopped. Started again. "Were you —"

"In love with her." "Yes."

Mina sat back down. Slowly. The floor was hard and she was grateful for it — something solid, something that didn't require interpretation.

"Tell me the rest," she said. "Not the institution part. The her part. Who was she, Warren? Really?"

---

"She was the smartest person I ever met."

He said it simply, like it was a measurement, not a compliment.

"Smarter than me. I mean that. I had — I had advantages she didn't have. The Harmony. The memory. Time, eventually. She had a fishing town and a sharp tongue and a mind that wouldn't stop moving. She figured out what I was before I told her. Not the details — nobody figured out the details — but she knew I wasn't what I said I was, and she waited until I was ready to say it, and when I said it she just nodded like I'd finally admitted something obvious."

"What did she do? In Magdala?"

"Her family had boats. She kept the accounts — that's how we met, actually. She hired me to fix a boat shed." A ghost of a smile. "She said my work was good but my prices were insulting. We argued about money for twenty minutes and I walked home thinking about her the whole way, which annoyed me, because I had — I had a mission, I had a purpose, I was very serious about all of it, and then this woman from Magdala told me I didn't know what a fair price was."

Mina smiled despite herself. "She sounds like Perla."

"She would have liked Perla. Perla would have —" He stopped. "Sorry. Go on. Ask me things. It's easier if you ask."

"Did she travel with you?"

"Yes. From early on. She left Magdala — left the boats, the family, all of it — and she came with us. It cost her more than the boats: in that country, a woman traveling with men who weren't her kin was a scandal, and the women who bankrolled us paid for it in reputation as well as coin. Miriam never asked me to apologize for it, and I never did. People talk about the disciples like it was twelve men and a crowd. It wasn't. There were women, the whole time, funding it, organizing it, keeping it from falling apart. Miriam was — she was the one who made sure we ate. She was the one who remembered where we were supposed to be. The men argued theology. She ran the operation."

"The money," Mina said, remembering the story. "The yoke money. The choice at the fire."

"That was her. All of that was her." Warren looked at his hands. "She understood what I was trying to do before I understood it. The Harmony — what it meant, what it could be. She asked me questions nobody else thought to ask. She wanted to know what it felt like. Not the power. The — the interior of it. What it was like to be two people and also one person. I tried to explain it and I did a bad job and she said, 'You're explaining it like a man,' which was fair."

"What was she like? As a person. Not a disciple. A person."

Warren was quiet for a long time.

"She laughed," he said finally. "Loudly. Inappropriately. At a funeral, once — she never forgave herself, but one of the men speaking over the dead had said something so profoundly stupid about him that she couldn't hold it in, and then she spent the whole walk home hitting my arm and saying 'don't you dare laugh, don't you —' and I was laughing too, we both were, it was terrible." He was smiling. "She was angry at injustice in a way that was almost a physical force. She cried when she was angry, which she hated. She was kind to people who didn't deserve it and ruthless with people who did. She —"

He stopped.

"She was the first person," he said, "in a very long time, who looked at me and saw a person instead of a purpose. Do you understand? Everyone else — the disciples, the crowds, even my mother, God help her — they saw what I was for. Miriam saw who I was. There's a difference. It's the whole difference."

Mina's throat was tight. "What happened to her? After. After Jerusalem."

"She lived." Warren's voice was firm. "Whatever they tell you — she lived. She was there. At the execution. At the — at what came after. She was the first one I —" He stopped, breathed. "She was there for all of it. And then she lived a long time, and she never stopped being exactly who she was, and when she died she was surrounded by people who loved her, and I wasn't there, and that's — that's one of the ones I carry."

"Why weren't you there?"

"Because I couldn't be. Because by then I was —" He gestured vaguely. "I was becoming what I am now. The leaving. The long part. I couldn't stay in one place anymore without the questions starting. I said goodbye to her and I left, and she understood, and it was still the worst thing."

They sat in silence. The tea had gone completely cold. The streetlight bar had moved a few inches across the floor.

"Warren," Mina said. "Why are you telling me this now?"

He looked at her. The containment was gone. There was no containment left.

"Because you asked," he said. "And because there's more. There's — Mina, the thing about Miriam, the thing I haven't told you —"

"What?"

He opened his mouth. Closed it.

"Tomorrow," he said. "Please. Tonight was — tonight was for her. She deserves a night that's just hers. Tomorrow I'll tell you the rest."

"What rest? Warren, what —"

"The reason I'm afraid," he said. "The reason I've been afraid for two thousand years. Tomorrow."

Mina wanted to push. Everything in her — the scientist, the woman, the person who'd just had the floor tilted under her — wanted to push.

But she looked at his face, and she nodded.

"Okay," she said. "Tomorrow."

She stayed on the floor. He stayed on the floor. The cold tea sat between them. After a while, without either of them deciding it, she leaned against his shoulder, and he put his arm around her, and they sat like that in the bar of streetlight, with Miriam of Magdala in the room with them — not a ghost, not a saint, just a woman who'd laughed too loud at a funeral once, and argued about money, and run the operation, and been there for all of it.

"She sounds like someone I would have liked," Mina said quietly.

"She would have liked you too," Warren said. "That's — that's part of what I'm afraid of."

Mina didn't ask what he meant. Tomorrow, she told herself. Tomorrow.

But she held his hand a little tighter, in the dark.
