Chapter Working draft

Chapter Thirty-Nine: The Eternal Connection

37_the_eternal_connection.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

She said the name back to him the way she'd repeat a lab result she didn't trust.

"Mary Magdalene."

She had said tomorrow. Tomorrow had lasted eleven minutes.

It stayed in the room. Warren was by the window, which in this apartment meant two steps from the bed and one step from the table. The curtain hung wrong on its rod the way it always hung wrong, and a bar of streetlight came through the gap and lay across the floor between them like something neither of them wanted to step on.

Mina was on her floor patch — back against the bed, knees up, the posture she'd had since childhood, the one that had never learned to be professional.

"Say the rest," she said.

"I just told you—"

"The rest. You're doing the thing."

"What thing."

"The thing where you stop and wait to see if I'll ask. There's always a second sentence."

Warren sat down on the floor across from her. The bed between them like a neutral country.

"This one isn't a second sentence," he said. "It's a second floor."

"Warren." She studied him. "Before the second floor. The story — her. Are you all right?"

He blinked. "Me?"

"You just spent an hour telling me about someone you loved. In detail. With voices." She shifted against the bed. "I'm the one who got the revelation. You're the one who had to say it out loud."

Warren was quiet. Then: "It cost less than I expected. That's either healing or numbness, and I've learned not to audit it too closely."

"That," Mina said, "is the most emotionally evasive sentence you've produced since the sacrifice thing."

"I'm pacing myself."

"The Soul joined to you — the one at the edges. The recognition. The dreams. The flickers."

"I haven't been calling them flickers. I've been ignoring them professionally."

"That Eternal is the same one that was joined to Mary."

Denny's radio downstairs lost a station and found it again.

Mina's hands went very still on her knees. "Say that differently so I can be sure I heard it."

"One Soul. Exceptionally intact. Old past — old. It entered Harmony before any of this was a project — with the forming star Sol. It called the One to Terra." He looked at her. "Much later it was joined to Mary Magdalene. And now it's joined to you."

Her laugh was short and unrelated to humor. "My mother gets a fractured one and I get the deluxe model."

"That's one way to put it."

"How long have you known."

"That the Soul was ancient and intact? Almost from the start. That it was hers — that specific one — " He turned the river stone over in his hand; she hadn't seen him pick it up off the table. "Your dreams started saying her name before I'd told you anything. The night you kissed me, when you felt the I am underneath the room — that wasn't your imagination finding patterns. That was pattern recognition finding an actual pattern."

"Not my genetics."

"No." Flat. "Your genetics are plumbing, Mina. Compatibility history. Inherited reliability. It tells you who can hold a Soul without breaking. It doesn't tell you who the Soul is. What's old here is the Soul. You're fifty-one."

"Let me do the inventory," Mina said. "Out loud. So you can correct me."

"All right."

"There's something I never told you. The dreams. A woman's face I kept almost recognizing. Waking up with sentences in my head that weren't mine."

"Yes."

"The Miriam stories — feeling like I'd heard them before you'd finished telling them."

"Yes."

"The night I kissed you. The I am under the room."

"Yes."

"The headaches in March."

"That was you not sleeping."

"Correcting me already."

"It's what I'm for."

She exhaled through her nose. "So my brain ran the pattern match weeks ago and I filed it under stress and perimenopause, and meanwhile you were standing there watching me do it."

"I wasn't watching you do it. I was watching you not do it. There's a difference, and it's worse."

"Why is mine coherent?" Mina said. "The dreams. The recognition. With Perla it comes through in pieces — weather, almost. Mine comes through like tuning a radio and finding the station on the first try."

"Because it's intact." Warren said it the way you'd say a diagnosis. "Fractured Souls come through as static and fragments. Yours doesn't. It's the most intact Soul I've ever seen joined to a living person."

"Including yours."

"Mine was never fractured." He said it without pride, which was how she knew it was true. "It didn't need a human to heal it. Yours — yours healed itself a long way before you were born, or it wouldn't have survived her."

"Her."

"Mary." He nodded. "Whatever she did in that life — however she lived it — it left the Soul more whole than it found it. That's not nothing, Mina. Most lives don't do that."

She absorbed that the way she absorbed everything — visibly, unwillingly, all at once. Then, because she was Mina: "Mary, though she did not yet know all of Mary."

"What?"

"The night I kissed you. When I looked at you and wondered who you were seeing. It went through my head — her, not quite her. Mary, though she did not yet know all of Mary." She shook her head. "I thought I was being clever."

"It wasn't clever," Warren said. "It was a hypothesis."

"I hate when you do that."

"Do what."

"Make my jokes turn out to be data."

That got a real smile out of him, brief as a struck match. Then it went out.

"What does it mean," she said. "Full Harmony. What actually happens. To me."

Warren set the stone down. Picked it up again. "The Soul has its complete available memory. Everything it still holds. Full Harmony puts that in your hands. Not as metaphor."

"Mary's life."

"Mary's life."

"Her love for — " She couldn't finish the sentence. Didn't need to.

"Yes."

"And the older part. Sol. The One."

"Yes."

"Who's Sol."

"Later."

"Warren—"

"Later means later." He held up a hand. "There's a full telling and tonight isn't it. You get the shape tonight: old, before Terra, and that Soul called mine here. The rest keeps."

Mina looked at the bar of streetlight on the floor. "That Soul knew yours before there were humans."

"Yes."

"Jesus, Warren."

"I know."

"I would remember loving you before I was me."

"You would remember her loving me." He said it carefully. "The Soul's memory, received whole. I don't know the interior texture, Mina. I know the structure. You would have access to all of it, and you would still be you."

"That's a large claim."

"It's a witnessed claim." He leaned forward, elbows on knees. "You watched your mother come through Harmony with a fractured Soul and come out the other side still Perla. Still arguing about the chrysanthemums. Still Catholic. Still herself, with everything she is, plus — " He stopped. "You saw it. That's not my theology. That's your data."

"One data point."

"One witnessed data point. Yours, not mine. That's why it counts."

The anger arrived then — it had been waiting behind the science, and the science ran out of questions.

"Here's the actual question," she said. "While you were telling me about the Universe and the unanswered question and the cook in Chengdu. While I was kissing you and you were stopping me because there's more to Harmony than I've told you. You knew. You knew whose Soul this is. You knew what I'd be walking into, and you let me walk."

"Yes."

"Say more than yes."

He was quiet for a moment. "I knew you couldn't hold it yet."

"Don't you dare."

"It's true and it's insufficient and I'm saying it anyway." He met her eyes. "If I'd given you the name Mary Magdalene before you'd met Miriam — before the stories, before you knew who she was — it would have landed like a brand. A Bible name, on you, before you had any say in what it meant. And if I'd told you about the Soul before you'd seen your mother come through Harmony still herself, it would have been a threat with no evidence against it. Information you can't hold yet isn't honesty, Mina. It's dropping something heavy on thin ice and calling it transparency."

"That's very well put."

"Thank you."

"It's a reason, not an excuse. I get to be angry that you decided the thickness of the ice."

"You do."

Warren stood. Went to the little counter. Picked up the kettle.

"If you make tea right now," Mina said, "I will throw the mug at you."

He set the kettle down. Sat back down.

"When I kissed you," she said. "You stopped because there was more to Harmony than you'd told me. That was this."

"Part of it was this."

"And you kissed me back first."

"Yes."

"Knowing."

"Yes." He didn't dress it. "I knew, and I wanted you, and I stopped us before the wanting could make the choice for you. That's the whole of it. It's not better than it sounds."

She stood — two steps to the window, two steps back; the room didn't permit pacing, which made the attempt both funnier and worse. "Do you know the worst part?"

"Tell me."

"I half-knew. The dreams. The way I'd look at you and feel — " She shook her head, the red streak falling across her forehead. "My brain ran the pattern match weeks ago and I filed it under stress and perimenopause. So now I get to be furious at you and furious at me, which is very efficient."

Warren almost smiled. Managed not to.

Mina sat back down on her patch. Looked at him across the neutral country of the bed.

"Am I someone to you," she said, "or am I the closest you've come in two thousand years to getting her back?"

Downstairs, somebody laughed in the store, faint through the floorboards. The radio found its station.

Warren opened his mouth.

"Don't," Mina said. "Not tonight. If you answer that fast, it won't be true no matter what you say."

He closed his mouth.

The question stayed in the room with them, unanswered — the way the curtain hung wrong and nobody fixed it.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty: Whom Do You Love

38_whom_do_you_love.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

She'd said not tonight.

It had been forty minutes.

Mina looked up from her knees. "I lied. I want the answer tonight."

Warren was still on the floor across from her. He hadn't moved. "Which answer."

"All of them. And don't give me a taxonomy. I don't want a lecture on three consciousnesses." She held his eyes. "I want to know what happens in you when you look at me and remember her."

He nodded slowly. "All right."

"Start with Mary."

So he talked about Mary. Not the Magdalene of the stained glass — Miriam. The woman who'd kept their money because someone had to and she'd been better at it than the men. Who'd negotiated with a steward who tried to cheat them and walked away with the better end of it. Who'd challenged him at the fire when he was wrong, in front of everyone, because she'd decided his feelings about being challenged were his problem.

Mina listened. Then: "You love her."

"Yes."

"Still."

"Yes." He didn't look away. "It's a completed love, Mina. It has an ending. She lived and she died and I — " He stopped. "Grief with the edges worn smooth. I can hold it now without it cutting. That's what finished means. Not gone. Finished."

"Say the rest."

"The rest is that I don't need you to be her, because she already happened. Fully. There's no unfinished business with Mary that you could complete for me. Do you understand? The tenderness isn't a threat. It's the proof. If I were trying to get her back through you, I wouldn't be able to talk about her like this. I'd be careful. I'm not careful. She's mine to remember, and remembering her doesn't take anything from you, because you're not her replacement. You're not her anything."

"Did she make you laugh."

"What?"

"Mary. Did she make you laugh, or is this all — " Mina gestured vaguely. "Reverent."

Warren's mouth moved. "She told me my parables needed editing."

"She did not."

"She said — " He dropped his voice half an octave, which was a terrible Miriam impression and they both knew it. "'If the kingdom is like a mustard seed, you could at least pick a seed people have heard of.'"

Mina laughed before she could stop herself, and then looked annoyed at having done it. "That's good."

"She was good." "That's what I'm trying to tell you. She was a person. Funny, competent, difficult. Mine to remember. Not yours to compete with."

Mina was quiet. Then, quieter: "The Soul, then. The old one."

Warren's expression changed — not softening, exactly. Widening. "That's not romance, Mina."

"I didn't say it was."

"I've known that Soul since before there were words for knowing. It called the One to Terra — called me, the part of me that's oldest — before your species had a name for itself." He searched for the shape of it. "That's not a love story. That's the oldest friendship anything has. Awe is the closest word and it's the wrong size."

"What did it feel like," Mina said. "Being called. To Terra."

Warren looked at the badly fitted curtain for a moment. "Like being surprised by something that wasn't a disaster. I hadn't — the One hadn't, in a very long time, been surprised in a way that didn't cost blood. Your Soul called, and I came, and it turned out to be the beginning of everything instead of the end of something." He shook his head. "You asked me once what I had to learn about you are. That other consciousnesses get to surprise you. It started there. Before there were humans to be surprised by."

"And me."

"And you."

"Name something." She leaned forward. "Something you know about me that has nothing to do with her and nothing to do with it. If I'm a person to you and not a — a venue — prove it with specifics."

Warren didn't hesitate. "You drop your scarf on purpose to watch me malfunction."

"That's one."

"You said the timing was catastrophic. About — " He nodded at the bed. "You used the word catastrophic about the worst-timed theological disclosure in history, and then you laughed until you couldn't breathe."

"You started it with the fitted sheets."

"You interrogated me about the Nazca lines like I'd personally disappointed you by not knowing."

"I'd built it up."

"You check lab results twice and then pretend you didn't."

"I do not."

"You do. I've watched." He was warming to it now, and that was its own evidence. "You have a red streak in your bangs because some part of you at fifty-one refuses to be entirely respectable. You eat takeout standing up when you think no one's watching. You say Jesus, Warren the way other people say bless your heart."

She stared at him. "That's — "

"And I never said that sentence to Mary." Quiet. "The Aramaic one. You have a beautiful mind. I waited a very long time to say it to someone and mean it the way I meant it, and I said it to you. Not to her. There is no version of this where you are her to me, Mina. I was afraid of wanting you incorrectly — that was about you. All of it was about you."

The room was very quiet. The radio downstairs had gone to static between stations.

"Say the selfish part," Mina said.

"What."

"The selfish part. You've got one. I can feel it sitting over there." She nodded at him. "Say it plainly. No dressing."

Warren looked at the river stone in his hand. Set it down. "Part of me wants you to choose Harmony because I am tired of being the only one who remembers."

There it was. Undressed.

"Two thousand years," he said. "Nobody alive remembers Yeshua as he was. Not the theology. Him. The person. The jokes that didn't make it into the book."

"Tell me one," Mina said.

"No."

"Why not."

"Because then I'd be spending it." He looked at her. "That's the whole problem, Mina. Every story I tell you is true, and every one of them is also an argument. I know that. So some of them I keep."

His voice thinned. "If you Harmonize, someone else will know. Really know. Not from stories. From memory. And part of me wants that so badly it — " He shook his head. "It doesn't matter how badly. Wanting something doesn't entitle you to arrange for it."

Mina looked at him for a long moment. "Have you ever said that out loud before. Any of that."

"No."

"Two thousand years, and you've never said it."

"There wasn't anyone to say it to," he said simply. "That's rather the point."

She absorbed that. Nodded once. "Okay."

"Okay?"

"I'm registering the cost. Don't rush me."

"Voss wanted things badly."

"Yes."

"Voss wanted to save the world."

"I know." Flat. "It's not virtue, Mina. Not choosing for you. It's not nobility. It's the only rule I have left that I haven't broken at least once, and I'm not breaking it now. I will tell you everything — there's more I haven't told you, about Sol, about what else Harmony — later. When you can hold it. But I won't choose. Not the act, not the timing, none of it. That's yours."

Mina pulled her knees up. "Love me as Mina or not at all."

"That's the only way I know how to do it."

"Is it." Not quite a question.

Then the hardest one. She said it looking at the floor. "What if I remember loving you as her, and it's bigger than anything that's mine? What if her love — " Her voice caught, and she hated it, and kept going. "What if I can't tell which memories are mine anymore? What if she dilutes me?"

Warren didn't answer fast. She'd told him not to, once, and he'd learned.

"I don't know what it feels like from the inside," he said finally. "Nobody's done this — a Soul that intact, a Harmony that complete — not in my memory. Your mother is the closest evidence, and she's one data point, and it's yours. Harmony did not erase her. That's a witnessed fact. It's not a promise about the texture." He paused. "The structure says you remain you. The evidence says the person survives. The rest — what it's like to remember a love that predates you and isn't yours, from the inside — I can't tell you, because I don't know."

"That's not very reassuring."

"It's the most honest thing I have."

She almost smiled. Didn't, quite. "The most honest thing you've said since the Aramaic."

"Low bar. I've been lying by omission for months."

"Don't." But she was smiling now, a little. "Don't do the self-flagellation thing. It's boring and it doesn't suit you."

"I want it in the record that I'm scared," Mina said.

"It's in the record."

"Not your record. Mine." She tapped her temple. "Fifty-one years of being the person who runs the numbers, and the numbers say: I don't know what this does to me. I know the structure. I know one data point. I don't know the texture, and the texture is where I live."

"I know."

"And if it's — if her love is bigger than mine, if I can't tell — "

"Then you'll tell me," Warren said. "That's the deal on offer. Not that it won't be strange. That you get to be angry about the strangeness with the one person alive who'll understand it."

Mina considered that. "That's not nothing."

"It's not a promise about the interior."

"I know. I'm counting it anyway."

He tried a joke. It didn't quite land — something about the curtain, about how he'd hung it himself rather than ask Denny and they'd both been pretending not to notice for years, and the punchline arrived a half-second late and sideways.

Mina laughed anyway. Or half-laughed. It was enough.

She stood, crossed the two steps to the table, and picked up the river stone. Weighed it in her palm.

"Collateral," she said, and put it in her pocket.

"That's theft."

"You can bill me." She looked at him — really looked, the red streak fallen across her forehead, fifty-one years old and furious and still here. "We're not done with this."

"No."

"Good." She went to the door. Stopped. Turned. "One more."

"All right."

"What did she call you? Mary. When it was just — "

"Yeshua." He paused. "When she was angry, just Yesh. Like everyone."

"Nobody's called you that in a long time."

"No."

Mina nodded. Filed it away with everything else. "For the record — the scarf thing? I only did it twice."

"Three times."

"You're counting."

"I'm ancient. Counting is all I have left."

She left him sitting on the floor, and the question stayed open behind her like a door neither of them had closed.

He stayed there a while, listening to the store settle below him, the river stone gone from the table like a small, deliberate absence. It was the first thing of his she'd ever stolen.

It would not, he suspected, be the last.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-One: Memory-Story: The Execution (Warren/Yeshua, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc memory-story, 2026-09-22. The crucifixion as genuine physical event; death-sleep rendered from the inside; Miriam present throughout. Not final prose.)
39_the_execution.md

Warren had been quiet for a long time after the fire story.

"You're thinking about the crucifixion," Mina said. "Jesus on the cross."

"Yeshua," he said, automatic as a reflex. Then: "Sorry. Go on."

"You don't have to apologize for it."

"It's a two-thousand-year itch. It doesn't scratch clean."

She waited.

"I'm going to tell this one straight through," he said. "The way it was, not the way anyone framed it afterward. Which means I need you to understand something first: Rome's part of it is just Rome. There is no cosmic transaction in what I'm about to tell you. A government killed a man in the way that government killed men, and the man survived it. Everything else was added later by people who needed it to mean more."

"Okay."

"You keep saying okay like that and I'm going to start suspecting you've been replaced."

"Tell the story, Warren."

*

The trial was shorter than anyone admits afterward.

People want there to have been weight to it. Deliberation. A long night of the law turning its great wheels. There wasn't. There was a prefect who had a festival to get through without a riot, a crowd that had been worked up by men who understood crowds, and a morning.

I don't remember much of what Pilate said to me. I remember his hands. He kept touching the edge of his desk, this little nervous tapping, like a man who has already made his decision and is irritated that the formalities are taking so long.

They gave me the crossbeam to carry. Not the whole cross — the upright was already waiting at the execution ground. Just the beam, laid across my shoulders.

It was heavy in exactly the way wood is heavy. That sounds stupid. But I remember thinking it, which tells you something about where my mind was. Not on destiny. On the weight of the wood, and the dust, and the fact that my shoulder had a raw place from the rope they'd used the night before and the beam kept finding it.

Jerusalem was crowded for the festival. The road out to the execution ground had people along it the way roads have people when there's something to look at.

Some of them were mine. Most weren't. Most were just there because a crucifixion is a thing happening and people go look at things happening. I don't say that with contempt. I've done it myself. Curiosity is one of the most honest things about people.

The soldiers were doing a job.

That's the part that never makes it into the telling. They're always either monsters or instruments of prophecy. They weren't either. They were men doing work they'd done before and would do again. One of them was young — couldn't have been twenty — and he kept looking at me the way you look at something you're supposed to be indifferent to and aren't quite managing. The older one, the one in charge of the detail, had the efficient, bored movements of a man who has done a thing so many times his body does it while his mind is elsewhere.

I hated that almost more than if they'd been cruel.

They stripped me. Nailed me up.

I am not going to dress that up. They drove the nails through my wrists — I remember thinking, stupidly, practically, that the hand wouldn't have held the weight — and then the feet, and then they raised the thing and dropped it into its hole, and the jolt of that went through my entire body like I'd been struck by something much larger than wood.

I was afraid.

I want that on the record, because the icon version of me was never afraid, and the icon version of me is a liar. I was afraid the way any animal is afraid when it understands exactly what's happening to it. My mouth went dry. My hands shook. Not trembled — shook, the way hands do when the body has stopped consulting you about its decisions.

The crowd made noise. The kind of noise crowds make. Some shouting, some just the low constant sound of a lot of people breathing and shifting and being there.

And Miriam was there.

Not at a distance, the way the later accounts politely put the women. Closer than almost anyone. Close enough that when the soldiers let the permitted few step nearer, she was among them.

She'd kept the promise.

The sun did what the sun does in that country. It climbed and it stayed. The heat came up off the rocks. My back was against wood that had held other men before me — I could feel the old stains under my shoulder blades, and I understood, with a clarity that almost made me laugh, that this was the closest thing to an inheritance Rome ever gave anybody.

Time stopped meaning anything I could have explained to you.

There is the pain, and then there is what the pain does to time, and they are not the same thing. The pain was straightforward. It was the body reporting damage, which is the body's job. What it did to time was stranger. Hours passed that I can account for almost minute by minute, and then there were stretches where I have no idea — where I was somewhere underneath the pain, the way you go somewhere underneath a fever.

They gave me sour wine on a sponge.

Posca. The soldiers' own drink. I don't think it was kindness — my guess, then and now, is routine: a man dying of thirst makes more noise than a man who's had something wet, and they'd learned that. I didn't care about the motive. My throat was closing and I drank, and it was the best thing I had ever tasted, and I have spent two thousand years being faintly embarrassed by that.

Miriam spoke to me once.

I don't know how long in. The light had changed.

"Yeshua."

I got my eyes to focus. She was right there, close enough to touch, and there was a soldier maybe ten feet away watching us the way you watch something you've decided isn't your problem.

"Go," I said. My voice didn't sound like mine. "You don't have to stay for this."

"I'm staying."

"Miriam—"

"But you don't get to yoke me with my own fear."

She said it quiet. Almost gentle. It was the same sentence she'd used at the fire, and hearing it there, nailed to wood, with my body coming apart — I laughed. It hurt to laugh. I did it anyway.

"Then stay," I said.

She stayed.

*

Here is the part no one has ever told correctly, because I never told it.

My body was failing. That's not a metaphor. Nailed up, losing blood, the heat, the slow work of it — my body was doing what bodies do, and I could feel the systems going. The breath getting shallow. The heart working harder for less.

And the One was with me. Not above me, not watching — with me, the way he'd been with me my whole life, in the Perfect Harmony that had never once broken.

We didn't discuss it in words. Harmony doesn't work that way. But there was a moment — a decision with the shape of a held breath — where I understood what was available to me.

I could let go.

Not die. Let go.

I could slow everything — the heart, the breath, the blood — past the point where any observer could tell the difference between what I was doing and death. The Eternal knew matter the way a musician knows an instrument, and in Harmony that knowing was mine. I could sink the body below death's threshold and hold it there. A thread held under water. Not drowned. Held.

I was afraid to do it, too. I should say that. I had never tested this. Miriam had asked me at the fire whether they could kill me, and I had told her the truth: I didn't know.

I decided to find out.

The pain was the last thing to go quiet.

That's the part that surprises people, when I've told the small piece of this I've ever told. You'd think the pain would be the first thing the Harmony took. It wasn't. The body kept reporting damage until the signals themselves thinned out. The pain went last, and then it went, and I thought — I actually thought this, which is absurd — I thought, well, that's a mercy.

Then the sounds went. The crowd. The wind. Miriam's breathing, which I had been listening to without knowing I was listening to it.

Then nothing.

I don't mean darkness. I don't mean peace. I mean nothing in the way a held breath is nothing — a suspension, a thread kept taut. The One held the thread. I was gone past any point a Roman soldier with a spear could have measured, and I was not gone past the point where I could be brought back.

Any man in that crowd would have told you I was dead.

He would not have been lying.

*

They came to check, the way they always came to check.

I wasn't there for the spear. I need you to understand exactly what I mean by that. What the body did when the steel went in was the body's business, and the body was empty of me by then. The wound bled the way a wound bleeds when the heart has nearly stopped — blood and the clear fluid around it. Your doctors have a name for that fluid. The soldier who drove the spear called it proof.

He was satisfied. They always were, when the body did that.

A man named Yosef — he'd heard me speak, had more courage than sense — claimed the body. They wrapped it. They put it in a tomb he owned, which was either generosity or the fastest way to get a corpse off his conscience before the Sabbath. Probably both. People are allowed to be both.

Miriam watched that, too.

She kept the promise all the way to the stone.

*

Warren stopped.

The room was very quiet. Mina had stopped writing some time ago; the notebook lay closed on her knee.

"Did it hurt?" she asked.

"Yes."

"That's it? Yes?"

"Yes," he said. "Terribly. Don't let anyone tell you different — not the theologians, not the ones who say I wasn't really there, not anyone. I was there. It hurt. That's the whole of that answer."

She nodded slowly.

"Why didn't you run?" she asked. "Before. When you still could."

"Because I told Miriam at the fire. What I'd said publicly became lies if I disappeared the moment saying it became dangerous." He rubbed the back of his neck. "And because someone had to be the man who didn't run, and there wasn't a line of volunteers."

"That's not theology."

"No."

"It's just stubbornness."

"Most of what I did was just stubbornness. The Harmony made the stubbornness interesting. It didn't make it holy."

Mina was quiet for a moment. Then she reached over and put her hand on his forearm, and left it there.

"There's more," he said. "The tomb. What happened after."

"Okay."

"Okay," he said. "The tomb, then."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Two: Memory-Story: The Return and the Ascension (Warren/Yeshua, first-person)

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc memory-story, 2026-09-22. Death-sleep reversal from the inside; Miriam as first witness; Ascension as genuine physical event; end of the Yeshua tellings. Not final prose.)
40_return_and_ascension.md

"You're going to tell me you walked out of a tomb," Mina said.

"I am."

"It's going to sound exactly as strange as it is."

"It will. I'm not going to sand the edges off."

She settled back. She'd learned his tells by now — the way he gathered himself before the past came up, the way his voice changed when he stopped being Warren remembering and started being the man who'd been there.

"One thing first," he said. "She was first. Mary. The tradition got that part right, and I want you to know it was real before it was a story. Whatever else anyone's done with her since — that morning was hers."

Mina nodded. "Go on."

*

The first thing I noticed was how much everything ached.

Coming back hurt worse than going under, in its way. Going under had been a release — the signals thinning, the pain going last and then going. Coming back was the reverse: every system reporting in at once, all of them complaining, the body furious at having been held below the threshold and then asked to resume business as usual.

I was lying on cold stone in the dark, wrapped in linen that had stuck to the wounds, smelling myrrh and damp rock and my own dried blood.

The tomb belonged to Yosef. Nicer than I deserved, honestly. Cut stone, a proper rolling stone in its track. Quiet.

The Eternal had been holding the thread for — I didn't know how long. Time hadn't passed for me the way it passes for people who are in it. But the body had been down long enough that bringing it back wasn't a single moment. It was work.

The breath came first. Shallow. Then the heart, finding its rhythm the way a man finds his footing on uneven ground — a stumble, a catch, a stumble, then steady.

Then the repair. That's the word I have for it, though your science would want a better one. The Harmony maintaining the body, knitting what could be knit. The wounds closed. The blood that had pooled where it shouldn't be was drawn back into use or broken down. It wasn't instant and it wasn't painless. It was the body doing, very fast, what bodies do very slowly — and my whole awareness was the ache of it.

Here's something no one's ever asked me, so I'll tell you unasked: the marks stayed.

The wounds healed. The scars remained. The body remembered what had been done to it, the way skin remembers a burn long after the burn has stopped mattering. The Harmony repaired me. It didn't unhappen me.

I lay there for a while, just breathing, letting the systems settle. Then I sat up, and the world tilted, and I sat back down, and tried again more carefully.

The linen wrappings had adhered to the wounds as they closed. I peeled them off. It hurt. Everything about that morning hurt. I have never been less majestic in my life, and I include the time I fell off a donkey in front of forty people.

The stone.

I need you to picture this correctly, because the later versions all make it sound like a special effect. It wasn't. I was a man who had been dead three days, still weak, still with my blood not entirely where it belonged, pushing a cut-stone disc in a track in the dark. It took me a long time. I had to stop and rest with my forehead against the rock, breathing like an old man, and then push again.

When it finally moved — when the track gave and the stone rolled and the first gray of morning came through the gap — I sat down in the dirt outside the tomb and laughed until I coughed.

Pre-dawn. Cold. The birds hadn't started yet. The garden around Yosef's tomb was wet with dew, and I was filthy, wrapped in a borrowed cloth, sitting in the dirt outside my own grave.

And then I heard someone coming.

*

She'd come to finish the burial work.

That's what the morning was for — the spices, the proper anointing, the things there hadn't been time for before the Sabbath. She came before light because grief doesn't sleep well and because she'd been lying awake anyway, and because there was work to do and Miriam had always been the one who did the work.

She saw me sitting there in the half-dark and stopped.

I know what she saw. A figure in the garden, filthy, in grave cloths, where no living person had any business being. The tradition says she thought I was the gardener, and two thousand years of jokes about that, and she earned it honestly. I'd have thought gardener too.

"Miriam," I said.

She stood there with her mouth open.

Then she said my name back. And it was the first time in three days the world made sense.

She didn't faint. She didn't fall down the way the later paintings like her to. She stood there, and her hands were shaking, and she said, "You're cold," which was such a Miriam thing to say — I had come back from death and her first observation was about my temperature — and then she reached for me and I had to catch her wrist.

"Careful," I said. "The wounds are still tender."

She laughed. It came out half a sob. She held my arm instead, through the cloth, like she was checking that it was solid, and I let her, because it was solid, and because she had stayed through the worst of it and she had earned the right to check.

"How—" she started.

"I don't know if I can explain it."

"Try."

So I tried. I told her about the thread held under water. I told her the One had kept it and I had come back up the way a diver comes back up — slowly, because fast would have killed me for real. She listened the way she'd always listened, which is to say she interrupted twice and then apologized and then interrupted a third time.

"Does it hurt?" she asked. "Now."

"Everything hurts."

"Good," she said. "Hurting means it's working."

That was Mary. That was Miriam. Whoever the later centuries needed her to be, that morning she was a practical woman in a garden telling a man who'd been dead that pain was a good sign.

*

What I remember as the forty days after that were the strangest of my life, and I include the three days of being dead.

I kept showing up.

That's really what it was. I'd appear — at a meal, on a road, in a room where the door had been barred because everyone was frightened — and people would try very hard to act as though this were normal. It wasn't normal. A man they'd watched die was eating bread at their table, and they would pass him the olives like the alternative was acknowledging the situation.

One of them — I won't name him, he's suffered enough — needed to put his hand in the wound in my side before he'd believe it. So I let him. Arguing would have taken longer.

"Satisfied?" I asked.

He looked like he wanted the ground to open.

"Don't be embarrassed," I said. "I'd have wanted the same."

They kept wanting me to stay. To lead. To fix everything, to settle the arguments, to tell them exactly what to do next. And I kept leaving, because I had somewhere else to be and no way to explain the itinerary, and because the longer I stayed the more the whole thing would build itself around my body instead of what I'd actually said.

It was, I will tell you, an undignified way to travel. Your stomach does not care that you understand matter.

*

The Ascension.

I can see you bracing for theology. Don't. It was a departure. That's all it was, and that's everything it was.

Rome would have come for me again — a man doesn't walk out of a government execution without the government noticing, and the notices were already being written. If I'd stayed, there would have been another arrest, another spectacle, and eventually they'd have gotten it right. The thread trick works once. Men learn.

And the movement — my people, the ones who'd stayed — they needed me gone. Not dead-gone. Gone-gone. A departure they could witness and remember, on my terms, instead of another arrest, another trial, another morning.

So I took them up the hill, the ones who were closest, and I told them what I needed to tell them, which was mostly: you'll be fine, stop looking at the sky, get to work.

And then I left the way I could leave.

The lift is the death-sleep in reverse. Instead of sinking below the body's business, I rose out of its reach. The One knew matter — knew it the way you know your own breathing — and in Perfect Harmony that knowing was mine. I loosened. That's the plainest word. I loosened my body's argument with the ground.

It took concentration. It wasn't effortless. The wind came up around me and the ground fell away and my stomach did the thing stomachs do, and I rose above the hill, and kept rising, and the people got smaller, and I kept going until the haze took me and I was out of their sight.

They stood there staring at the sky for a long time. I know, because I could still see them getting smaller until I couldn't.

I went east, toward the hills, and came down a long way off where no one was watching, and walked the rest of the way out like any other traveler on any other road.

It happened. It was physical. It was also just a man leaving.

*

Warren stopped.

Mina was staring at him.

"You levitated," she said.

"Yes."

"Explain it."

"I can't. Not the way you mean." He rubbed the back of his neck. "The One knows matter the way your hands know dough — no, that's too small. He knows what things are made of the way you know what you're thinking. And in Perfect Harmony, that knowing was mine too. So I slowed my heart until a Roman was satisfied I was dead, and later I loosened the ground's hold until I was out of sight. Same knowing. Different direction."

"Could you do it now?"

"Yes."

"Have you? Since?"

"Not in a long time." A pause. "People notice."

"Did it scare you? The first time."

"The slowing, yes. The lifting, no — by then it was just the only door left." He looked at her. "You're taking this very calmly for a scientist."

"I'm not calm," she said. "I'm deciding how many follow-up questions I get before you start deflecting, and spending them carefully."

"How many do I get?"

"Two." She held up her fingers. "One: the scars. You kept the scars."

"Yes."

"Why? If the Harmony could repair you — why keep the marks?"

Warren was quiet for a moment.

"Because it didn't unhappen me," he said. "That's the truest thing I know about Harmony, Mina. It doesn't erase. It carries. The body kept the record the same way I keep the memory. I don't know if that's a rule or just what happened. But it's what happened."

She nodded slowly. "Two: Mary. She was first. Before anyone else believed any of it — she saw you first."

"Yes."

"That's hers. Not the church's, not the tradition's. Hers."

"It was hers," he said. "It was real before it was a story. I need you to know that — especially now, knowing what you know about her. About all of it."

Mina looked at him for a long time.

Then: "That's the last one, isn't it. The last Yeshua story."

Warren didn't answer right away.

"Yes," he said finally. "That's all of it. That's the last one I have. I've told you everything about that life now."

The weight of it sat between them. Two thousand years, told out, and now there was nothing left in that direction — only the present, and whatever came next, and the woman sitting across from him who knew all of it and was still there.

Mina reached over and took his hand.

"Thank you," she said, "for telling me."

"It was yours to know," he said.

They sat like that, and the evening went on around them, and neither of them reached for the notebook.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Three: The Threshold

41_the_threshold.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

Mina came up the back stairs of Vasko Hardware after closing, the way she had been doing for weeks, and found Warren sitting on the floor with his back against the wall, reading something on his phone with the expression of a man watching a slow leak.

"Denny's radio finally died?" she asked.

"He's letting it rest." Warren set the phone down. "It's earned it."

The apartment was the same small inventory it had always been: one chair, the bed, the table, the little kitchen counter, the cheap lamp throwing its apologetic light, the curtain that had never once fit the window it was supposed to cover. Her business card still stood propped on the windowsill where she'd left it the first week — Minerva Mendez, Clinical Genetics, Elliott Bay Regional Medical Center — a tiny flag planted in occupied territory.

Mina took off her shoes and sat on her patch of floor by the window. The smooth river stone was there, where she'd left it. She picked it up and turned it over in her palm, the way she'd been doing for weeks, and didn't pretend she didn't know why she was here.

"I've met Mary," she said.

Warren waited.

"I've met the Eternal, more or less. I've watched you die." She looked at him. "What's left is the thing we've been circling since the scarf."

"The scarf was weeks ago."

"The scarf was the end of my plausible deniability."

He almost smiled. It didn't reach anywhere. He was watching her the way he'd watched her since the night she'd kissed him — the way a man watches a door he has decided not to open, standing close enough to feel the heat through it.

"You know what stopped me last time," he said.

"Yes."

"Nothing about that has gotten simpler. If anything—"

"I know."

"Mina."

She set the stone down. "I'm going to say this plainly, because everything else about this has been ridiculous. I want you. Not the Eternal. Not the history. You. The man who washes mugs like he's defusing them and argues with Denny about drill bits." She held his eyes. "I am asking. Explicitly. No ambiguity for you to hide behind."

The room went very quiet. Downstairs, something in the hardware store ticked as it cooled.

Warren stood up. He crossed the three steps to where she sat and went down on one knee in front of her, which was absurd and formal and completely him, and put his hand on the side of her face like it was the first time he'd ever been allowed to do it on purpose.

"Then yes," he said.

It started the way everything with them started: her, deciding, and him finally permitted to stop pretending he hadn't already decided months ago.

There was nothing hesitant about him this time. Restraint, Mina had learned, was not the absence of appetite. It was appetite with its hand over its own mouth. Now the hand came away.

His mouth found hers, and then her throat, and then the loose neckline of her blouse, and Mina discovered that fifty-one years of knowing exactly what her body was for had not prepared her for the experience of being paid attention to by someone who had spent two thousand years learning how to notice things.

She had worried, in some unexamined corner, about the logistics. The bed was narrow. The room was small. She was fifty-one and he looked it and they were both, technically, middle-aged people in a room above a hardware store.

It turned out not to matter. What mattered was weight, and breath, and the specific heat of his hands through fabric and then without it, and the sound he made — quiet, wrecked, honest — when she pulled him down to her.

Clothes became a negotiation they conducted without words. Her scarf ended up on the floor again, and this time neither of them picked it up, and Mina laughed against his mouth because some jokes only got better.

His hands learned her quickly. That was the unnerving part — not speed, exactly, but attention. He touched her the way he did everything he'd decided mattered: completely, without waste, as though he'd been studying for this in secret for months and was finally allowed to take the exam. She had lived in her body for fifty-one years without needing anyone's assessment of it, and now here was all of it, noticed, and the noticing was itself a kind of touch.

She arched into him. The bed was narrow and they kept nearly falling off it and neither of them cared, and at one point his knee found the floor and he didn't adjust, just stayed there, half-kneeling, because moving would have meant stopping.

"Mina." Her name, wrecked. "Tell me—"

"Don't stop."

"I need to know you're—"

"I am here. I am exactly here."

Then the humor changed, the way it had before. Deepened. Went somewhere past language.

And that was when she felt it.

Not a thought. A turning.

Something vast and very old turning toward her — toward them — the way a person turns toward a voice they love in another room.

Beneath the room, under the cheap lamp and the badly fitted curtain and the whole ordinary inventory of the evening, another awareness opened. She knew Warren was there without seeing him. Not his body. Him. The way she had once before, except this time it was stronger, and at the edges of it there were flickers of something older — not her memories. Lamplight on stone. Dust in a doorway. Olive oil and sweat and a handwriting she almost recognized, the loops of it, the hurry. Someone else's grief, worn smooth as the river stone, arriving inside her like weather. And underneath all of it, vast and patient and unbearably fond, a second attention turning toward her the way the sun turns toward nothing in particular and warms it anyway.

She gasped.

Warren went rigid.

He knew what it was before she did. She saw it happen — the recognition crossing him a full second ahead of her understanding, the way he'd always been one step ahead of every impossible thing, except this time the step was a wall.

"Mina."

"What—"

"We stop."

She stared at him. "You have got to be kidding me."

"I'm not."

"Warren."

He pulled back. Not away — back. His hands left her with visible effort, and he sat up, breathing hard, and looked at her with an expression she had never seen on him: not restraint, not patience. Alarm.

"Talk," she said. "Now. Because I am extremely close to throwing something."

"She knows me." His voice was rough. "The Eternal. She's known me longer than your species has had writing, and she is reaching for me right now, through you — not around you, through — and if we keep going, what happens next won't be an accident and it won't be your choice. It will be two thousand years of that bond answering itself, and I will not let it choose for you."

The fury arrived first, exactly on schedule.

"Do you have any idea," Mina said, sitting up, dragging her blouse back into place with more force than it required, "how tired I am of the universe putting consent forms between me and everything I want? I waited months. I did the ethical thing. I faced Mary. I faced the execution. I asked you explicitly, and now you're telling me there's a line I can't see and I crossed it by wanting you too much?"

"Yes."

"That's insane."

"I know."

She stood up. Paced the three available steps. Picked up the river stone and put it down again.

"Is it her?" she asked finally. "Am I feeling her?"

"You're feeling the approach. She's close to the surface in you — she's always been close in you, that's what a seer is — and the One in me recognized her, and they started toward each other." He rubbed his face. "It's the oldest thing I know. Older than Terra. And it almost made your decision for you just now, while you were underneath me, and that is the one thing I won't survive doing to you."

"That's very noble."

"It's very selfish, actually. If I let that happen, I could never be sure you chose me. I'd spend the next thousand years wondering."

"Oh, good," Mina said. "Selfish I can work with. Noble makes me tired."

And then, underneath the fury, the understanding arrived — because she had felt it too, the turning, the vast fond attention, and she knew what it would have been like to be carried past the line by something that loved her and didn't ask. She had spent her whole professional life obtaining informed consent from people about to have their genomes read back to them. She knew exactly what a signature extracted mid-pleasure would be worth.

She hated it. She also knew he was right.

Both things fit in the room. Neither one moved over for the other.

"Fine," she said. "Fine. You're right and I hate it. Those are both true."

Warren let out a breath. "I know."

"Don't be gracious about it."

"I'm trying very hard not to touch you right now. Gracious is the best I can do."

She almost laughed. She didn't want to. It happened anyway, the way it always did with him — fury and laughter, grief and jokes, stacked on top of each other like dishes.

"What were the flickers?" she asked. "At the edges. Lamplight. Dust. Whose were they?"

Warren was quiet for a moment. "Hers," he said. "Not yours. Not yet." He looked at the curtain, the way it hung wrong on the rod, as if it had personally offended him. "That's the part of tonight's talk I'm not going to preview. You get the whole thing, in order, or I start skipping and you catch me skipping and we fight about that instead."

"That is irritatingly self-aware."

"Two thousand years. You pick things up."

She sat back down on her patch of floor. After a moment, he sat beside her. Close. Not touching. The scarf lay on the floor between them like a small white flag, and neither of them picked it up, and that felt like its own kind of honesty.

For a while they just sat. The lamp hummed. Downstairs the building settled. Her heart was still going too fast, and his breathing was still uneven, and the ordinary physical facts of it — two middle-aged people on a floor, half-dressed, furious and tender at the same time — struck her as the most honest thing that had happened all evening.

"You stopped us in time," she said. "That's the part I'm going to remember when I'm less angry. That you could feel it coming and you stopped us in time."

"I've had practice stopping."

"I know. I watched you do it with a kiss once." She picked up the river stone, turned it over. "Warren."

"What."

"Thank you. Don't let it go to your head."

His hand found hers on the floor between them. Just the hand. That was all, and it was enough, and it wasn't, and that was the whole problem in miniature.

"Then talk," Mina said. "All of it. Tonight. No more 'there's more to Harmony than I've told you.' I've paid for the whole explanation. I want the whole explanation."

Warren looked at the curtain, the lamp, her card on the windowsill. Then back at her.

"Yes," he said. "Tonight."

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Four: The Full Disclosure

42_the_full_disclosure.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

They got dressed. That took longer than it should have, because getting dressed after not-finishing was its own small ceremony of frustration, and because Warren kept finding reasons to hand her things.

Then Mina sat on her patch of floor by the window, wrapped the scarf around her shoulders like armor, and said, "Start at the beginning. And don't be coy. I'm fifty-one, I'm furious, and I have questions stacked up like planes over Sea-Tac."

Warren took the chair. The cheap lamp made a halo of his hair, which was unfair, and he knew it was unfair, and he sat there anyway.

"Everything," he said. "In order. Souls first."

"Go."

"An Eternal is a consciousness whose first memory is the thought I am." He said it the way you'd state a street address. "That's the whole foundation. Thought is fundamental to reality — not an accident of brains, not a trick meat plays. The first thing any of us remembers is being aware of being. I am."

"And before that."

"Nothing. Not darkness, not waiting. A wall. Every Eternal has the same wall in the same place. We've been arguing about what's on the other side since before your species had fire, and we haven't gotten one inch closer."

"So no one made you."

"The One didn't make the others. Nobody made anybody, as far as any of us can remember. We arrived. Then we found each other." He leaned forward, elbows on knees. "I am was first. You are took longer — learning that another consciousness is real and not just a problem you've already solved. Then we are. Then Harmony."

"Union without erasure," Mina said. "I've heard the slogan."

"You've watched it work. Your mother." He held up a hand before she could answer. "Hold that. It matters later."

"Fine. Keep going. What goes wrong."

"Memory. Coherence." Warren's voice went flatter, the way it did when the subject was old pain. "Over enough time, an Eternal starts to lose pieces of itself. Not like forgetting where you put your keys. Like forgetting you ever had hands. It compounds. Without recovery, an Eternal can come apart — less and less of itself, until there's barely an I am left to do the losing."

"That's horrifying."

"It is. And then humanity showed up."

Mina's eyes narrowed. "Doing what, exactly."

"Being conscious. A human being's first real self-awareness recreates the essential experience — a fresh I am, brand new, intact. And it turns out a fractured Eternal joined to a compatible human can use that. Borrow the coherence. Ride along while the human lives a whole life, thinking its own thoughts, being its own person, and across that lifetime the Eternal recovers memory, recovers itself."

"So it's symbiotic."

"When it works. The human isn't a battery, Mina. The human is a person, and the Eternal knows it, or the whole thing fails. That's the you are part. You can't use someone who isn't real to you."

"And when the human dies."

"The sufficiently restored Eternal may leave Terra rather than take another life. The badly fractured ones need more lives. Many more, sometimes." He paused. "This is the thing later cultures misread as reincarnation. It isn't the same human personality coming back. It's two distinct consciousnesses, and one of them is healing."

"Two consciousnesses," Mina said slowly. "Not one wearing the other like a coat."

"Never like a coat."

"Good. Because I have follow-up questions about coats." She turned the river stone over in her palm. "Terra. You've said that word like it's a place and a project. Which is it."

"Both. Terra is where the broken ones come to get fixed." Warren stood up, which meant he was getting to the part he didn't like sitting still for. "The restoration — the joining, the healing across human lives — it happens here. This world. Whether it happens anywhere else, whether there are other worlds doing the same work, whether new Eternals can arise somewhere we haven't looked — I don't know. If this is the only place, the work could run for a trillion years. Bookkeeping, not poetry."

"And Sol." Mina watched him. "You've been avoiding Sol since the night you told me about the unanswered question. Stop avoiding Sol."

Warren stopped pacing.

"Your Eternal," he said, "is one of the most intact surviving Eternals. Nearly as much ancient memory as the One. And once, a very long time ago, she did something almost none of us have ever done." He came back and sat down, which told Mina this part cost him more than the pacing part. "She entered Harmony with a forming star. Not a human. Not an Eternal. A star."

Mina waited.

"Harmony that complete, between a whole Eternal and a burning thing — she participated in the formation of the star itself. It has a name because she named it."

"Sol."

"Sol."

"Sol," Mina said, "like the sun."

"Not the sun."

"Please tell me my Soul didn't make the sun."

"She didn't make the sun."

"But she helped make a star." Mina stared at him. "Do I get visitation rights?"

"Sol is a star, Mina. It doesn't do visitation."

"I'm just saying. Family's complicated."

A corner of his mouth moved. "In the loosest possible sense."

Warren's expression did something complicated. "After Sol — after she knew what Harmony could do at that scale — she called the One to Terra." He met her eyes. "She's the intact one, Mina. That's why you're a seer. That's why she's still here, still whole enough to be close — close enough that you feel her at the edges. Most Eternals in humans are fragments, whispers. Yours is nearly all there."

"And she called the One to Terra." Not a question.

"Yes."

"Your Eternal. The one inside you right now. She called him."

"She did."

Mina was quiet for a long moment. The lamp hummed. Somewhere downstairs, a pipe knocked once, like the building clearing its throat.

"Say the God part," she said. "Plainly. No coy."

Warren nodded. "The being ancient Israel experienced as God — the voice, the law, the presence — was an Eternal. The One. The most intact of us. The one who remembers his own first I am clearest."

"And then."

"Then, much later, that same Eternal joined a human life from its beginning. A child in Galilee. Yeshua." He said the name the way you'd set down something breakable.

"Why?"

"Because after all those centuries of speaking at humans, guiding them, correcting them, deciding for them, the One finally understood that knowing more about people wasn't the same as understanding what it was to be one. Joining Yeshua was deliberate. He wanted to stop reaching humanity only from the outside and live a human life from the inside. He didn't know what that would change. Neither did Yeshua."

Warren looked down at his hands. "It changed both of them. And the two of them — human and Eternal, both fully real, both fully themselves — reached a Harmony nobody had ever managed. Perfect Harmony. Unprecedented. I'm what's left of that. Both of them. The man and the Eternal, harmonized, continuing."

"So when people called you God—"

"They were talking to the One. And the One is in me, the way your Eternal is in you, except our Harmony is complete and always has been, since his first breath." Warren's voice stayed level. "But Mina. He is not the Creator."

"The unanswered question."

"The unanswered question. I know an enormous amount about how the Universe works. I don't know what was before my first I am. I don't know whether something made us. The Eternals say the Universe instead of the Creator because we won't claim certainty we don't have." He looked at her directly. "I can tell you the mechanism of everything. I can't tell you the purpose of any of it. If there's an Almighty above all of this, I've never met it, and neither has anyone I know."

Mina absorbed that. "The lineage, then. Abraham. All of it. You were running a program."

It wasn't a question, but Warren answered it like one he'd been dreading.

"Compatibility is heritable. We learned that early. And the One needed — needs — a human lineage capable of sustaining an intact Eternal without fracturing across a lifetime. So yes. Guidance. Over generations. Nudges, protections, interventions. Mixed motives, start to finish — we needed humanity for our own survival, and deep Harmony genuinely benefits the human, and both of those things were true at the same time."

"That's a breeding program, Warren."

"I know what it looks like."

"Do you know what it is?"

He was silent for a moment. "It was necessary. I'm not proud of all of it. The lineage isn't evidence of superiority — it's a compatibility history, nothing more, and anyone who tells you otherwise is selling something." His jaw tightened. "You want the ugly part? The ugly part is that real people lived real lives inside a project they didn't consent to, because the alternative was the One fracturing and taking the whole restoration effort down with him. I made that trade. I'd make it again. You get to decide what that makes me."

Mina looked at him for a long time.

"Noted," she said. "Filed. We'll come back to it." She picked up the stone again. "Everything you just said might be true, Warren. Some of it is still monstrous. I'm allowed to hold both of those."

"You are."

"The bond. You said the One loves my Eternal most. Say that part plainly too."

"He loves all Souls. He loves yours most." Warren said it simply, the way you'd say water is wet. "It predates Terra. Predates everything we've been talking about. Before the fracture, before the restoration, before humanity — there was that. Two consciousnesses that found each other at the beginning of things and never — " He stopped. Started again. "It's the oldest relationship I know of. It's why, tonight, when she reached — " He gestured at the space between them. "They know each other, Mina. The way you know your own heartbeat."

"And Mary." Her voice was steady. "Where does Mary sit in all this. Exactly."

"Mary was a human being. A complete I am. Brilliant, difficult, funny — you met her in the stories. She wasn't a vessel and she wasn't a costume. She was a person, and your Eternal joined with her, the way your Eternal is joined with you now, and they had years."

"And you loved her."

"Yes."

"And the Eternal."

"Yes. As herself. She's — " Warren searched for words, and for once the two thousand years didn't help. "She's the closest thing I have to a contemporary. Everyone else I knew at the beginning is fractured or gone. She's still her."

"And me."

Warren looked at her. Really looked — the way he had the night she'd made him say who he was looking at.

"You," he said. "Mina Mendez. Fifty-one. Genetics scientist. Terrible at sitting with a feeling, excellent at running a dataset, carries a river stone around like a rosary." His voice dropped. "I didn't go looking for the Eternal in you. I found you, and the rest of it was already there, the way the foundation is already under a house. I love the foundation. I live in the house."

Mina's eyes were bright. She refused to do anything about it.

"That was almost a good metaphor."

"It was the best I had."

"Don't oversell it." She stood up, paced to the window, looked out at the dark street. Her card stood on the sill, ridiculous and brave. "Seers. You keep using that word. What am I, exactly, in your taxonomy."

"Not a taxonomy. An observation." Warren leaned back in the chair. "A seer is a human whose Eternal is unusually intact and unusually close to the surface. Close enough that Soul-memory leaks. Dreams that aren't dreams. Recognitions you can't source. Your readings, your rituals, the practices you've built over the years — those aren't magic, Mina. They're instruments. You built yourself a set of human interfaces for noticing resonance, the way a radio is an interface for noticing a signal that was already there."

"So I'm not psychic."

"You're well-instrumented."

"I like that better." She turned around. "It also means I've been doing science this whole time and nobody told me."

"You've been doing science this whole time."

"Don't agree so fast. I was enjoying being wronged."

He almost smiled. Then the smile faded, because they were coming to the part neither of them had been circling.

"Full Harmony," Mina said. "What it would actually do. To me. Specifically."

Warren nodded slowly. "Your Eternal is intact. Complete, or near enough that the difference barely matters. Full Harmony with her would restore her complete available memory — to you. Not as stories I tell you. As yours."

"Define complete."

"Her ancient existence. Before Terra. Sol — you'd know Sol the way you know your own childhood. The bond with the One — you'd feel it from the inside, not as something I'm describing." He paused. "And Mary. Her life. Her years with Yeshua. All of it."

Mina sat down very slowly on her patch of floor.

"Say the ugly part," she said.

"You'd remember loving him. As Mary. A love older than your species' writing, and it would be inside you, and it wouldn't be yours, and you would still be you." Warren's hands were flat on his knees. "That's the cost, Mina. Not a trick, not a metaphor. You would never again be able to be only Mina Mendez. You'd be Mina Mendez with the whole of it behind her. I won't pretend that's small."

She was quiet. The lamp hummed. The curtain hung wrong on its rod.

"I watched it not erase my mother," she said finally.

"Yes."

"Three consciousnesses. All still themselves."

"That's what Harmony is. That's what it has to be, or it's just possession wearing a better name." He leaned forward. "You saw the proof with your own eyes. I'm not asking you to take it on faith."

"What could I do." Not a question, quite. "After. If I chose it."

Warren chose his words with visible care. "You'd be powerful. And inexperienced. Those two things together are — " He stopped. "You'd have her knowledge. Her memory. An instinctive relationship to matter and reality that you don't have words for yet, because the words are hers and you haven't met them. Real physical manipulation — real — but limited at first. It takes learning. Practice. I've had two thousand years of embodied practice at being what I am. You'd be starting."

"So not you-equivalent."

"Not even close, at first. Maybe never the same shape. You're not me; she's not the One. It would be its own thing." He met her eyes. "I could teach you. If you wanted. Slowly. The way you'd teach anyone something dangerous — which it is."

"Dangerous."

"Power always is. I'm not going to dress it up."

Mina nodded, filing that. "Lifespan."

"Full Harmony would maintain and repair your body — the way it maintained your mother's, those three days, except yours wouldn't stop." He said it plainly, the way you'd read a chart. "I won't give you a number, because I don't have an honest one. Healthy for a very long time. But mortal, Mina. You'd still be mortal. Harmony extends the warranty; it doesn't void death." He caught her look. "And that's not the same thing as what happened to me. Mine is older and stranger, and it isn't the standard package — don't file two thousand years under what Harmony does."

"One more bodily consequence," Warren said, "and it has to be on the table. Harmony maintains the body. All of it. That includes fertility — your body would return to full function, not a fifty-one-year-old's version of it."

Mina stared at him. "You're telling me my eggs would un-retire."

"I'm telling you the mechanism doesn't do partial. It's a consequence, not a promise and not a plan. But you get to know it before you choose, not after."

"Filed," Mina said. "Under: things I'll decide what to feel about later."

"And the other thing." She looked at him. "The immortality thing. The separate thing."

"Separate." Warren's voice went careful. "What I could do — what only I currently know how to do — is a deliberate genetic intervention. In your lab. On your body. It would stop the aging, not just slow it. It requires your informed consent, separately, and I won't fold it into tonight because it isn't part of tonight." He paused. "And I won't sell it to you. Everyone else keeps dying, Mina. Perla. Your daughter. Everyone you know. I know exactly what that costs, because I've paid it more times than I can count, and I won't pretend it's only upside."

"Noted." She studied him. "Why do you want this. The Harmony. Say it ugly."

He was quiet long enough that she thought he might actually dodge. He didn't.

"I want to stop being alone with the memory." The words came out flat, unadorned. "Two thousand years, Mina. I want to be known by someone who remembers — who knows, the way I know — what it was. Yeshua. Mary. All of it. Not as stories. As memory." He looked at his hands. "That's selfish. It's true and it's selfish. And it's why I keep stopping us — because wanting something the way Voss wanted his daughter back doesn't entitle me to arrange for it. That's the difference between me and him. Or it had better be."

"And there's the other fear. The one with a name." His voice went quieter. "Tamar taught me what it costs to not be there. I wasn't home when she needed me, and I have never once been able to prove to myself it wouldn't have mattered. I'm not asking you for anything. I'm telling you the fear is sitting at this table too, so it doesn't ambush us later."

"Then it's on the table," Mina said. "It doesn't get to vote."

Mina let that sit. Then: "Perla."

Warren exhaled. "Perla."

"She's going to die."

"Yes." He said it without flinching, which was its own mercy. "Not soon. Not the way we thought. What happened in those three days was real, and it's durable. Three days is about the practical limit — the body can't go without water indefinitely — but in that interval her Soul received massive healing. A Soul healed that deeply maintains and repairs the body for a very long time. The old stories about people living centuries aren't myths, Mina. They're this phenomenon, remembered at a distance." He met Mina's eyes. "I won't put a number on it. But this isn't some extra years. She's mortal, Mina — on a long horizon, she's mortal. Harmony gave her back her life; it didn't make her eternal."

"So she's the argument."

"She's the clearest argument I've ever seen for what a finite life is worth." Warren's voice was quiet. "Fully lived. Fully hers. Ending."

Mina looked out the window at the dark street. Her card stood on the sill. After a while she said, "That's the part that's going to keep me up."

"I know."

"Not the cosmology. Not Mary. That."

"I know."

She turned back. "The unknowns. Give me the list. The real one."

"What Eternals were before the first I am. Whether Terra is the only place the restoration happens. Whether every fractured Soul gets recovered or the supply ends. What happens afterward. Whether any of it was designed or whether we're all benefiting from an accident." He counted them the way he'd counted them before, on his fingers, without looking. "I don't know. Any of it. I've had longer than anyone to think about it, and I don't know."

"Good," Mina said. "I mean — good that you say it. Most men would have invented something by now."

"Most men haven't had two thousand years to learn how that ends."

She stood up. Paced the three steps. Came back. Sat down again, closer this time, her knee nearly touching his.

"Let me make sure I have it," she said. "Souls are real, distinct consciousnesses. Mine is ancient and intact and once entered Harmony with a forming star, which means the sun is basically family. The God of Israel was your Eternal, who is now you, and he is not the capital-G Creator, about whom you know nothing, which is the most honest theology I've ever heard. Your Eternal loves my Eternal most, and that's older than dirt. I'm a seer because she's close to the surface and I built good instruments. Full Harmony gives me her whole memory, including Mary, including loving Yeshua, and I stay me — I've seen it work. I'd be powerful and clumsy. I'd live a very long time and still die. The immortality thing is separate and we're not deciding it tonight. You want this partly because you're lonely, which is the most human thing you've said all evening. My mother is going to live a long time and then die, and that's the point. And nobody knows what any of it is for."

Warren stared at her.

"That's — " He stopped. "Yes. That's all of it."

"I'm a scientist," Mina said. "Pattern recognition is the job."

"The floor dropped away," he said quietly, "and then you rebuilt it."

"That's the job too." She looked at him. "I can choose now. That's what you wanted."

"That's what I wanted."

"I'm not choosing tonight."

"I know."

She leaned her head against his shoulder. He went still for half a second — then his arm came around her, careful, deliberate, chosen.

They sat like that while the lamp hummed and the curtain hung wrong and her card stood on the windowsill, a tiny flag in occupied territory.

"Tomorrow," Mina said, "you're teaching me fitted sheets."

"I already taught you fitted sheets."

"Then tomorrow you're teaching me something else."

"Name it."

She was quiet for a moment. Then, very softly: "How to be brave about this."

Warren held her a little tighter.

"We'll get there," he said.
