Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Five: Mina's Choice

43_mias_choice.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

The disclosure took most of the night.

Sol. Terra. The Seers — her own bloodline, the reason she could feel things no instrument could measure, the reason the Eternal in her had stayed so close to the surface all her life. What Harmony would restore: all of it, everything the Soul had available, Mary's life and Sol's light and two thousand years of Warren's memory-adjacent history, laid open inside her own skull. What it would give her: access, instinct, a relationship with matter she'd have to learn the way a child learns hands. What it might do to her body: slow the clock, not stop it — probably a long life, healthy, nobody could promise the number; full restoration, fertility included, filed under things she'd decide the meaning of later. And the other thing, the separate thing: what Warren could do with her lab and a deliberate hand, the immortality that had nothing to do with Harmony and everything to do with consent. And his selfishness, which he said plainly this time, without dressing. And Tamar — the fear with a name, on the table, not voting. And Perla — her mother alive in the world, likely to stay that way a long while, and the fact of that sitting in the room like a chair nobody needed to mention.

Mina took notes. Actual notes, on the back of a Denny's receipt, because she was who she was and nobody was going to change that at this point.

Then she went home, and slept badly, and came back the next evening with the receipt folded in her pocket and the red streak in her bangs freshly touched up, which Warren noticed and did not comment on, which she appreciated and also resented a little, because she was in no mood to be let off easy.

"I've been doing the thing where I pretend I'm deciding," she said, sitting on her patch of floor. "I'm not. I decided around three in the morning. What I've been doing is checking my own work."

Warren was sitting across from her, back against the wall, hands empty. "Then I'm listening."

"I'm going to tell you the reasoning, because you don't get to just receive the answer. The answer without the reasoning is a coin flip."

"Understood."

"First." She held up a finger. "The data. I watched it work on my mother. One data point, uncontrolled, witnessed by a daughter with a massive conflict of interest — I know the limitations. But it's the only experiment of this kind ever run in front of a clinician, and the outcome was unambiguous: Harmony did not erase her. That's a fact. Not a promise about texture. A fact about structure."

"Second." Another finger. "The alternative to knowing is staying afraid of my own interior, and I am fifty-one years old and I have spent enough of my life being managed by fear wearing a lab coat. The thing inside me has been carrying memory older than language, and it has been with me, Warren — not beside me, with me — for fifty-one years, and I refuse to die a stranger to it. That's not curiosity. That's not even science. That's just — " She searched. "You don't leave a houseguest in the dark for fifty-one years without ever learning their name. It's rude."

The corner of his mouth moved. "She's not a houseguest."

"You know what I mean."

"I do."

"Third." She put her hand down. "Mary. I want to meet her — not become her, not borrow her, meet her. She's been loving you for two thousand years through that Soul, and nobody alive has ever looked at that love from the inside. I can. And I think — " Her voice did something she didn't authorize, and she cleared it, annoyed. "I think she'd want a witness. Not a replacement. A witness."

Warren was quiet.

"And fourth, the one that's actually the decision." Mina looked at him directly. "I refuse to be a vessel. That is a condition, not a preference. What goes into Harmony is Mina Mendez, fifty-one, genetics scientist, Seer, terrible at parallel parking, and what comes out is still Mina Mendez. If your Soul tries to overwrite me, I will be extremely unpleasant about it, and I expect you to back me up."

"I will."

"Say it like you mean it."

"I will back you up," Warren said, "against everything, including myself, including her."

She nodded. "Then yes."

He didn't move. Didn't smile. Just sat there looking at her, and after a moment she realized he was doing the thing again — the containment thing, the appetite with its hand over its own mouth, except this wasn't appetite.

"What," she said.

"I need to tell you the rest of the exits. Not because you're missing one. Because I need them said."

"Fine."

"You can say no now. You can say no tomorrow. You can stop it in the middle — I'll pull us both out. You can decide in a year that it was a mistake, and I will not argue with you, and I will not — " He stopped. "Harmony is not a condition of anything. Not of us. Not of this." He gestured at the room, the bed, the whole wreckage of the last two nights. "If you walked out of here and never chose it, nothing between us changes. That's not a promise I'm making to be generous. It's a structural fact. I need you to have it in your hands."

"I have it."

"You don't get to decide by telling me what I want to hear."

Mina stared at him. "Warren. I've spent the last month being told I might be the reincarnation of your dead girlfriend, watching my mother nearly die and then get up and ask about her plants, sitting through an explanation of the universe that broke three of my assumptions before breakfast. Do I look like someone who tells people what they want to hear?"

"No."

"Then hear me. I am saying yes."

He let out a breath. "All right."

"That's it? All right?"

"I'm trying not to persuade you by reacting."

"You're allowed to react. You're just not allowed to aim the reaction."

He almost laughed. Then didn't. "There's something else."

"Of course there is."

"I'm afraid." He said it flatly, the way he said things that cost him. "Not of it failing. Of it working."

"Say more."

"Two thousand years, Mina. Nobody has known me — actually known me — since." He looked at the curtain, the way it hung wrong. "Being unknowable is a habit. It's also a shelter. If you Harmonize, you'll have Mary's memories of Yeshua from the inside. You'll know what his laugh sounded like in a room that isn't this one. And I'm afraid that afterward, when you look at me, you'll see him. Only him. And the man who washes mugs like he's defusing them will be — " He stopped. "A footnote. A vessel with a better kitchen."

Mina was quiet for a long moment.

"Come here," she said.

He crossed the floor and sat beside her. Close. She took his hand — allowed, this time, both of them knowing it was allowed — and held it in both of hers.

"Listen to me, Yeshua-who-washes-mugs." She waited until he looked at her. "I am going to remember your jokes from two thousand years ago. That's going to happen. And then I am going to come back to this room, and you are going to be sitting on this floor being an idiot about a curtain, and I am going to know both things, and neither one is going to cancel the other. That's the whole structure. That's what I watched my mother do. Three different true things. None of them canceling."

"That's the theory."

"That's the data." She squeezed his hand. "And for the record — the man I kissed first was not Yeshua. He was a man standing at a kitchen counter being wrong about wine. I have the sequence. I checked my work."

He laughed, finally, a real one, short and wrecked. "Fitted sheets."

"See? That's mine. Yeshua never said fitted sheets. I know, because I checked." She let go of his hand. "Now. Before we do anything else, I'm calling my mother."

"Now?"

"Yes, now. She's going to want to be the one sitting in the chair, and she's going to fight you about the breathing notes, and I want her to have time to win that fight before tomorrow."

Perla answered on the second ring. In the background: a television, low, and the particular clatter of a kitchen being used by someone who had opinions about it.

"Ma. It's me."

"I know it's you. The phone tells me." A pause. "You're calling at this hour, so it's decided."

Mina blinked. "How — "

"You get a voice when you've decided something. You had it when you were nine and you told the dentist you were switching to the other one. I'm your mother. I have a dataset."

"Ma."

"Are you scared?"

Mina looked at Warren. "Terrified."

"Good." Perla said it like a diagnosis confirmed. "That means it's your choice and not the fear talking. Fear makes the sound different. I know both sounds."

"I want you there. In the chair."

"I was already going to be. I already told Denny I'd need a ride at nine." Another pause, and the television went quiet. "Mija. The thing inside you has been waiting a very long time to be properly met. You don't leave someone waiting. That's all. That's the whole of my advice."

"That's good advice."

"I know. I'm a retired teacher. Go to sleep. Both of you. You sound exhausted."

Mina hung up and sat there holding the phone.

"She's already arranged a ride," Warren said.

"She's Perla."

They sat for a while. The lamp hummed. Downstairs the building settled into its night sounds.

"Tomorrow," Mina said finally. "Like my mother. Three days, your note, the whole thing."

"Three days," Warren said. "Maybe less. Your Soul isn't fractured — there's nothing to gather. It's more like — " He searched. "Like opening a door that's been closed a long time. The room behind it is intact. It's just been dark."

"And you'll be in there with me."

"The whole time."

"And if I want out."

"I bring you out." Simple. "That's the job."

Mina nodded. Stood. Picked up the river stone from the windowsill and turned it over in her palm, the way she'd been doing for weeks — except this time it felt different, weighted, like it had been waiting too.

"Collateral," she said, and put it in her pocket.

"You stole that already."

"I un-stole it. I put it back." She looked at him. "I'm taking it again. For the duration."

"Highway robbery."

"Bill me."

She went to the door. Stopped. Looked back at him sitting on the floor in the lamplight, two thousand years old, afraid, staying anyway.

"Warren."

"What."

"Thank you for being afraid. It would have been worse if you weren't."

He nodded. Didn't try to dress it.

She left him there, and the choice stayed made behind her — not a door left open this time, but one walked through, deliberately, with the receipt still in her pocket and her mother's voice in her ear and the stone warm in her hand.

Tomorrow, the door would open from the other side.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Six: Full Harmony

44_full_harmony.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

They did it in Warren's apartment, because Mina refused to be anywhere else and Perla refused to let her do it without a proper chair, so Denny carried up the good armchair from the hardware store office at eight in the morning, complaining the whole way, and set it down by the window like he was delivering a throne.

Warren had told them twice that Full Harmony did not require a watcher. If anything went wrong, he would know before Perla did. Mina had told him twice that her mother was staying anyway. Perla, when informed that this was not medically necessary, had said, "I know. I'm her mother. Different job."

"The note," Perla said to Warren, holding out her hand. "Let me see the note."

"It's the same note."

"Let me see it anyway."

Warren handed it over. Perla read it twice, folded it, put it in her apron pocket, and sat down in the armchair with her knitting — which she had taken up again, badly, in the last month, producing something that might have been a scarf — and looked at her daughter lying on the narrow bed.

"You look nervous," Perla said.

"I am nervous."

"Good. Nervous means you're paying attention." She picked up the knitting. "Go on, then. I'll watch the breathing. I have the note. I know what wrong-wrong looks like. I'm extremely qualified."

Mina lay back. Warren sat on the edge of the bed beside her, took her hand the way he had at her mother's kitchen table that first night, and said, to both of them, "Same as before. Whatever you see, I'm working. Especially when it looks like nothing's happening."

"Three days?" Mina asked.

"Maybe less." He looked at her. "Ready?"

She thought about the receipt in her pocket. The stone, warm in her fist. Her mother's voice: you don't leave someone waiting.

"Yes."

She closed her eyes. His closed a half-second after. And then there was nothing to do but what she'd done for her mother: stay.

Perla picked up her knitting. The lamp hummed. Downstairs, Denny's radio found a station and lost it — resurrected; Warren must have fixed it sometime in the last two days without mentioning it.

*

Inside, it was not dark the way the space between stars is dark.

Mina had expected that. She'd heard Warren describe it — the unlit dark, the fragments scattered like grief wearing the shape of geography. She'd braced for the labor of it, the reaching.

Instead there was light. Not bright — deep. The particular blue of water over a reef shelf, the color she'd seen once snorkeling off Guam as a girl, when her father had held her hands and told her to just look down and trust him. Light with weight to it. Light you could lean on.

And it was not empty, and it was not scattered, and it was not waiting to be gathered.

It was there. All of it. Whole.

Oh, Mina thought, with the part of her that was still a scientist making field notes at the edge of wonder, it's been here the whole time. Right under everything. Like a floor.

She said the only thing she knew how to say, the way Warren had taught her without teaching, the way every Eternal had said it at the beginning of everything:

I am.

And the vastness answered — immediately, completely, the way a held note answers a struck one —

You are.

Not a fragment. Not a piece choosing to come home. A whole presence, turning toward her with an attention so complete it felt like being read by someone who loved the book.

I know you, the presence said, without words, the way warmth says itself. Fifty-one years. I've been practicing your name since before you had teeth.

Mina's throat closed — she didn't have a throat, here, but something in her did the equivalent — because she'd spent her whole life thinking of the Soul as a passenger, a tenant, a mystery, and it had been, all along, the opposite of a stranger. It had watched her learn to parallel park. It had been there for every lab result she'd checked twice. It knew about the red streak before the red streak.

I thought you were far away, she said.

I was never far, it said. You were just looking outward. That's fine. That's what the young ones do.

The young ones. Said with such fondness that Mina — fifty-one, mortal, brand-new — felt, absurdly, like a child being welcomed home from her first day of school.

Then the memories began. Not all at once. The Soul was careful with her — she could feel the care, the deliberate gentleness, the way you'd hand someone a stack of letters oldest-first only when they'd sat down.

Mary came first. Of course Mary came first.

*

It arrived through the hands.

Miriam's hands — counting coins at a table in a lamplit room, quick and sure, the steward's face doing the thing men's faces did when they realized they'd been out-negotiated by a woman. The particular satisfaction of it, warm in the chest. Mina knew this memory. She'd heard this story. But hearing was a postcard; this was the weather.

Then the fire. The argument. Miriam challenging him in front of everyone, her heart hammering and her jaw set, because somebody had to say it and the men were all being idiots about it.

Then — deeper, the Soul turning the pages with terrible tenderness — the rest of it. Loving him. Not the stained-glass version. The actual thing: his laugh in a room with dust in the light. The way he said her name when he was tired. The particular grief of the cross, which arrived not as theology but as a body horror, a woman's knees in dirt, the worst day, the one the Soul had carried longest and most carefully.

Mina gasped — no lungs, but the equivalent — and the Soul held her through it, the way you'd hold someone's hand through a contraction.

This was hers, the Soul said. Not yours. You are allowed to witness it without carrying it.

But I feel it like it's mine.

You feel it like it's true. Those are different. You'll learn the difference. It takes practice. She had to learn it too, with mine.

And then, vertiginous and tender both at once, the strangest thing: Mina, from inside Mary's memory, looked at Yeshua — and loved him, the way Mary had, completely — and was simultaneously, undeniably, Mina Mendez, fifty-one, lying on a narrow bed above a hardware store, holding a river stone, loving a different man in a different century who happened to be the same continuing person.

Both true. Neither canceling.

Oh, Mina thought. Oh, that's how it works. It's not a palimpsest. It's a —

It's a shelf, the Soul said, amused. You shelve them side by side. You're a scientist. You know about shelving.

Mina laughed — no mouth, but the equivalent — and the vastness laughed with her, and for a moment the oldest friendship anything had felt exactly like what it was: two people who'd shared a body for fifty-one years finally introducing themselves properly.

Then the Soul turned older pages, and the laughter faded into awe, because awe was the closest word and it was the wrong size.

*

Sol.

Mina had no apparatus for this. The Soul knew it, and translated — that was the mercy of it, the whole enormous memory arriving not as raw cosmic data but through the particulars of her own life, the infinite arriving through the particular, not instead of it.

So: Sol arrived as warmth on her face through a kitchen window. As her mother's hands, floury, shaping dough. As the smell of pancit steam and vinegar, patient on a stove. As her father's voice on a boat off Guam, just look down and trust me. The Soul took the oldest light anything had and poured it through a fifty-one-year-old woman's sensory memory, and Mina understood — not as information, as recognition — what it had been to enter Harmony with a forming star, to lend her own I am to the making of Sol. To be known by something that burned. To call, across the dark before Terra had a name, to the One — to him, the oldest part of him — and be answered.

She saw it the way you see a thing in a dream that is truer than waking: the calling. Not words. The I-am reaching until something else had room to answer, and the answer coming, and the long journey after, and Terra, blue and unsuspecting, and the whole immense project of it — the fracture, the diminishment, the human lives, one after another, each one a small brave I-am offered up to hold a breaking thing together.

You called him, Mina said. Before writing. Before — everything.

I called, the Soul agreed. He came. He's always come. It's his best quality and his worst habit.

And Mary —

Mary was one life. A good one. A complete one. The Soul's attention warmed. You're another. Not a repetition. He'd be furious if you thought that. He was very clear with me about it, before we started. Unusually clear, for him.

Mina felt the tears anyway, and didn't bother with dignity. There was no one here to perform it for. There was only the oldest friend anyone had, holding her the way the reef water had held her when she was seven, saying just look down and trust me.

I'm afraid I'll lose the edges of me, Mina said. It was the last fear, the one from the floor of the apartment, the dilution question. She brought it here deliberately, the way you'd bring a wound to a doctor.

Look at your hands, the Soul said.

She did. They were her hands. Fifty-one years old. A small scar on the left thumb from a paring knife, 2019. The nails cut short because long nails and gloves were a bad combination.

Now look at mine.

And the Soul showed her — not hands, exactly, but the equivalent: the shape of itself, intact, entire, unmistakably other. Not her. Never her. Alongside her. The way Warren's hand had lain beside hers on the floor that night — close, not touching, both fully themselves.

I don't absorb, the Soul said. That's never been what this is. I recognize. That's all any of this has ever actually asked of anyone.

*

Outside, on the second day, Perla watched her daughter's breathing change and did not panic, because the note said what it said and she had the note in her apron pocket and she was extremely qualified.

What she hadn't been prepared for was Warren.

He'd gone still in the particular way he went still — not asleep-still, working-still — and sometime in the middle of the second day his face did something Perla had never seen it do in all the months she'd known him. It unmade itself. Not grief, exactly. Not joy. The expression of a man hearing his name spoken by someone who actually knew how to say it.

Perla put down her knitting. She didn't touch him — the note was clear about not touching — but she sat forward in the good armchair and watched with the satisfaction of watching Mina take her first steps: there. That's it. Go on.

Downstairs, Denny's radio played something old and forgettable. The lamp hummed. The curtain hung wrong on its rod, the way it had hung wrong for years, as if it had personally offended the man who'd hung it.

*

Inside, in the last full moment before it stopped being an inside at all, Warren felt it happen — the turn, the three of them turning toward each other the way people turn toward a voice they love in another room.

He had facilitated this more times than he wanted to count. He knew the shape of it the way a surgeon knows an incision. He had always been the third consciousness — the helper, the witness, the one who held the door.

This time the door opened both ways.

Because Mina was there — fully, unmistakably Mina, red streak and checked-twice lab results and all — and the Soul was there, intact and enormous and known to him — known longer than anything but his own first thought — and for the first time in two thousand years, someone else in the room held Yeshua the way he'd actually been. Not the theology. The person. The jokes that didn't make it into the book. The way he'd said Miriam's name when he was tired.

It undid him. There was no dignified word for it.

He tried a joke — something about how he'd spent two millennia being mysterious and it had all been defeated by a woman with a receipt in her pocket — and it came out wrong, sideways, the punchline arriving a half-second late.

Mina — inside, all three of them inside now, the boundaries gone thin as light through a curtain — heard it, and laughed, and said, terrible,

and Warren dropped the deflection the way you'd drop a heavy bag at the top of the stairs, and said the true thing underneath it:

I have been alone with this for so long.

And the Soul — the oldest friend anything had, the one who'd called him to Terra before writing — answered him the way it had answered at the beginning of everything:

You are.

We are, Mina said. And meant: I am still me, and I know you, and I'm staying.

We are, the Soul said. And meant: I am remembered, and I am whole, and I am not alone in the remembering.

We are, Warren said. And meant: I am known. For the first time in two thousand years, I am known, and it didn't cost anyone herself.

All three of them said it together, meaning three different true things by it, none of them canceling any of the others out.

And then, gently, without any drama at all, the way a held breath finally, naturally, lets go, it was over, and he was sitting on the edge of a narrow bed in a room above a hardware store with the morning coming through a badly fitted curtain, and someone was crying, close by, and it took him a full disoriented second to understand the sound was relief, and that some of it was his.

*

Mina opened her eyes first.

The ceiling was the same ceiling. The lamp was the same lamp. The curtain still hung wrong. Her mother's face appeared above her, and her mother said, in a voice that was entirely, unmistakably, gloriously her own:

"You drool in your sleep. Some daughter of a Seer."

Mina laughed, and the laugh turned into a cough, and Warren got her upright with a hand at her back, and Perla produced a glass of water from nowhere — mothers could do that — and Mina drank it, and it was the best water she'd ever tasted, because it was water, ordinary, specific, hers.

"How long?" she croaked.

"Two days," Perla said. "You're faster than me. I was always the slow one."

Mina looked at Warren. He looked back at her — and she saw it happen, the question crossing him: who are you looking at?

"Mina Mendez," she said, before he could ask. "Still. Check the receipt."

His breath left him all at once. "Hi."

"Hi."

She swung her legs off the bed. Stood. Swayed. Perla's hand was there, and Warren's, and she was fine — she was fine, fifty-one years old and starving and entirely herself, with the reef-light still fading at the edges of her vision like sunspots.

Then she reached for the water glass on the windowsill, missed it with her elbow instead, and knocked it sideways.

The glass tipped. Water arced out toward the floor.

And stopped.

Not fell. Stopped — hung in the air in a loose, trembling curve, catching the morning light, as if the world had briefly forgotten which way was down and was waiting for someone to remind it.

Mina stared. Warren stared. Perla said, very quietly, "Ay."

Mina blinked — and the water fell, all at once, splashing across the floorboards, entirely ordinary, entirely wet.

Nobody said anything for a full five seconds.

"Did you do that?" Warren asked.

"I don't — " Mina looked at her hand. Her ordinary hand, scar on the thumb, nails cut short. "I don't think so. I think it just — " She looked up at him. "It felt like reaching for something before I knew I was reaching."

Warren looked at the puddle. Then at her. Then at the puddle again.

"Well," he said. "That's going to take some practice."

"That's your professional assessment?"

"Two thousand years of practice, and that's my assessment, yes."

Perla got up, went to the little kitchen counter, and came back with a towel. She dropped it on the puddle and stood on it, in her slippers, the way she'd stood on a thousand spills in a thousand kitchens.

Mina looked at the other glass on the windowsill — Perla's, set down beside her own, her card still propped behind them both, holding its ground.

"Don't," Warren said.

"I wasn't going to—"

"You were."

She grinned, caught, and left the glass where it was.

"Some things," Perla said, "don't change." She looked at her daughter — really looked, the way mothers do, checking for the person underneath the event. "You're still you."

"I'm still me," Mina said. And then, quieter, because it was also true and she was done being afraid of true things: "I'm also more."

Perla nodded, like this was a perfectly ordinary thing for a daughter to say. "Eat something. More can wait until after breakfast."

The morning came all the way through the badly fitted curtain, striping the floorboards. The lamp hummed. Downstairs, Denny's radio found its station.

Mina Mendez sat on the edge of a narrow bed, fifty-one years old, scientist, Seer, daughter — and also, now, the one person alive who remembered Yeshua the way he'd actually been, who'd stood in reef-light older than language and been recognized back.

She was Mina. She was also more. Both true.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Seven: We Are

44b_we_are.md

(WORKING DRAFT — post-Harmony intimacy, 2026-09-24. Creator-approved direction; prose still subject to revision.)

It happened six days after Full Harmony.

Not immediately.

Mina was grateful for that.

The first two days she mostly slept.

On the third she spent four hours arguing with the Eternal inside her, which was difficult to explain because the argument contained almost no words.

On the fourth she accidentally cooled a cup of coffee before picking it up.

On the fifth she cried in the shower because a memory of sunlight on Galilee had arrived attached to a feeling that belonged to Miriam and did not belong to Mina, and somehow both facts were perfectly clear.

By the sixth day she understood the important part.

She was still herself.

Entirely.

Miriam was not a ghost wearing Mina's skin. Not a second personality waiting to seize the controls. Not an ancestor. Not an alternate version.

She was another consciousness with whom Mina had finally learned how to listen.

You are.

That evening Perla went home.

Not because anyone sent her away.

Because Perla looked from Mina to Warren and said, "I have been your mother for fifty-one years. I know when I am no longer needed in a room."

"Ma."

"I said needed. Not wanted. Different word."

Then she'd kissed Mina on the forehead, told Warren not to be stupid, and left.

The door closed.

Mina and Warren stood in the quiet.

For once, neither of them made a joke.

Warren looked almost nervous.

That fascinated her.

"You're nervous."

"No."

"You are terrible at lying."

"I invented several forms of lying."

"And apparently none of the useful ones."

That got the smallest smile.

Mina stepped toward him.

Nothing happened beneath reality.

No sudden pull.

No hidden door opening without her permission.

The connection was simply there.

Known.

Miriam beside her.

The One within Warren.

Present without demanding.

Mina touched Warren's face.

"You stopped twice."

"Yes."

"The second time I wanted to murder you."

"I remember."

"I understand now."

His eyes changed.

Not relief exactly.

Something softer.

"I know what she is," Mina said. "I know what he is. I know what happens when they recognize each other."

"Yes."

"And I know where I end."

Warren swallowed.

"And where she begins."

"Yes."

Mina smiled.

"That's rather inconvenient for your last excuse."

He actually laughed.

His eyes moved over her for a second too long.

Mina caught him.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"No. You were staring."

"I was looking."

"There is a distinction?"

"A considerable one."

She raised an eyebrow.

Warren's mouth twitched. "You are more than just a hot mom who is very, very fun to look at."

Mina stared at him.

"That may be the least divine thing you've ever said."

"It's also true." His smile faded into something quieter. "I meant what I said before. You have a beautiful mind, Mina."

She went still.

"Not Miriam's mind," he said. "Not something the Eternal gave you. Yours. The way you question things. The way you refuse an answer just because everyone else is satisfied with it. The way you can be terrified and still need to understand."

He touched her face.

"That's all Mina."

Her expression softened despite her best effort to prevent it.

"And the hot-mom part?"

"Also entirely Mina."

"Good answer."

"I've had some time to prepare."

"Two thousand years?"

"Mostly the last few months."

"Better answer."

She kissed him.

Warren began the next kiss.

That mattered.

Not because Mina needed him to prove anything.

Because for months every step toward her had required him to calculate what he might accidentally take.

Now there was nothing hidden.

Nothing she could not understand.

Nothing reaching through her without being recognized.

So Warren finally allowed himself the simple human luxury of wanting first.

He kissed her slowly.

No desperation.

No fear.

No hand hovering a centimeter away waiting for authorization already given.

Mina felt the difference immediately.

"You're considerably better at this when you're not conducting an ethics review."

"I've been told my bedside manner is excessive."

"By whom?"

"You."

"When?"

"Repeatedly."

She kissed him before he could continue.

They reached the bed laughing.

Again.

Mina decided this was probably going to be a recurring feature of their relationship.

This time there was no threshold hidden beneath the physical one.

She knew exactly what existed beyond it.

So when Warren touched her, she could simply be touched.

When he looked at her, she didn't wonder whether he saw Miriam.

She knew he didn't.

He saw the red in her hair.

The tiny line beside her mouth she had acquired sometime around forty.

The body she'd lived in for fifty-one years and had no intention of apologizing for.

He saw Mina.

And because she now carried Miriam's memory without being Miriam, Mina also knew something she hadn't known before.

Miriam had been looked at differently.

Loved differently.

In another body.

In another century.

By another version of this same continuing man.

That wasn't competition.

It was history.

Warren kissed her shoulder.

Mina closed her eyes.

For a while there was only physical reality.

Warmth.

Weight.

Breath.

Skin.

His attention moving over her without hurry, and hers returning it.

He touched her the way he did anything he'd finally decided he was allowed to care about openly: completely.

She discovered, with considerable satisfaction, that two thousand years of composure could still be dismantled by the right kiss.

He discovered that saying her name in that particular voice made her pull him closer.

They learned each other without treating the learning like a performance.

The bed was still too narrow.

The curtain still hung wrong.

One of them nearly knocked the river stone to the floor and both of them stopped long enough to save it.

Then they were laughing again.

The laughter faded naturally.

Not because the joy left.

Because something deeper arrived underneath it.

Warren said her name.

"Mina."

Not Miriam.

Mina.

She answered him by pulling him closer.

Touch became rhythm.

Rhythm became trust.

The ordinary human world narrowed until there was nothing left but breath, warmth, the pressure of him close to her, and the astonishing fact that after everything impossible about Warren Locke, this part required no miracle at all.

Then, very gently, another awareness unfolded.

Not taking control.

Joining.

Mina felt Miriam turn toward the One.

Warren felt it too; she knew because his eyes opened.

For one instant they simply looked at each other.

The question passed between them without words.

Mina smiled.

"Yes."

Warren's forehead touched hers.

And neither of them stopped.

The room remained.

Bed.

Curtain.

Streetlight.

The muted machinery of a small town settling into night.

Mina remained inside her body, completely herself, even as another horizon opened beneath sensation.

Miriam knew the One.

The One knew Miriam.

Not Warren.

Not Mina.

The consciousnesses beneath them.

Ancient beyond anything the human nervous system could properly imagine.

Recognition moved between them.

I am.

Mina felt herself.

You are.

Warren.

And beneath that—

Miriam.

The One.

Four truths.

None erased.

Then:

We are.

Mina understood, finally, why Harmony had never meant fusion.

Nothing disappeared.

Everything became more distinct.

Warren was more Warren because she could perceive where he ended.

Miriam was more Miriam because Mina no longer mistook her memories for her own.

The One was not Warren and yet could not be separated from what Warren had become.

And Mina—

Mina was gloriously, stubbornly, unquestionably Mina.

Her physical pleasure climbed.

Human.

Specific.

Hers.

She held Warren and let it.

This time there was no fear waiting at the edge of it.

No interruption.

No ancient mechanism stealing the decision.

Only the decision already made.

Her body reached its answer first.

Pleasure overtook thought, leaving her laughing and breathless against him because apparently even this couldn't make her stop being herself.

Warren followed a moment later, losing that impossible composure in a way Mina suspected she would treasure for the rest of however long their lives became.

And beneath them—

not simultaneously.

Separately.

Something happened for which Mina had no human vocabulary.

The Eternal equivalent was not physical climax.

It contained none of the body's mechanics.

It was recognition reaching completion.

The One opened.

Miriam answered.

Memory that had been separated across ages became mutually visible.

The ancient distance between them vanished without either consciousness crossing into the other.

Mina felt the magnitude of it and understood only the shape:

a beginning completed.

Not I am swallowed by we.

Not two becoming one.

Two saying, with absolute comprehension:

You are.

And receiving the same answer.

The release of that recognition moved through Harmony like light through water.

Miriam's joy was not Mina's climax.

The One's was not Warren's.

But Mina could feel enough of both to understand why human beings had spent thousands of years reaching for religious language whenever ordinary vocabulary failed.

Then it receded.

Not gone.

Settled.

Mina became aware of the room again.

Warren's weight beside her.

Her heartbeat.

The curtain.

One sock inexplicably still on Warren's foot.

She stared at it.

Then started laughing.

Warren opened his eyes.

"What?"

"You still have one sock on."

He looked down.

For perhaps three seconds he appeared genuinely offended by the universe.

"I had other priorities."

"I noticed."

He pulled it off and threw it toward the chair.

Missed.

Mina laughed harder.

Warren rolled onto his back.

After a moment Mina rested her head against his chest.

They were quiet.

Then Warren said, very softly, "Was that all right?"

Mina lifted her head.

"Two thousand years and that's your line?"

"It seems like an important question."

She studied him.

The man.

The One.

Both.

Neither reducible to the other.

"Yes," she said.

Then, because precision mattered:

"All of it."

His hand moved slowly through her hair.

Inside her, Miriam was quiet.

Not absent.

Content.

Mina could feel the difference now.

She closed her eyes.

"Warren?"

"Yes?"

"I think I understand it."

"What?"

She smiled against him.

"Why it starts with I am."

He waited.

"Because we are doesn't mean anything unless both people get to stay."

For a long time Warren said nothing.

Then his arm tightened around her.

"Yes."

Outside, Corbin's Landing went on existing.

Cars passed.

A dog barked.

Somewhere below them, Denny had forgotten to turn off a radio.

The universe remained enormous.

The room remained small.

And for once neither of those things contradicted the other.

Chapter Working draft

Chapter Forty-Eight: For Now, We'll Be

45_for_now_well_be.md

(WORKING DRAFT — central arc, 2026-09-22. Not final prose.)

The immortality conversation happened on a Thursday, over breakfast, because Warren had decided — correctly — that Mina would incinerate him if he tried to make it a moment, and because the eggs were getting cold.

"You've had a week," he said. "The water thing hasn't repeated. You've slept. Your mother has stopped calling twice a day." He slid the plate toward her. "So. The other thing. The separate thing. I want it said completely, once, and then I want us to not decide anything for a while."

Mina picked up her fork. "Say it, then."

He did. Not coyly — she'd have known, and she'd have been furious. Plainly, the way he'd learned to say plain things to her.

What he could do: a deliberate genetic alteration, performed with her lab, her equipment, her oversight. Not Harmony — this had nothing to do with the Soul. This was mechanics. His body didn't age because it had been built, over two thousand years of Harmony with the One, to maintain itself the way a Harmony-maintained body maintained itself — and he understood the mechanism well enough now to rebuild it in someone else. On purpose. From the outside in.

"It would make you like me," he said. "Physically. Non-aging. Not invulnerable — I can still be hurt, I can still be killed, don't let anyone tell you otherwise. But the clock stops. You'd stay — " He looked at her. "Fifty-one. As you are. For as long as the body holds, which would be a very long time."

"What would it cost."

"The procedure itself is the easy part. It's your lab. You'd run it. You'd know every step." He turned his coffee mug in his hands. "What it costs is everything around it. You'd watch everyone you know age. Your mother — " He stopped. "Perla's Soul will keep her a long time, Mina. A long time is not forever. You'd watch Denny go. You'd watch the town change out from under you. You'd have to leave, eventually, and come back as someone else, the way I've done it a hundred times, and it doesn't get easier, it gets efficient, which is worse."

"What can't you promise."

"That it works the way I think it does at century scale. Nobody's done this — taken a fifty-one-year-old human body and rebuilt it from the outside. I've maintained mine from the inside for two thousand years, which is a different thing, like the difference between growing a garden and building one out of parts." He met her eyes. "There could be drift. There could be things I don't know to look for. I know the mechanism. I don't know the next three hundred years of it. Anyone who tells you they do is selling something."

"Long-term unknowns are real," Mina said.

"They're real."

"And the Soul? Harmony already — "

"Harmony will keep you healthy a long time on its own. Probably. I won't give you a number because I don't have an honest one." He shrugged. "This is different. This is me, deliberately, with tools, changing what your cells do. Separate consent. Separate decision. It doesn't come with Harmony and Harmony doesn't require it."

Mina ate an egg. Thought. Ate another egg.

"You're not going to tell me what you want," she said.

"No."

"Even though you obviously have an opinion."

"Obviously." He drank his coffee. "Wanting something doesn't entitle me to arrange for it. I've said that before. I meant it then, too."

"You're learning."

"I'm ancient. Learning is all I have left."

She almost smiled. Then didn't, quite, because the weight of it was sitting on the table between them next to the toast, enormous and quiet.

"I'm not deciding today," she said.

"I know."

"I'm not deciding this month."

"Good."

"Warren." She looked at him. "I need you to not wait for the answer like it's a verdict. I need us to just — live. With the question open."

He nodded. "We can do that. We're good at open questions. We have a curtain that's been an open question for years."

"The curtain is not an open question. The curtain is wrong and we both know it."

"See? Dialogue."

The kettle whistled. Mina got up — still a little too fast, still catching herself, the new instincts misfiring in small embarrassing ways all week — and reached for the handle.

The metal cooled under her hand before she touched it.

Not a lot. Just — the heat went out of it in the half-second before her fingers closed, the way a held breath lets go. She stood there holding a lukewarm kettle handle over a whistling kettle, and stared at it.

Warren stared at it.

"Did the stove — " he started.

"The stove is fine. The kettle is whistling. The handle is not hot." She set it down carefully. "I didn't do it on purpose."

"I know."

"I was just — I didn't want to burn myself, and it — " She looked at her hand. "It's like the water. It happens in the half-second before I decide."

"Instinct," Warren said. "The Soul knows how matter behaves. Your body is starting to listen before your brain catches up." He paused. "This is the part where practice comes in. Eventually you won't cool every handle in the kitchen by accident."

"Eventually I might set something on fire by accident."

"Probably. We'll keep a fire extinguisher." He got up and poured the water, testing the handle first out of a caution so deliberate it was almost a joke. "See? Lukewarm. You're already house-trained."

"Don't make it cute."

"It's a little cute."

"It's really not."

Perla arrived at ten with a bag of calamansi and opinions about the curtains — not Warren's curtain, her own, which she was rehanging — and stayed for coffee, and told them about the neighbor's dog, and asked Mina, with the particular casualness of a woman who had rehearsed the question, whether she was sleeping.

"Like the dead," Mina said. "Then I wake up and there's more of me than there was."

"Eat," Perla said, pushing the calamansi across the table. "More needs feeding."

Denny came by at noon with a box of drill bits and stayed for an hour, arguing with Warren about a customer who'd tried to return a shovel, and at no point did anyone mention that the woman making sandwiches had reef-light older than language shelved neatly beside her grocery lists. The radio played. The building settled. The town went on being the town, which is what towns are for.

That evening Mina went back to work — half days, to start, her supervisor watching her the way people watch someone who's been through something they don't have language for — and checked lab results twice, and pretended she didn't, and came home to find Warren on the floor by the window, reading, with the river stone in his hand.

"You stole that," she said.

"I'm holding it. There's a difference." He tossed it to her. She caught it one-handed — a little too fast, the new reflexes showing off — and turned it over in her palm, smooth and warm.

She sat on her patch of floor. He sat beside her. Close. The lamp hummed. The curtain hung wrong on its rod, striping the last of the daylight across the floorboards.

For a while neither of them said anything. There was nothing that needed saying that hadn't been said — the offer made, the decision unmade, the question sitting between them like the stone, warm, patient, in no hurry.

Below them, the town. Above them, the ordinary ceiling. Inside her, reef-light and a steward's face and flour on her mother's hands and a man's laugh in a room with dust in it, two thousand years gone, shelved side by side with a grocery list.

"There's a lot of future," Mina said finally.

"There is."

"More than there used to be."

"Yes."

She leaned her head against his shoulder. He was warm. He was specific. He was here — not the theology, not the history, the man, fifty-one years old in appearance and two thousand in fact, who washed mugs like he was defusing them.

"The infinite can wait," she said.

"It can," Warren said. "It's very patient. It's had practice."

"And today — "

"Today there's breakfast. And work. And your mother." He paused. "And a curtain that's wrong."

"We're not fixing the curtain."

"We're never fixing the curtain."

She smiled against his shoulder. Outside, Corbin's Landing went about its evening — doors closing, engines turning over, somebody's radio finding a station. Ordinary. Particular. Hers.

The question stayed open. The future stayed unchosen. And that was not a cliff they were standing on — it was a floor, solid, the two of them on it, in the present tense of their lives, which was the only tense anyone had ever actually lived in.

For now, we will be.
